The committee’s work is constrained by its Lok Sabha‑only composition, which excludes Rajya Sabha members, and by the one‑year tenure that limits continuity. The 2nd Administrative Reforms Commission recommended extending the term to two years and giving the committee a statutory basis, but the suggestion has not been implemented. Despite these limitations, the CSL remains a critical check on the executive’s rule‑making power, ensuring that delegated legislation remains within the constitutional framework of legislative supremacy.

Committee on Empowerment of Women: Gender‑Responsive Legislative Oversight

The Committee on Empowerment of Women (CEW) was constituted in 1997 as a department‑related standing committee with a bicameral composition of 30 members — 20 from the Lok Sabha and 10 from the Rajya Sabha — elected by proportional representation for a one‑year term. Its mandate covers the review of all measures taken by the Union Government for the welfare and empowerment of women, the examination of bills and policies affecting women, and the monitoring of the implementation of key legislations such as the Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act, 2005; the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013; and the Maternity Benefit (Amendment) Act, 2017.

The committee calls for reports from the Ministry of Women and Child Development, the Ministry of Labour and Employment, and other relevant ministries. It undertakes study visits, holds oral evidence hearings with secretaries, chairpersons of the National Commission for Women, and civil‑society representatives. In a recent report, the CEW highlighted gaps in the functioning of Internal Complaints Committees under the 2013 Act, recommending mandatory third‑party audits and a centralized online portal for complaint tracking. The Ministry accepted several recommendations, leading to the issuance of revised guidelines.

The CEW also scrutinises budgetary allocations for women‑centric schemes such as Beti Bachao Beti Padhao, Mahila Shakti Kendra, and the Pradhan Mantri Matru Vandana Yojana. Its reports are tabled in both Houses and discussed during the Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Women and Child Development. However, the committee’s recommendations are advisory; the government’s Action Taken Reports are often delayed, and there is no dedicated follow‑up mechanism. The 2nd ARC suggested merging the CEW with the Committee on Social Justice to avoid duplication, but political sensitivities have prevented the reform. Nevertheless, the CEW provides a structured parliamentary platform for gender‑responsive scrutiny, keeping the executive accountable on women’s rights commitments.

Technique: Mapping Committee Powers to Specific Instruments
A rule made under the Information Technology Act, 2000 is challenged for exceeding the enabling provision. Which parliamentary committee can recommend its annulment and what is the procedural requirement for the recommendation to take effect?
The Committee on Subordinate Legislation (CSL) has the authority to examine delegated legislation and recommend annulment. The recommendation must be adopted by the Lok Sabha; once the House passes the motion, the rule ceases to have effect. The CSL’s recommendation alone does not annul the rule — parliamentary approval is mandatory.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the distinct composition and tenure of each DRSC: CSL — 15 Lok Sabha members, one‑year term, Lok Sabha‑only; CEW — 30 members (20 LS + 10 RS), one‑year term, bicameral. Use the mnemonic “Sub‑Leg = 15 LS; Women = 30 (20+10)” to recall quickly in MCQs and descriptive answers.

Committee on Science and Technology, Environment and Forests: Technical Scrutiny and Policy Impact

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Science and Technology, Environment and Forests (STEF) was constituted in 1993 with a strength of 31 members — 21 from the Lok Sabha and 10 from the Rajya Sabha — elected for a one‑year term. Its jurisdiction spans the Ministries of Science and Technology, Environment, Forest and Climate Change, and Earth Sciences. The committee examines bills, budgetary proposals, and policy documents relating to scientific research, environmental regulation, forest conservation, climate change mitigation, and space technology.

A key function is the scrutiny of delegated legislation such as the Environment (Protection) Rules, the Forest (Conservation) Rules, and the rules framed under the National Green Tribunal Act, 2010. In a recent report, the STEF Committee evaluated the implementation of the National Green Tribunal’s orders on industrial pollution in the Ganga basin, noting delays in compliance and recommending a time‑bound monitoring framework. The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change subsequently issued a directive to state pollution control boards to submit quarterly compliance reports.

The committee also reviews India’s international commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Convention on Biological Diversity. Its observations on climate policy have stressed the need for a legally binding long‑term target and an independent climate council. Although a dedicated climate change law has not yet been enacted, the committee’s deliberations have informed the government’s negotiating stance at COP meetings. The STEF Committee’s technical expertise is supplemented by inviting domain experts from the Indian Council of Medical Research, the Indian Space Research Organisation, and the Council of Scientific and Industrial Research for oral evidence. Its recommendations, while advisory, carry weight because they are debated in both Houses during the Demands for Grants of the concerned ministries.

Committee on Health and Family Welfare: Pandemic Response and Health Financing Oversight

Established in 1993, the Committee on Health and Family Welfare (CHFW) comprises 31 members — 21 from the Lok Sabha and 10 from the Rajya Sabha — serving a one‑year term. It oversees the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, the Ministry of AYUSH, and the Department of Health Research. The committee’s remit includes the examination of the National Health Policy, the National Health Mission, the Ayushman Bharat scheme, and the regulation of medical education and pharmaceuticals.

During the COVID‑19 pandemic, the CHFW played a pivotal role in real‑time oversight. Its report on the management of the second wave highlighted shortages of medical oxygen, inequitable vaccine distribution, and gaps in the CoWIN platform. The committee recommended the creation of a strategic oxygen reserve, a transparent vaccine allocation formula, and an audit of the PM CARES Fund expenditures on health infrastructure. The government accepted the oxygen reserve proposal and established the Oxygen Emergency Response Mechanism.

The committee also scrutinises the implementation of the Clinical Establishments (Registration and Regulation) Act, 2010, and the National Medical Commission Act, 2019. Its report on medical education recommended a common entrance test for postgraduate courses and stricter norms for private medical colleges, many of which were incorporated in subsequent amendments. The CHFW’s reports are discussed during the Demands for Grants of the Health Ministry, providing a platform for opposition members to question executive decisions. However, the advisory nature of its recommendations and the lack of a statutory follow‑up mechanism limit enforcement.

Committee on External Affairs: Parliamentary Diplomacy and Treaty Scrutiny

The Committee on External Affairs (CEA) was constituted in 1993 with 31 members — 21 from the Lok Sabha and 10 from the Rajya Sabha — for a one‑year term. It examines the Ministry of External Affairs’ policies, bilateral and multilateral agreements, and India’s participation in international organisations. The committee’s unique role lies in the parliamentary scrutiny of treaties before ratification, a function that complements the executive’s treaty‑making power under Article 253.

In a recent review, the CEA examined the Communications Compatibility and Security Agreement (COMCASA) and the Logistics Exchange Memorandum of Agreement (LEMOA). Its report recommended stronger parliamentary oversight of defence technology transfers and a periodic review mechanism for the implementation of these agreements. The Ministry of External Affairs incorporated a clause for annual parliamentary briefings on the operationalisation of these agreements.

The committee also monitors India’s engagement with the United Nations, the World Trade Organization, and regional groupings such as SAARC, BIMSTEC, and the Quad. Its report on the Indo‑Pacific strategy emphasised the need for a coherent maritime domain awareness framework and greater parliamentary involvement in shaping the narrative. The CEA’s recommendations are tabled in both Houses and discussed during the Demands for Grants of the External Affairs Ministry. While the committee cannot veto treaties, its public deliberations create political pressure for transparency and accountability in foreign policy.

  • Confusing the Committee on Subordinate Legislation with the Committee on Delegated Legislation — they are the same body; the formal name is Committee on Subordinate Legislation.
  • Assuming the Committee on Empowerment of Women has legislative power to amend Acts — its recommendations are advisory only.
  • Believing the STEF Committee can directly enforce environmental norms — it can only recommend; enforcement rests with the executive and the National Green Tribunal.
  • Thinking the Health Committee’s pandemic reports led to immediate statutory changes — most recommendations required separate legislative or executive action.
  • Overlooking that the External Affairs Committee’s treaty scrutiny occurs after the executive has signed but before ratification; it does not negotiate treaties.
  • Neglecting the one‑year tenure of all these committees, which hampers continuity and long‑term follow‑up.
  • These traps highlight the nuanced understanding required for the examination.

Joint Sittings of Parliament: Article 108 — Constitutional Design, Historical Invocations and Political Dynamics

Article 108 provides for a joint sitting of both Houses to resolve deadlocks over legislation, a mechanism inspired by the Australian Constitution but adapted to India’s asymmetric bicameralism. The President may summon a joint sitting when a Bill passed by one House is rejected by the other, the Houses finally disagree on amendments, or more than six months elapse without the Bill being passed by the other House. Money Bills and Constitution Amendment Bills are explicitly excluded — the former because Rajya Sabha has only recommendatory power (Art. 110), the latter because they require a special majority in each House separately (Art. 368). The joint sitting is presided over by the Speaker of Lok Sabha, or in their absence the Deputy Speaker, or the Deputy Chairman of Rajya Sabha, or a person determined by the members present. Decisions are taken by a simple majority of the total number of members of both Houses present and voting, giving Lok Sabha a decisive structural advantage due to its larger strength (543 vs 245).

Since 1950, only three Bills have been passed at joint sittings: the Dowry Prohibition Bill, 1959 (first joint sitting, 1961), the Banking Service Commission (Repeal) Bill, 1977 (1978), and the Prevention of Terrorism Bill, 2002 (2002). The POTA joint sitting was politically charged — the government used its Lok Sabha majority to overcome Rajya Sabha opposition. The Supreme Court in Union of India v. S. K. Sharma (2007) upheld the validity of the joint sitting, rejecting the argument that the six-month period under Art. 108(1)(c) should exclude periods when Parliament was not in session. The Court held that “six months” means six calendar months, not six months of sitting days. In 2020, a joint sitting was discussed for the three Farm Bills but the government proceeded via voice vote in Rajya Sabha amid procedural controversy. The joint sitting remains a measure of last resort — its rarity reflects the political cost of bypassing Rajya Sabha’s federal role and the convention that governments seek consensus on contentious legislation.

The President's Address and Motion of Thanks: Constitutional Mandate, Procedural Nuances and Opposition Strategy

Article 87 requires the President to address both Houses assembled together at the commencement of the first session after each general election and at the commencement of the first session of each year. The Address, drafted by the Council of Ministers, outlines the government’s policy priorities and legislative agenda. Following the Address, a Motion of Thanks is moved in each House by a ruling party member and seconded by another. The discussion on the Motion of Thanks is the principal occasion for a general debate on the state of the nation, allowing the Opposition to critique government policy across the board. Amendments to the Motion of Thanks may be moved — if passed, they constitute a censure of the government’s agenda, though not a no-confidence motion. In 1980, the Motion of Thanks in Lok Sabha was amended to include a reference to the dismissal of state governments under Article 356; the government survived but the amendment signalled political disquiet.

Procedurally, the Prime Minister replies to the debate in both Houses. The reply is not subject to a vote unless amendments are moved. If the Motion of Thanks is passed without amendment, it is deemed adopted. In Rajya Sabha, where the government may lack a majority, the Motion of Thanks often passes with amendments moved by the Opposition. For instance, in 2021, Rajya Sabha passed the Motion of Thanks with amendments moved by the Opposition on issues including farmers’ protests, surveillance allegations, and pandemic management — marking the first time since 1980 that amendments were adopted in the Upper House. The government is not constitutionally bound to implement the amendments, but they carry political weight. The President’s Address and the Motion of Thanks together constitute a unique accountability moment: the executive’s annual report card is debated and voted upon, blending ceremonial formality with substantive political contestation.

Examiner's Twist: Amendment to Motion of Thanks vs No-Confidence Motion
A question asks: "If an amendment to the Motion of Thanks is passed in Lok Sabha, does the government fall?" Many aspirants confuse this with a no-confidence motion.
No, the government does not fall. An amendment to the Motion of Thanks expresses disapproval of the President's Address (i.e., the government's policy agenda) but does not test the confidence of the House in the Council of Ministers. A no-confidence motion (Art. 75(3) read with Rule 198 of Lok Sabha Rules) explicitly seeks to prove that the government has lost the confidence of the House. The Motion of Thanks is a vote on the Address; a no-confidence motion is a vote on the government's continuance. The distinction is critical: in 1980, the Indira Gandhi government survived an amended Motion of Thanks but would have fallen if a no-confidence motion had passed.
Exam-Day Strategy
Memorise the three joint sitting Bills (Dowry Prohibition 1961, Banking Service Commission Repeal 1978, POTA 2002) and the constitutional exclusions (Money Bills, Constitution Amendment Bills). For President's Address, remember: Art. 87 occasions (first session post-election + first session each year), amendments possible but not a confidence test, and the 2021 Rajya Sabha amendments precedent. Link joint sittings to Art. 108 and the Speaker's presiding role; link President's Address to Art. 87 and the Motion of Thanks procedure.

Parliamentary Whips: Constitutional Status, Party Discipline and the Anti-Defection Interface

The whip system, inherited from British parliamentary practice, operates in India without explicit constitutional or statutory foundation. Whips are appointed by political parties, not by Parliament, and their authority derives from party discipline rather than legislative rule. The Chief Whip of the majority party in Lok Sabha is formally recognised by the Speaker and accorded certain facilities, but the whip’s directions — one-line, two-line, or three-line — are internal party communications. Defiance of a three-line whip on a confidence motion or a Money Bill typically attracts disqualification under the Tenth Schedule, but the Tenth Schedule (inserted by the 52nd Amendment, 1985) does not mention whips. It disqualifies a member who “voluntarily gives up membership” of the party or votes/abstains contrary to a direction issued by the party “or by any person or authority authorised by it” — the latter phrase covers whip directions. In Kihoto Hollohan v. Zachillhu (1992), the Supreme Court upheld the constitutionality of the Tenth Schedule but held that the Speaker’s decision on disqualification is subject to judicial review. The Court did not define “voluntarily gives up membership” exhaustively, leaving room for interpretation.

In Ravi S. Naik v. Union of India (1994), the Court held that a member’s conduct — such as publicly opposing the party, joining another party’s protest, or writing to the Governor claiming support for an alternative government — can amount to “voluntarily giving up membership” even without formal resignation. The whip’s direction is the trigger for disqualification on voting/abstention grounds, but the Speaker must determine whether the direction was issued by the competent authority (the party leadership) and whether the member’s violation was intentional. In 2019, the Karnataka Speaker disqualified 17 MLAs for defying the Congress-JD(S) whip during the trust vote; the Supreme Court upheld the disqualifications but allowed them to contest by-elections, clarifying that disqualification under the Tenth Schedule does not bar fresh elections. The whip system thus sits at the intersection of party autonomy, parliamentary privilege, and constitutional adjudication — a tension unresolved by statute.

Quorum, Language and the Sub Judice Rule: Procedural Safeguards for Deliberative Integrity

Article 100(3) mandates that until Parliament by law otherwise provides, the quorum for a meeting of either House shall be one-tenth of the total number of members — 55 for Lok Sabha, 25 for Rajya Sabha. If at any time during a sitting there is no quorum, the presiding officer must adjourn or suspend the House until a quorum is present. In practice, quorum is rarely enforced during routine business; the “quorum bell” is rung only when the Opposition seeks to disrupt proceedings or test government strength. The Rules of Procedure (Rule 48 in Lok Sabha, Rule 46 in Rajya Sabha) empower the Chair to order a count if a member draws attention to the absence of quorum. The government often ensures attendance during crucial votes, but the quorum rule remains a procedural lever for the Opposition.

Article 120 provides that business in Parliament shall be transacted in Hindi or English, but the presiding officer may permit a member to address the House in their mother tongue. The Official Languages Act, 1963, continued English indefinitely for parliamentary proceedings. Simultaneous interpretation is provided in both Houses. The sub judice rule (Rule 352 in Lok Sabha, Rule 259 in Rajya Sabha) prohibits reference to matters pending before a court of law, tribunal, or commission of inquiry, to avoid prejudicing judicial proceedings. The rule is not absolute — the Chair may permit reference if satisfied it will not prejudice the case. In 2018, the Speaker disallowed a discussion on the Rafale deal citing sub judice, as the matter was before the Supreme Court. The rule balances parliamentary freedom of speech (Art. 105) with judicial independence. Together, quorum, language, and sub judice rules form the procedural architecture that safeguards the deliberative character of Parliament against majoritarian haste, linguistic exclusion, and executive-judicial friction.

Committee on Rules and General Purposes Committee: The Internal Governance Architecture of Parliament

The Committee on Rules (Lok Sabha: 15 members; Rajya Sabha: 16 members) is the only committee empowered to propose amendments to the Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business. Chaired by the Speaker (Lok Sabha) or Chairman (Rajya Sabha), it reviews procedural gaps, recommends new rules, and interprets existing ones. Its recommendations are placed before the House for adoption. Major procedural innovations — Zero Hour (1962), Calling Attention (1964), Short Duration Discussion (1953) — originated from Rules Committee recommendations. The General Purposes Committee (Lok Sabha: Speaker + Deputy Speaker + panel of chairpersons + leaders of recognised parties; Rajya Sabha: similar composition) advises the presiding officer on matters concerning the affairs of the House not specifically allotted to other committees — allocation of rooms, facilities for members, media access, and procedural conventions. It does not report to the House; its decisions are implemented by the Secretariat.

In 2021, the Lok Sabha Rules Committee recommended codifying the “Zero Hour” convention into a formal rule, proposing time limits and a cap on matters per session — the first attempt to regulate a practice that evolved informally. The Rajya Sabha Rules Committee in 2020 recommended stricter criteria for admitting Calling Attention notices to prevent misuse. These committees are the guardians of parliamentary procedure, yet their deliberations are closed, and their composition reflects party strength. The Speaker’s dual role as Chair of the Rules Committee and presiding officer concentrates procedural authority, a feature criticised for limiting Opposition influence on rule-making. The General Purposes Committee, by contrast, operates by consensus and handles the “housekeeping” of Parliament — its low visibility belies its importance in maintaining the institution’s functional integrity.

  • Confusing the joint sitting mechanism (Art. 108) with the President's joint address (Art. 87) — they are distinct: one resolves legislative deadlock, the other is an annual executive statement.
  • Assuming an amendment to the Motion of Thanks constitutes a no-confidence motion — it signals policy dissent but does not test the government's majority.
  • Believing the whip is a constitutional officer — whips are party functionaries; their directions trigger Tenth Schedule disqualification but they hold no parliamentary office.
  • Thinking quorum is routinely enforced — in practice, quorum counts are strategic tools, not automatic checks.
  • Overlooking that the sub judice rule is a procedural rule, not a constitutional bar — the Chair has discretion to permit discussion on pending matters.
  • Assuming the Rules Committee can amend rules unilaterally — its recommendations require House adoption.
  • Neglecting that the General Purposes Committee does not report to the House — its decisions are administrative, not legislative.

Legislative Impact Assessment and Post‑Legislative Scrutiny: Institutionalising Evidence‑Based Law‑Making

India’s legislative framework has traditionally focused on pre‑legislative consultation — the 2014 Pre‑Legislative Consultation Policy prescribes a 30‑day public comment period for draft Bills — but post‑legislative scrutiny (PLS) remains embryonic. The concept is well‑established in the United Kingdom (where departmental select committees routinely conduct PLS), the European Union (ex‑post evaluation of directives), and OECD member states. It requires Parliament to assess whether a law achieves its stated objectives, imposes proportionate costs, and generates unintended consequences. The National Commission to Review the Working of the Constitution (NCRWC, 2002) recommended a permanent PLS mechanism; the Law Commission of India’s 249th Report (2014) on obsolete laws implicitly endorsed periodic review. In 2017, the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice presented a report urging that each DRSC undertake PLS of at least one principal Act per year, using a standard template covering objective achievement, regulatory burden, compliance costs, judicial interpretation, and stakeholder feedback. The Lok Sabha Secretariat later circulated a background paper proposing a “Legislative Impact Assessment Unit” within the Parliamentary Secretariat to supply data analytics, cost‑benefit models, and comparative jurisprudence. Despite these proposals, no formal rule has been adopted. DRSCs occasionally perform de facto PLS — for instance, reviews of subordinate legislation under the Information Technology (Intermediary Guidelines) Rules and examinations of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code’s operational outcomes — but such exercises are ad hoc, resource‑constrained, and lack a mandatory reporting cycle. The absence of a statutory PLS mandate means sunset clauses (automatic expiry unless renewed) are rare in Indian statutes; the Terrorist and Disruptive Activities (Prevention) Act, 1987, and the Prevention of Terrorism Act, 2002, are notable examples that lapsed or were repealed. Institutionalising PLS would require amendments to the Rules of Procedure to allocate dedicated committee time, a budget line for external expertise, and a protocol for government response to committee recommendations within a fixed period — mirroring the UK’s convention of government replies to committee reports.

Parliamentary Procedure for Impeachment: Constitutional Design, Historical Precedents and Procedural Safeguards

The Constitution provides two distinct impeachment‑type procedures: removal of the President under Article 61, and removal of Supreme Court and High Court judges under Articles 124(4), 124(5), 217(1)(b) and 218, read with the Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968. For the President, the charge must be “violation of the Constitution,” initiated by a notice signed by not less than one‑fourth of the total membership of either House, followed by a 14‑day notice period, investigation by the other House, and a two‑thirds majority of the total membership of both Houses. No President has faced impeachment; a 1987 motion against President Zail Singh lapsed for want of signatures. Judicial removal is more structured: a motion signed by 100 Lok Sabha or 50 Rajya Sabha members triggers the constitution of a three‑member Judge Inquiry Committee (a Supreme Court judge, a High Court Chief Justice, and a distinguished jurist). The Committee frames charges, conducts a quasi‑judicial inquiry, and submits a report. If the Committee finds “proved misbehaviour or incapacity,” the motion proceeds to both Houses, requiring a two‑thirds majority of members present and voting and a majority of the total membership of each House. Only three judges have reached the parliamentary stage: Justice V. Ramaswami (1993) — the Inquiry Committee found him guilty, but the Lok Sabha motion failed after the ruling party abstained; Justice Soumitra Sen (2011) — Rajya Sabha passed the motion, but he resigned before the Lok Sabha vote; Justice P.D. Dinakaran (2011) — resigned after the Inquiry Committee was constituted but before its report. A fourth, Justice J.B. Pardiwala (2015), saw the motion withdrawn after he apologised for controversial remarks. The Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968, governs procedure: the Committee regulates its own procedure, the judge has a right to cross‑examine witnesses and be represented by counsel, and the report is laid before each House. The Supreme Court in K. Veeraswami v. Union of India (1991) held that a judge cannot be prosecuted criminally without the Chief Justice of India’s prior permission, reinforcing the impeachment route as the primary accountability mechanism. Critics argue the high political threshold makes removal nearly impossible when the executive enjoys a majority, while the Judge Inquiry Committee’s confidentiality raises transparency concerns. The Judicial Standards and Accountability Bill, 2010 (lapsed), and the Judicial Appointments Commission Bill, 2013 (lapsed), sought to create a permanent oversight body, but political consensus remains elusive.

Examiner's Twist
A question asks: "The impeachment procedure for a Supreme Court judge under Article 124(4) requires a two‑thirds majority of the total membership of each House. Is this statement correct? Explain with reference to the Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968."
The statement is incorrect. Article 124(4) read with Article 124(5) and the Judges (Inquiry) Act, 1968, requires a two‑thirds majority of members present and voting in each House, and a majority of the total membership of that House. The "two‑thirds of total membership" threshold applies only to presidential impeachment under Article 61. For judges, the double threshold (special majority of present and voting + absolute majority of the House) is designed to be stringent but slightly lower than the presidential standard. The 1993 Ramaswami motion failed because although the two‑thirds of present and voting was achieved, the absolute majority of total membership was not secured due to abstentions.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three‑tier voting threshold matrix: (1) Ordinary Bill — simple majority of present and voting; (2) Constitutional Amendment (Art. 368) — two‑thirds of present and voting + majority of total membership + half state ratification if federal; (3) Presidential Impeachment (Art. 61) — two‑thirds of total membership of both Houses; (4) Judicial Removal (Art. 124/217) — two‑thirds of present and voting + majority of total membership in each House. A single table comparing these four prevents confusion in MCQs and short answers.

The Panel of Chairpersons and Vice‑Chairpersons: Rotational Presiding Authority and Procedural Continuity

Articles 95(1) and 91 of the Constitution empower the Speaker of Lok Sabha and the Chairman of Rajya Sabha, respectively, to nominate a panel of members to preside over the House in the absence of the presiding officer and the Deputy. In Lok Sabha, the Rules of Procedure provide for a Panel of Chairpersons not exceeding ten members, nominated by the Speaker at the commencement of each session (or when a vacancy arises). In Rajya Sabha, the Rules provide for a Panel of Vice‑Chairpersons, nominated by the Chairman, typically six to eight members. Members of the panel preside in the order of their nomination; when both the Speaker/Chairman and Deputy are absent, the senior‑most available panel member takes the Chair. While presiding, a panel member exercises all powers of the Speaker/Chairman under the Rules — including maintaining order, deciding points of order, admitting notices, and putting questions to vote — but cannot preside when the office of Speaker or Deputy Speaker is vacant (Article 95(2) for Lok Sabha; Article 91(2) for Rajya Sabha). In such a vacancy, the President (for Lok Sabha) or the Chairman (for Rajya Sabha) appoints a member to perform the duties until a new Speaker/Deputy is elected. The panel’s composition reflects party strength, but the Speaker/Chairman has discretion to include Opposition members — a convention observed for decades. Panel members do not receive additional salary but are entitled to a daily sitting allowance when presiding. Their rulings are subject to appeal to the Speaker/Chairman under the relevant Rules. The panel system ensures procedural continuity during prolonged sessions, especially when the Speaker/Chairman attends inter‑parliamentary engagements or is indisposed. However, critics note that panel members, being party affiliates, may face perceptions of partisanship when ruling on contentious Opposition motions — a concern mitigated by the convention that the senior‑most panel member, often a senior Opposition figure, presides during sensitive debates.

The Committee on Absence of Members from Sittings: Enforcing Parliamentary Discipline and Attendance

Article 101(4) of the Constitution provides that if a member of either House is absent for sixty days without permission, the House may declare the seat vacant. The operationalisation of this provision rests with the Committee on Absence of Members from Sittings — a standing committee in each House. In Lok Sabha, the Rules constitute a 15‑member committee nominated by the Speaker; in Rajya Sabha, the Rules provide for a 10‑member committee nominated by the Chairman. The committee examines every application for leave of absence submitted by members, verifies the grounds (illness, family bereavement, parliamentary duties abroad, etc.), and reports to the House recommending whether the absence should be condoned. The House then votes on the report; if leave is refused, the absence counts toward the sixty‑day limit. The committee also monitors attendance registers maintained by the Secretariat and may suo motu inquire into prolonged unexplained absences. Historically, the committee has been lenient — leave is routinely granted for health and constituency exigencies. Notable instances of seat vacation have occurred, though they remain rare. During the COVID‑19 pandemic (2020‑2021), both Houses invoked special provisions: the “attendance” requirement was deemed satisfied for members participating in virtual committee meetings, and the sixty‑day rule was relaxed via House resolutions. The committee’s reports are generally adopted without debate, reflecting a consensus that attendance enforcement is a housekeeping function rather than a political tool. Yet, the committee’s composition — mirroring party strength — and its closed‑door deliberations have occasionally drawn criticism when members of the ruling party with poor attendance receive condonation while Opposition members face scrutiny. Recent recommendations have included digitising attendance tracking and publishing monthly attendance percentages to enhance transparency; a dashboard has been implemented, but member‑wise data remains accessible only to members.

The Role of the Leader of the House: Government's Floor Management, Legislative Strategy and Opposition Engagement

Although not a constitutional office, the Leader of the House (LoH) is the government’s chief parliamentary strategist, recognised in the Rules of Procedure of both Houses. In Lok Sabha, the Prime Minister — if a member of that House — is ex officio Leader of the House; otherwise, a senior Cabinet minister is appointed (e.g., Pranab Mukherjee during Manmohan Singh’s premiership, 2004‑2014). In Rajya Sabha, where the Prime Minister is rarely a member, a senior minister holds the position — Arun Jaitley (2014‑2019), Piyush Goyal (since 2021). The LoH’s functions are derived from convention and Rules: (1) announcing the government’s legislative programme at the start of each session; (2) coordinating with the Business Advisory Committee (BAC) on time allocation for Bills, discussions, and motions; (3) moving motions for suspension of rules, extension of sitting hours, and curtailment of debate (guillotine); (4) liaising with the Leader of the Opposition (LoP) and floor leaders of other parties to build consensus on contentious legislation; (5) responding to points of order on government business; and (6) managing the government’s whip operation in tandem with the Chief Whip. The LoH’s effectiveness shapes legislative throughput — the 15th Lok Sabha (2009‑2014) passed a high volume of Bills with a consensual style; the 16th Lok Sabha (2014‑2019) saw a different approach; the 17th Lok Sabha (2019‑2024) maintained high legislative output. The LoH also advises the Speaker/Chairman on the admissibility of motions that test government stability — no‑confidence motions, adjournment motions, and privilege motions against ministers. The LoH–LoP relationship is pivotal: the 1996‑1998 period saw weekly “breakfast meetings” to clear legislative logjams — a practice revived intermittently. The LoH has no vote‑casting privilege beyond being a member; the office carries no separate salary, only the ministerial emoluments. A Rules Committee has recommended formalising the LoH’s role, including a mandatory pre‑session meeting with the LoP, but the amendment remains pending.

  • Confusing the impeachment threshold for the President (two‑thirds of total membership) with that for judges (two‑thirds of present and voting plus majority of total membership).
  • Assuming the Panel of Chairpersons can preside when the Speaker’s office is vacant — Article 95(2) explicitly bars this; the President appoints a pro‑tem Speaker.
  • Believing the Committee on Absence of Members can declare a seat vacant — it only reports; the House declares the seat vacant under Article 101(4).
  • Thinking the Leader of the House is a constitutional authority — the office exists only in the Rules of Procedure and carries no independent constitutional status.
  • Overlooking that post‑legislative scrutiny has no mandatory statutory basis in India — unlike the UK’s established committee-led PLS practice.
  • Assuming the Judge Inquiry Committee’s report is binding on Parliament — the Houses must still vote on the motion; the Committee’s finding of “proved misbehaviour” is a precondition, not a verdict.
  • Confusing the Leader of the House with the Chief Whip — the LoH sets legislative strategy; the Whip enforces party attendance and voting discipline.

Parliamentary Oversight of the National Security Council and Strategic Decision‑Making

The National Security Council (NSC) was constituted in 1998 by a Cabinet resolution under Prime Minister Atal Bihari Vajpayee. It is chaired by the Prime Minister and includes the National Security Adviser (NSA), the Ministers of Defence, Home, External Affairs and Finance, and the Chief of Defence Staff (since 2020). Unlike the Cabinet Committee on Security (CCS), the NSC is an advisory body; its deliberations are classified and its minutes are not tabled in Parliament. The primary parliamentary window into NSC functioning is the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Defence, which examines defence policy, strategic doctrine and the annual defence budget. In a 2018 report, the committee recommended that the NSC produce a written national security strategy for parliamentary scrutiny. The government has since drafted a National Security Strategy, but it remains classified. The Public Accounts Committee (PAC) and the Estimates Committee also review expenditure incurred on strategic programmes such as the Strategic Forces Command (SFC) and the Nuclear Command Authority. A CAG audit of the SFC highlighted gaps in parliamentary oversight of nuclear command‑and‑control expenditure, noting that the PAC had not examined the SFC's accounts for several years. The absence of a dedicated parliamentary committee on national security — unlike the US Senate Select Committee on Intelligence — means that oversight relies on ad‑hoc briefings to the Defence Committee and the occasional secret sitting of the House under the relevant rules. A 2023 Standing Committee on Defence report urged the government to institutionalise a biannual confidential briefing to the committee on NSC decisions affecting nuclear posture. Until such a mechanism is formalised, parliamentary oversight of the NSC remains episodic and dependent on executive willingness.

Parliamentary Scrutiny of the Defence Budget and Capital Acquisition Process

The defence budget is presented as part of the Union Budget under Demand No. 20 (Ministry of Defence). Capital acquisition, which accounts for roughly 30 % of the defence outlay, is governed by the Defence Procurement Procedure (DPP). The DPP 2020 replaced the DPP 2016 and introduced categories such as “Buy (Indian‑IDDM)”, “Buy (Indian)” and “Buy (Global)”. The Defence Acquisition Council (DAC), chaired by the Defence Minister, accords Acceptance of Necessity (AoN) for each procurement case. Parliamentary scrutiny operates at three levels: (1) the Standing Committee on Defence examines the Demands for Grants, the Annual Acquisition Plan and the DPP amendments; its 2022 report on defence procurement and indigenisation flagged delays in major programmes such as the Light Combat Aircraft (LCA) Mk‑2 and the Future Infantry Combat Vehicle (FICV). (2) The Public Accounts Committee reviews CAG audit reports on capital acquisition; CAG reports have identified significant cost overruns in major deals, though specific figures are often classified. (3) The Estimates Committee evaluates the reasonableness of budgetary allocations; a 2021 report recommended a separate “Capital Acquisition Fund” to insulate procurement from revenue‑expenditure pressures. The “Make in India” and “Atmanirbhar Bharat” initiatives have added a policy layer: the 2020 DPP mandates a minimum 50 % indigenous content for “Buy (Indian‑IDDM)” cases. A 2023 follow‑up report noted that a minority of AoNs in 2022‑23 met the indigenous content threshold, prompting the Ministry to issue a revised “Positive Indigenisation List” in August 2023. The committee also highlighted the absence of a parliamentary “defence procurement watchdog” akin to the US Government Accountability Office’s Defence Acquisition Assessment Programme.

Technique: Identifying the Voting Threshold in GST Council
A question asks: “What is the voting majority required for the GST Council to take a decision, and which constitutional provision prescribes it?”
The GST Council decides by a three‑fourths majority of the weighted votes of members present and voting (Article 279A(9)). The Union Government’s vote carries a weight of one‑third of the total votes cast, while all State Governments together carry two‑thirds. This threshold is higher than a simple majority and is explicitly laid down in Article 279A(9) of the Constitution (inserted by the 101st Amendment, 2016).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question cites a specific constitutional article (e.g., Article 279A), locate the exact clause in the bare act. Memorise the voting weight distribution (Centre 1/3, States 2/3) and the three‑fourths majority rule; these are frequently tested in both prelims and mains. For defence procurement, remember the hierarchy: DAC → AoN → DPP category → Parliamentary Committee review. A quick mental flowchart saves time.

Parliamentary Oversight of the Central Bureau of Investigation: Autonomy, Appointment and Accountability

The Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI) derives its powers from the Delhi Special Police Establishment (DSPE) Act, 1946. The Director is appointed by a high‑powered committee comprising the Prime Minister (chair), the Leader of the Opposition in Lok Sabha (or the leader of the single largest opposition party) and the Chief Justice of India (or a Supreme Court judge nominated by the CJI). This mechanism was mandated by the Supreme Court in Vineet Narain v. Union of India (1998) and reinforced in Common Cause v. Union of India (2014). The 2019 judgment in Alok Kumar Verma v. Union of India directed the government to appoint the Director strictly as per the committee’s recommendation, curbing unilateral executive action. Parliamentary oversight is exercised primarily through the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Home Affairs, which examines the CBI’s annual report, its functional autonomy and the status of high‑profile cases. A 2020 committee report highlighted significant vacancy rates in senior investigative posts and recommended a fixed tenure of two years for the Director, extendable by one year only with committee approval. The Public Accounts Committee reviews CAG audits of CBI expenditure; a 2021 CAG audit flagged irregularities in the procurement of forensic software. The Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act, 2013, also provides for the Lokpal to supervise CBI investigations into offences under the Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988, adding a layer of external accountability. However, the CBI’s “single directive” requirement — prior government approval for investigating officers of the rank of Joint Secretary and above — was struck down by the Supreme Court in 2014 as unconstitutional. The Standing Committee on Home Affairs has repeatedly urged amendment of Section 6A of the DSPE Act to replace it with a collegial approval mechanism.

Parliamentary Scrutiny of the Goods and Services Tax Council: Federal Decision‑Making and Revenue Sharing

The GST Council, constituted under Article 279A (inserted by the 101st Constitutional Amendment, 2016), is the apex federal body for GST policy. It comprises the Union Finance Minister (Chair), the Union Minister of State for Finance, and the Finance Ministers of all States and Union Territories with legislatures. Decisions require a three‑fourths majority of the weighted votes of members present and voting; the Centre’s vote carries one‑third weight, the States together two‑thirds. The Council’s recommendations are binding on both Parliament and State Legislatures. Parliamentary scrutiny occurs through three channels: (1) The Standing Committee on Finance examines the Council’s recommendations before they are incorporated into the Central GST (CGST) and Integrated GST (IGST) Acts; a 2018 report criticised the frequent rate changes and the lack of a dispute‑resolution mechanism. (2) The Comptroller and Auditor General audits GST revenue collection and the compensation cess; a 2020 CAG report revealed a substantial shortfall in GST compensation to states for 2019‑20, prompting the Council to extend the compensation cess beyond the original five‑year period. (3) The Estimates Committee reviews the fiscal impact of rate rationalisation; a 2022 report recommended a “rate stability framework” with a minimum two‑year moratorium on rate changes. The Council’s 2023 decision to bring petroleum products under GST was deferred due to lack of consensus, illustrating the federal bargaining dynamics. A recent Standing Committee on Finance report suggested live‑streaming Council meetings and publishing minutes within 30 days, a proposal yet to be accepted by the Council.

Parliamentary Oversight of the Reserve Bank of India: Monetary Policy, Autonomy and Accountability

The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) operates under the RBI Act, 1934, as amended in 2016 to establish the Monetary Policy Committee (MPC). The MPC comprises six members — three from the RBI (including the Governor) and three external members appointed by the Central Government for a four‑year term. The Governor, appointed by the Appointments Committee of the Cabinet, serves a three‑year term, extendable by two years. Parliamentary oversight is exercised primarily by the Standing Committee on Finance, which summons the Governor for an annual “Monetary Policy Review” hearing. In 2018, Governor Urjit Patel’s resignation amid differences with the Ministry of Finance over the RBI’s surplus transfer and the “prompt corrective action” framework triggered a debate on central bank autonomy. A 2019 committee report recommended a statutory “autonomy charter” defining the RBI’s operational independence, a proposal not yet legislated. The Public Accounts Committee examines the RBI’s annual report and the CAG audit of its accounts; a 2021 CAG audit highlighted deficiencies in the RBI’s oversight of non‑banking financial companies (NBFCs) during the IL&FS crisis. The Estimates Committee evaluates the fiscal implications of monetary policy, particularly the impact of repo‑rate changes on government borrowing costs. The MPC’s mandated review of the inflation targeting framework was tabled before Parliament, and the Standing Committee on Finance held a sitting to assess the 4 % ± 2 % inflation target’s credibility. The RBI’s recent currency management report, showing a significant increase in currency in circulation post‑demonetisation, was also scrutinised for its implications on fiscal deficit financing.

  • Assuming the National Security Council has a dedicated parliamentary committee — no such committee exists; oversight is fragmented across the Defence Committee, PAC and ad‑hoc secret sessions.
  • Confusing the Defence Acquisition Council’s AoN with final contract award — AoN is only the first of three stages (AoN, RFP, Contract).
  • Believing the CBI Director can be removed by the government alone — removal requires the high‑powered committee’s recommendation and Supreme Court approval in certain cases.
  • Thinking the GST Council decides by simple majority — the Constitution mandates a three‑fourths weighted majority (Centre 1/3, States 2/3).
  • Assuming the RBI Governor appears before Parliament only during the Budget Session — the Standing Committee on Finance holds a separate annual monetary policy hearing, usually in February.
  • Overlooking that the MPC’s external members are appointed by the Central Government, not by the RBI — this affects perceptions of independence.
  • Mixing up the “single directive” under the DSPE Act with the Lokpal’s supervisory powers — they operate in parallel but have distinct legal bases.

These advanced sections equip aspirants to tackle nuanced questions on institutional oversight mechanisms that are frequently tested in mains answer‑writing and interview discussions.

Standing Committee on Finance: Mandate, Working and Impact on Fiscal Policy

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Finance was constituted in 1993 under the Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha. It consists of 31 members — 21 from Lok Sabha and 10 from Rajya Sabha — nominated by the Speaker and the Chairman respectively, reflecting the party strength in each House. The Committee examines the Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Finance, the Reserve Bank of India, the Securities and Exchange Board of India, the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority and the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority. It also scrutinises the Annual Financial Statement, the Medium‑Term Fiscal Policy Statement and the Fiscal Policy Strategy Statement laid before Parliament under the Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management Act, 2003.

Its working method combines written memoranda from the Ministry, oral evidence from the Finance Secretary, the RBI Governor and the Chairpersons of financial regulators, and on‑the‑spot visits to institutions such as the National Institute of Public Finance and Policy. Reports are adopted by consensus; dissent notes are appended when members disagree. The 2018 report on the Goods and Services Tax (GST) highlighted frequent rate changes, the absence of a dispute‑resolution mechanism and the need for a “rate stability framework” with a two‑year moratorium on rate revisions. The 2020 report on the RBI’s surplus transfer recommended a statutory “autonomy charter” defining the central bank’s operational independence, a proposal that remains under legislative consideration.

The Committee’s impact is measured by the acceptance rate of its recommendations. Between 2014 and 2023, the Ministry of Finance accepted 68 % of the Committee’s suggestions on tax administration, 54 % on fiscal consolidation and 41 % on financial sector regulation. The 2022 report on the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC) urged a time‑bound resolution process for small‑value cases; the government subsequently amended the IBC in 2023 to introduce a pre‑packaged resolution framework for MSMEs. The Committee also monitors the implementation of the FRBM Act’s escape clause, questioning the Centre’s invocation of the clause in 2020‑21 and 2021‑22.

Key Procedural Features

  • Reports are presented to both Houses; the Minister concerned must reply within 30 days.
  • The Committee can summon any officer of the Ministry or regulator; refusal constitutes a breach of privilege.
  • Sub‑committees may be formed for detailed examination of specific subjects such as cryptocurrency regulation or public sector bank recapitalisation.

Committee on Defence: Structure, Functioning and Oversight of Capital Acquisition

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Defence, also constituted in 1993, comprises 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha). Its mandate covers the Ministry of Defence, the Department of Defence Production, the Department of Defence Research and Development, the three Services and the Ordnance Factory Board (now corporatised into seven Defence Public Sector Undertakings). The Committee examines the Defence Services Estimates, the Capital Acquisition Plan, the Defence Procurement Procedure (DPP) and the annual report of the Defence Research and Development Organisation (DRDO).

Its scrutiny follows a three‑stage cycle: (1) pre‑budget examination of the Demand for Grants, (2) mid‑year review of the Acceptance of Necessity (AoN) clearances and Request for Proposal (RFP) issuance, and (3) post‑audit of contract execution through the Comptroller and Auditor General’s reports. The 2019 report on the Medium Multi‑Role Combat Aircraft (MMRCA) procurement criticised the prolonged AoN‑to‑contract timeline — averaging 7.2 years — and recommended a “fast‑track” clause for critical platforms. The 2021 report on the indigenous Light Combat Aircraft (LCA) Tejas highlighted delays in the supply chain of the Kaveri engine and urged a separate budget line for engine development.

The Committee’s recommendations have influenced policy: the 2020 DPP amendment introduced a “Buy (Indian‑IDDM)” category with a 50 % indigenous content threshold, directly reflecting the Committee’s 2018 suggestion. The 2022 report on the Strategic Partnership model for naval utility helicopters led to the issuance of a fresh RFP with relaxed offset obligations. However, the Committee’s oversight is constrained by the classified nature of many procurement files; it receives only sanitized summaries unless a special in‑camera session is convened, which has occurred only twice since 2014.

Operational Challenges

  • Limited technical expertise among members; the Committee relies on DRDO and Service Headquarters for domain knowledge.
  • Frequent changes in the Defence Minister and the Secretary (Defence Production) disrupt continuity of follow‑up.
  • Absence of a dedicated secretariat with defence‑economics analysts hampers independent cost‑benefit analysis.
Examiner's Twist
A question asks: “Explain why the Standing Committee on Defence cannot directly access classified procurement files, and suggest two institutional reforms to strengthen its oversight without compromising national security.”
The Committee’s access is restricted by the Official Secrets Act, 1923 and the Defence Procurement Procedure, which classify AoN, RFP and contract details as “Secret” or “Top Secret”. Only the Cabinet Committee on Security (CCS) and the Defence Minister hold full clearance. Reforms: (1) Create a permanent “Parliamentary Defence Oversight Cell” staffed by officers with appropriate security clearance, reporting directly to the Committee; (2) Mandate a quarterly in‑camera briefing by the Defence Secretary on the status of all AoN‑to‑contract pipelines, with a redacted summary tabled in the Committee’s report. Both measures preserve classification while ensuring continuous parliamentary scrutiny.
Answer‑Writing Strategy
When a question asks for “institutional reforms”, structure the answer in three parts: (i) identify the legal/ procedural barrier, (ii) propose a concrete mechanism (new body, mandatory briefing, statutory amendment), (iii) explain how the mechanism balances accountability and secrecy. Use the Committee’s own reports (cite year) as evidence of the gap.

Committee on Home Affairs: Internal Security, Police Reforms and Border Management

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Home Affairs, constituted in 1993, examines the Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA), the Central Armed Police Forces (CAPFs), the Intelligence Bureau, the National Investigation Agency, the National Disaster Management Authority and the Border Security Force. Its 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) review the Demand for Grants, the Annual Report of the MHA and the reports of the National Police Commission (1979‑81) and the Second Administrative Reforms Commission (2005‑09) as they relate to police reforms.

The Committee’s 2017 report on “Police Reforms: Implementation of Supreme Court Directives in Prakash Singh (2006)” found that only 12 states had constituted State Security Commissions, 9 had established Police Establishment Boards and 7 had fixed a minimum tenure for the Director General of Police. The 2020 report on “Border Management and Coastal Security” highlighted gaps in the Comprehensive Integrated Border Management System (CIBMS) — only 48 % of the sanctioned 2,000 km of smart fencing was operational by March 2020. The Committee recommended a dedicated “Border Technology Fund” and a joint task force with the Ministry of Defence for riverine border surveillance.

In 2022, the Committee examined the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act (UAPA) amendment of 2019, noting a 37 % increase in UAPA cases between 2019 and 2021. It urged the MHA to publish an annual “UAPA Implementation Review” disaggregated by state, offence category and conviction rate. The Ministry accepted the recommendation in principle but has not yet institutionalised the review. The Committee also monitors the implementation of the National Disaster Management Plan, questioning the delay in the constitution of the National Disaster Mitigation Fund mandated under the Disaster Management Act, 2005.

Key Oversight Tools

  • Calling for “Action Taken Reports” (ATRs) on each recommendation within six months.
  • Conducting field visits to CAPF camps in Left‑Wing Extremism (LWE) affected districts.
  • Summoning the Home Secretary and the Director, Intelligence Bureau for in‑camera briefings on internal security threat assessments.

Committee on Agriculture: Food Security, MSP Mechanism and Climate Resilience

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Agriculture, constituted in 1993, oversees the Ministry of Agriculture and Farmers’ Welfare, the Department of Agricultural Research and Education (DARE), the Indian Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR), the Food Corporation of India (FCI) and the National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD). Its 31 members examine the Demand for Grants, the Annual Report of the Ministry, the Report of the Commission for Agricultural Costs and Prices (CACP) and the Economic Survey’s agriculture chapter.

The Committee’s 2018 report on “Minimum Support Price (MSP) and Procurement Policy” analysed the CACP’s cost‑plus‑50 % formula introduced in 2018‑19. It found that procurement at MSP covered only 38 % of paddy and 22 % of wheat production, leaving a large marketable surplus exposed to price volatility. The Committee recommended a statutory MSP guarantee for 23 crops, a proposal that remains under legislative debate. The 2021 report on “Climate‑Resilient Agriculture” urged the integration of the National Innovations on Climate Resilient Agriculture (NICRA) outputs into the Pradhan Mantri Fasal Bima Yojana (PMFBY) premium setting. The Ministry accepted the recommendation and, in 2022, directed the Agriculture Insurance Company of India to incorporate district‑level climate risk indices.

The Committee also scrutinises the implementation of the Pradhan Mantri Kisan Samman Nidhi (PM‑KISAN). Its 2020 report identified a 12 % exclusion error due to outdated land records and recommended a “real‑time land‑record linkage” with the PM‑KISAN portal. The Ministry launched the “Digital Agriculture Mission” in 2021 to address this. On the FCI front, the 2022 report highlighted a storage gap of 34 million tonnes against the normative requirement, prompting the government to approve 100 new silos under the Public‑Private Partnership mode.

Emerging Focus Areas

  • Digital agriculture: satellite‑based crop health monitoring, AI‑driven advisory services.
  • Agri‑export clusters: monitoring the “One District One Product” scheme for horticulture.
  • Farmer Producer Organisations (FPOs): evaluating the ₹ 6,865 crore FPO formation scheme (2020‑24).

Committee on Labour: Labour Codes, Social Security and Gig‑Worker Protection

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Labour, constituted in 2004 (later than the 1993 batch), examines the Ministry of Labour and Employment, the Employees’ Provident Fund Organisation (EPFO), the Employees’ State Insurance Corporation (ESIC), the Directorate General of Mines Safety and the National Labour Institute. Its 31 members review the four Labour Codes passed in 2019‑20 — the Code on Wages, the Industrial Relations Code, the Social Security Code and the Occupational Safety, Health and Working Conditions Code — and monitor their notification and rule‑making.

The Committee’s 2020 report on the “Code on Wages” examined the floor wage fixation methodology. It noted that the National Floor Level Minimum Wage (NFLMW) remained at ₹ 178 per day since 2017, while the Code empowers the Central Government to fix a “national floor wage” after consulting the Central Advisory Board. The Committee recommended a time‑bound revision linked to the Consumer Price Index for Industrial Workers (CPI‑IW). The 2021 report on the “Social Security Code” highlighted the exclusion of gig and platform workers from the definition of “employee”. It urged the Ministry to frame separate “Gig Worker Social Security Rules” providing portable benefits across platforms. The Ministry released draft rules in 2022, but final notification is pending.

On occupational safety, the 2022 report on the “Occupational Safety, Health and Working Conditions Code” analysed the provision for a “National Occupational Safety and Health Advisory Board”. It found that the Board had not been constituted two years after the Code’s passage. The Committee recommended an interim “Tripartite Safety Council” with representation from trade unions, employer associations and the Ministry. The Ministry constituted the Council in early 2023. The Committee also tracks the EPFO’s investment pattern; its 2023 report flagged a 9.2 % exposure to equity‑linked schemes, exceeding the 5 % ceiling prescribed by the EPF Scheme, 1952, and called for immediate rebalancing.

Implementation Gaps

  • Delayed notification of the Labour Codes — only the Code on Wages has been partially notified (2021).
  • Absence of a unified labour inspection framework across states.
  • Inadequate data on migrant workers; the Committee recommended a national migrant worker database linked to Aadhaar and the e‑Shram portal.
  • Confusing the Standing Committee on Finance’s role with the Public Accounts Committee — the former examines policy and estimates, the latter audits expenditure after the fact.
  • Assuming the Committee on Defence can veto procurement contracts — it only reviews and recommends; final authority rests with the Cabinet Committee on Security.
  • Believing the Committee on Home Affairs has statutory powers to enforce police reforms — its recommendations are advisory; implementation depends on state governments.
  • Thinking the Committee on Agriculture can set MSP — MSP is announced by the Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs based on CACP recommendations.
  • Overlooking that the Committee on Labour’s reports on the Labour Codes are pre‑legislative; the Codes themselves become law only after Presidential assent and notification.
  • Mixing up the Committee on Labour with the Committee on Welfare of SC/ST/OBC — the former deals with labour legislation, the latter with social‑justice programmes.
  • Remember that each Department‑Related Standing Committee operates under the same Rules of Procedure but its effectiveness varies with the political composition and the Ministry’s willingness to accept ATRs.

Parliamentary Committee on Information Technology: Mandate, Digital Governance Oversight and Emerging Challenges

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Information Technology was constituted under the Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha rules governing department‑related committees. It comprises 31 members — 21 from Lok Sabha and 10 from Rajya Sabha — nominated by the Speaker and the Chairman respectively, reflecting the party composition of both Houses. The Committee examines the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY), the Department of Telecommunications (DoT), the Department of Posts, and statutory bodies such as the Unique Identification Authority of India (UIDAI), the National Informatics Centre (NIC), and the Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI). Its mandate covers policy formulation, legislative proposals, budgetary demands, and the implementation of flagship programmes including Digital India, BharatNet, the National Cyber Security Policy, and the Personal Data Protection framework.

In its report on the Personal Data Protection Bill, the Committee recommended a narrower definition of “sensitive personal data”, a mandatory data localisation clause for critical sectors, and an independent Data Protection Authority with adjudicatory powers. Several suggestions were incorporated in the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, notably a graded penalty structure and a Data Protection Board. A subsequent report on BharatNet highlighted a significant shortfall in gram panchayat connectivity against targets, urging the DoT to adopt a “state‑led implementation model” with viability gap funding. The Ministry revised the rollout plan with enhanced allocation. On cyber security, the Committee examined the National Cyber Security Strategy and flagged the absence of a dedicated Computer Emergency Response Team for the financial sector (CERT‑Fin), recommending its creation under the Reserve Bank of India’s oversight; the RBI subsequently issued a framework for the same.

The Committee’s effectiveness hinges on timely Action Taken Reports (ATRs) from ministries. ATR compliance rates have varied across ministries. The Committee has increasingly used “subject‑specific sub‑committees” — for instance, a sub‑committee on Artificial Intelligence — to deepen technical scrutiny. However, the lack of in‑house technical expertise remains a structural constraint; reliance on external domain experts can delay report finalisation.

Parliamentary Committee on Urban Development: Housing, Smart Cities and Metropolitan Governance

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Urban Development, originally constituted in the early 1990s and renamed the Committee on Housing and Urban Affairs in 2017, consists of 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha). It scrutinises the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) and its attached organisations — the Central Public Works Department (CPWD), the National Buildings Construction Corporation (NBCC), the Delhi Metro Rail Corporation (DMRC), and the various Smart City Special Purpose Vehicles (SPVs). The Committee’s work programme covers the Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana – Urban (PMAY‑U), the Atal Mission for Rejuvenation and Urban Transformation (AMRUT), the Smart Cities Mission, the Metro Rail Policy, and the National Urban Transport Policy.

A report on the Smart Cities Mission evaluated selected cities and found that fund utilisation remained low relative to projections. It identified procurement delays, weak SPV governance, and inadequate citizen participation as primary bottlenecks. The Committee recommended a “mission‑mode” monitoring dashboard with real‑time fund‑flow tracking, which MoHUA launched as the “Smart Cities Geospatial Management Information System”. On PMAY‑U, a report noted a shortfall in sanctioned houses against targets, attributing it to land‑availability disputes in several states. The Committee urged a “land‑bank” model and fast‑tracking of the “Affordable Rental Housing Complexes” (ARHC) sub‑scheme; the Ministry issued revised guidelines, leading to improved sanctions. Regarding metro rail, a report examined the Metro Rail Policy and operational networks, highlighting cost overruns and recommending a standardised “Public‑Private Partnership” (PPP) framework with risk‑sharing clauses. The Ministry adopted a revised PPP model applied to new metro projects. The Committee also monitors the “National Urban Digital Mission” (NUDM); its interim report stressed the need for interoperable data standards across municipal corporations.

Technique: Distinguishing Recommendatory from Binding Powers
Which of the following statements about the Committee on Housing and Urban Affairs is correct?
1. Its recommendations on Metro Rail projects are binding on the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs.
2. It can veto the allocation of funds under the Smart Cities Mission.
3. Its reports are tabled in both Houses and the Ministry must submit an Action Taken Report within six months.
4. It has the power to summon the Chief Minister of a state for urban governance matters.
Correct answer: 3. Department‑Related Standing Committees, including the Committee on Housing and Urban Affairs, have only recommendatory powers. Their reports are laid on the Table of both Houses, and the concerned Ministry is required to furnish an Action Taken Report (ATR) usually within six months (Rule 331E). They cannot veto allocations, bind the executive, or summon state Chief Ministers.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the “powers” of any Department‑Related Standing Committee, remember the uniform rule: they are advisory. Look for keywords such as “binding”, “veto”, “summon”, or “enforce” — these are always incorrect. The only mandatory procedural step is the submission of an ATR by the Ministry. Memorise the standard six‑month ATR timeline; it appears frequently in both prelims and mains.

Parliamentary Committee on Rural Development: MGNREGA, PMAY‑G and Livelihood Convergence

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Rural Development, constituted in the early 1990s, comprises 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) and examines the Ministry of Rural Development (MoRD) and its flagship programmes — the Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (MGNREGA), the Pradhan Mantri Awaas Yojana – Gramin (PMAY‑G), the Deendayal Antyodaya Yojana – National Rural Livelihoods Mission (DAY‑NRLM), and the Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY). The Committee’s reports are tabled in both Houses and the Ministry must respond with an ATR within six months.

A report on MGNREGA analysed wage‑rate revisions and found that the national average wage lagged behind the statutory minimum wage in several states. The Committee recommended a “wage‑indexation mechanism” linked to the Consumer Price Index for Agricultural Labourers (CPI‑AL). The Ministry subsequently issued a notification linking MGNREGA wages to CPI‑AL, resulting in an increase. A report on PMAY‑G highlighted a shortfall in the house target, primarily due to delayed fund releases in some states. The Committee urged a “direct benefit transfer (DBT)‑only” fund flow; MoRD implemented DBT for the entire PMAY‑G budget, improving fund utilisation. On livelihood convergence, a report examined the integration of DAY‑NRLM self‑help groups (SHGs) with MGNREGA works, finding low access among SHG members. The Committee recommended a “convergence portal” mapping SHG databases with MGNREGA job‑card data. MoRD launched a portal, and early data show a rise in SHG participation. The Committee also monitors PMGSY; its report noted significant unconnected road length against targets, urging “green‑road” specifications and climate‑resilient designs.

Parliamentary Committee on Social Justice and Empowerment: Reservation Policy, Scholarship Schemes and Data‑Driven Monitoring

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Social Justice and Empowerment was constituted in 2004 with 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha). It oversees the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment (MoSJE) and the Ministry of Tribal Affairs (MoTA) — though the latter also has a separate Committee on Welfare of Scheduled Tribes. The Committee’s remit includes the implementation of reservation in education and public employment, the Post‑Matric Scholarship Scheme for SC/ST/OBC students, the National Fellowship for Scheduled Castes, the Scheme for Development of Scheduled Castes (SCSP), and the monitoring of the National Commission for Scheduled Castes (NCSC) and the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes (NCST).

A report on the Post‑Matric Scholarship Scheme revealed significant disbursement delays across states with large arrears. The Committee recommended a “centralised scholarship portal” with Aadhaar‑linked DBT and real‑time tracking. MoSJE launched an upgraded portal, reducing average disbursement time substantially. On reservation, a report examined the “creamy layer” exclusion for OBCs and noted that several states had not revised income ceilings for years. The Committee urged a uniform revision linked to the Consumer Price Index; the Department of Personnel and Training issued an Office Memorandum raising the ceiling. A report on the SCSP analysed fund utilisation across states and found average utilisation below allocation. It recommended “outcome‑based budgeting” with district‑level scorecards. MoSJE adopted an outcome monitoring framework integrated with NITI Aayog’s “Aspirational Districts” dashboard. The Committee also scrutinises the NCSC; a report highlighted vacancies in the Commission’s investigative staff, recommending immediate recruitment. The Ministry filled a substantial portion of posts.

Parliamentary Committee on Water Resources: Inter‑State River Disputes, Jal Jeevan Mission and Groundwater Regulation

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Water Resources, constituted in the early 1990s, consists of 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) and examines the Ministry of Jal Shakti, the Central Water Commission (CWC), the Central Ground Water Board (CGWB), the National Water Development Agency (NWDA), and the various Inter‑State River Water Dispute Tribunals. Its oversight spans the Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM), the Namami Gange programme, the Pradhan Mantri Krishi Sinchayee Yojana (PMKSY), the Dam Safety Act, 2021, and the Model Groundwater (Sustainable Management) Bill.

A report on the Jal Jeevan Mission assessed the “Har Ghar Jal” target of providing functional household tap connections (FHTCs) to rural households. It identified a significant gap in FHTC coverage, with several states lagging. The Committee recommended a “state‑wise performance index” linked to fund releases. The Ministry introduced a performance grading index; national coverage improved substantially. On inter‑state disputes, a report reviewed major tribunals, noting prolonged adjudication periods. The Committee urged a permanent “River Basin Authority” with technical secretariat support; the Ministry drafted a River Basin Management Bill under parliamentary consideration. Regarding groundwater, a report examined the National Aquifer Mapping Programme and found numerous assessment units classified as “over‑exploited”. The Committee recommended mandatory “water‑use efficiency” audits for major industrial extractors and integration of groundwater data with the National Water Informatics Centre. The Ministry issued groundwater extraction guidelines incorporating audit requirements. On dam safety, a report monitored the Dam Safety Act implementation, noting that many large dams had not completed mandatory safety inspections. The Committee pressed for a centralised “Dam Health Monitoring System”, which the CWC launched in pilot mode.

  • Assuming any Department‑Related Standing Committee can enforce its recommendations — all such committees are purely advisory; only the executive can act.
  • Confusing the Committee on Information Technology with the Committee on Communications — the former deals with MeitY and DoT, the latter (if constituted) would handle the Ministry of Communications separately.
  • Believing the Committee on Urban Development can directly allocate Smart Cities funds — fund allocation is a Cabinet decision; the Committee only reviews utilisation.
  • Thinking the Committee on Rural Development can set MGNREGA wage rates — wages are fixed by the Central Government on the basis of CPI‑AL; the Committee only recommends indexation mechanisms.
  • Mixing up the Committee on Social Justice and Empowerment with the Committee on Welfare of SC/ST/OBC — the former handles scholarship and reservation policy, the latter focuses on welfare programme implementation.
  • Assuming the Committee on Water Resources can adjudicate inter‑state river disputes — adjudication rests with tribunals constituted under the Inter‑State River Water Disputes Act; the Committee only reviews tribunal functioning.
  • Overlooking the six‑month ATR deadline — while the rule prescribes six months, ministries often delay; examiners test awareness of this procedural requirement.

Select Committees and Joint Committees: Constitution, Working and Legislative Impact

Select Committees and Joint Committees are ad‑hoc bodies constituted for the detailed examination of a specific Bill or subject. A Select Committee is formed by one House — Lok Sabha or Rajya Sabha — while a Joint Committee draws members from both Houses in proportion to their strength (typically 2:1 Lok Sabha:Rajya Sabha). The motion to refer a Bill to a Select Committee is moved after the first reading; the Speaker or Chairman decides the composition, broadly reflecting party strength. The Committee elects its own Chairperson; by practice, a senior ruling‑party member often chairs, though Opposition members have been appointed for Bills of high political sensitivity.

The Committee’s mandate is to scrutinise the Bill clause‑by‑clause, hear expert witnesses, invite memoranda from stakeholders, and propose amendments. Its report, containing the amended Bill and dissent notes, is presented to the House. The House may adopt, reject or recommit the report. Landmark legislation such as the Companies Act, 2013 and the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016 underwent substantial shaping in Select Committees. Joint Committees have been constituted for Bills with significant federal or cross‑cutting implications — for instance, the Constitution (122nd Amendment) Bill (GST) was examined by a Select Committee of Rajya Sabha, while the National Medical Commission Bill, 2019 was scrutinised by the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Health.

Procedurally, the Committee follows the same rules of procedure as the parent House, including quorum (one‑third of members), voting by majority, and the power to summon documents and persons. Deliberations are confidential until the report is laid, encouraging candid expert testimony. However, the advisory nature of the report means the government is not bound to accept amendments; political negotiations often determine the final text. The effectiveness of a Select or Joint Committee therefore hinges on the quality of its membership, the time allotted (typically 30‑90 days), and the willingness of the executive to engage constructively.

Available data indicates a decline in the number of Bills referred to Select Committees over recent Lok Sabhas, raising concerns about legislative scrutiny. The Committee on Rules has examined proposals for mandatory referral thresholds for Bills affecting fundamental rights or the federal structure; such recommendations remain under consideration.

Parliamentary Procedure for the Passage of Ordinary Bills: Detailed Stages from Introduction to Assent
