An ordinary Bill (any Bill other than a Money Bill, Financial Bill, or Constitutional Amendment Bill) follows a defined legislative journey in Parliament. The process begins with the First Reading, where the Minister or Private Member moves for leave to introduce the Bill. After leave is granted, the Bill is published in the Gazette and circulated to members. No debate occurs at this stage.

The Second Reading comprises two sub‑stages: the general discussion on the principles of the Bill, and the clause‑by‑clause consideration. During general discussion, members may move that the Bill be referred to a Select Committee, a Joint Committee, or be circulated for eliciting public opinion. If referred, the Committee examines the Bill and reports back with amendments. Upon receipt of the report, the House proceeds to clause‑by‑clause consideration, where each clause is debated, amended, and voted upon. Amendments must be relevant to the clause and comply with the rules of admissibility.

The Third Reading is a final debate on the Bill as amended. Only formal, verbal, or consequential amendments are permitted. After the Third Reading, the Bill is passed by the originating House and transmitted to the other House, where it undergoes the same three readings. The second House may pass the Bill as received, pass it with amendments, or reject it. If the second House makes amendments, the Bill returns to the originating House for concurrence. Disagreement between the two Houses on amendments can lead to a Joint Sitting under Article 108, convened by the President after a prescribed period (six months from the date of receipt of the Bill by the other House for ordinary Bills).

Once both Houses agree on the final text, the Bill is presented to the President for assent under Article 111. The President may give assent, withhold assent, or return the Bill (if not a Money Bill) for reconsideration. If the Houses pass the Bill again, with or without amendments, the President must give assent. The Bill then becomes an Act, and the date of assent is the date of enactment unless the Act specifies a different commencement date.

Key procedural safeguards include the requirement of a quorum (one‑tenth of total membership) at each stage, the sub‑judice rule restricting discussion on matters pending before courts, and the role of the Speaker/Chairman in certifying the Bill’s classification. The entire process, from introduction to assent, can span a single session or multiple sessions, depending on political consensus and the legislative calendar.

Worked Example: Tracing a Bill through Parliament
A Government Bill to amend the Representation of the People Act is introduced in Lok Sabha. After the First Reading, the Opposition moves a motion to refer it to a Joint Committee. The motion is adopted. The Joint Committee submits its report with amendments after 45 days. Lok Sabha adopts the report at the clause‑by‑clause stage, passes the Bill at Third Reading, and sends it to Rajya Sabha. Rajya Sabha passes the Bill with two further amendments. Lok Sabha disagrees with one amendment. Explain the subsequent procedural steps.
Since the Houses disagree on an amendment, the Bill does not proceed to the President. Under Article 108, the President may summon a Joint Sitting of both Houses after six months from the date of receipt of the Bill by Rajya Sabha (or earlier if both Houses agree). At the Joint Sitting, the disputed amendment is put to vote; if passed by a majority of total members present and voting, it becomes part of the Bill. The Bill, as finally agreed, is then presented to the President for assent. If the President returns it for reconsideration, both Houses must pass it again, after which assent is obligatory.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three‑reading sequence and the specific triggers for a Joint Sitting (six‑month deadlock, disagreement on amendments). In MCQs, watch for distractors that confuse Money Bill procedure (no Joint Sitting) with ordinary Bill procedure. For descriptive answers, structure the response chronologically: Introduction → First Reading → Second Reading (general discussion, referral, clause‑by‑clause) → Third Reading → Transmission → Second House → Joint Sitting (if needed) → Presidential Assent. Cite Article numbers (107, 108, 111) to score extra marks.

Parliamentary Oversight of the National Human Rights Commission: Mandate, Reporting and Accountability

The National Human Rights Commission (NHRC) was established under the Protection of Human Rights Act, 1993, as a statutory body with the mandate to inquire into violations of human rights, review safeguards, and recommend remedial measures. Parliamentary oversight of the NHRC operates through multiple channels: the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Home Affairs examines the Commission’s annual report, the Committee on Petitions receives individual complaints about NHRC’s functioning, and the House debates the Commission’s recommendations during the discussion on the Ministry of Home Affairs’ Demands for Grants.

The NHRC submits its annual report to the Central Government, which lays it before each House of Parliament. The Standing Committee on Home Affairs scrutinises the report, focusing on the disposal rate of cases, the timeliness of inquiries, the implementation of recommendations, and the adequacy of the Commission’s budget and staffing. Recent Committee reports have highlighted significant case backlogs and recommended the creation of additional benches and the filling of vacant judicial and non‑judicial posts. The Ministry has responded with measures such as sanctioning additional posts and approving a digital case‑management system.

Parliament also exercises oversight through the Question Hour and Zero Hour, where members raise specific instances of alleged NHRC inaction or delay. The Committee on Petitions provides a direct grievance‑redressal route: citizens can petition Parliament if they believe the NHRC has failed to act. The Committee may call for a report from the Ministry and recommend corrective action.

Judicial pronouncements have reinforced parliamentary oversight. The Supreme Court has held that the Commission’s recommendations, while not binding, carry persuasive authority and that the government must respond within a reasonable time. This principle is frequently cited in Committee reports to press for timely Action Taken Reports (ATRs). The Protection of Human Rights (Amendment) Act, 2019 introduced changes to the composition and tenure of members; subsequent executive instructions have emphasised the need for timely ATRs, strengthening the accountability loop.

Despite these mechanisms, challenges persist: the NHRC’s recommendations are often not implemented, the Commission lacks independent investigative staff, and its budgetary dependence on the Ministry of Home Affairs can affect autonomy. Parliamentary committees continue to advocate for financial independence, a separate secretariat, and the power to enforce its orders through contempt proceedings.

Parliamentary Scrutiny of the Central Vigilance Commission and Anti‑Corruption Architecture

The Central Vigilance Commission (CVC) is the apex vigilance institution, created by a 1964 Government resolution and given statutory status by the Central Vigilance Commission Act, 2003. Its mandate covers vigilance administration in Central Government ministries, departments, and public sector undertakings. Parliamentary scrutiny of the CVC is exercised primarily through the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice, which examines the CVC’s annual report, and through the Committee on Public Accounts (PAC) when vigilance matters intersect with audit findings.

The CVC’s annual report, laid before each House, details the number of complaints received, investigations initiated, disciplinary proceedings recommended, and the status of prosecution sanctions. The Standing Committee reviews the disposal rate, the timeliness of granting prosecution sanctions (a frequent bottleneck), and the adequacy of the CVC’s staffing and infrastructure. Committee reports have flagged delays in sanctioning prosecution and recommended statutory timelines coupled with deemed sanction provisions.

Parliament also monitors the CVC through the Question Hour, where members seek data on high‑profile cases, and through Calling Attention Motions on alleged vigilance failures. The Committee on Petitions provides a channel for whistle‑blowers and public servants to bring instances of CVC inaction to Parliament’s notice. Amendments to the CVC Act have introduced provisions for enhanced reporting to the Standing Committee, improving real‑time oversight.

Judicial intervention has shaped the accountability framework. The Supreme Court has directed that the selection of the Central Vigilance Commissioner and Vigilance Commissioners follow a transparent, high‑powered committee process, a recommendation later incorporated into amendments. The Court also emphasised that the CVC’s advice to the government on vigilance matters, while not binding, must be recorded and reasons for deviation communicated to Parliament.

Emerging challenges include the overlap between the CVC, the Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI), and the Lokpal, leading to jurisdictional ambiguities. The Standing Committee on Personnel has repeatedly called for a unified anti‑corruption architecture with clear demarcation of investigative, prosecutorial, and advisory functions. Recent reports have recommended a legislative framework to integrate these bodies under a single oversight statute, a proposal currently under inter‑ministerial consultation.

  • Confusing Select Committee referral with Joint Committee referral — Select Committees are single‑House bodies; Joint Committees require a motion in both Houses.
  • Assuming the President can return a Money Bill for reconsideration — Money Bills cannot be returned; the President must either assent or withhold assent.
  • Believing the NHRC’s recommendations are legally binding on the government — they are persuasive but not enforceable without legislative backing.
  • Overlooking the requirement for timely government Action Taken Reports on NHRC recommendations — executive instructions and Committee pressure emphasise this accountability loop.
  • Thinking the CVC can directly prosecute corruption cases — it only recommends prosecution; the sanctioning authority is the competent government department.
  • Remember to avoid these traps in the exam.

Joint Parliamentary Committees: Constitution, Powers and Landmark Inquiries

A Joint Parliamentary Committee (JPC) is an ad‑hoc body constituted by a motion passed in both Houses of Parliament. Unlike the permanent Department‑Related Standing Committees, a JPC draws its membership proportionally from Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha, usually in a 2:1 ratio reflecting the numerical strength of the two Houses. The motion that creates a JPC specifies its terms of reference, the period within which it must report, and the quorum (normally one‑third of its total membership). The committee enjoys the same powers as a House committee: it can summon witnesses, call for documents, administer oaths, and examine evidence on oath. Its report is presented to both Houses and, once adopted, acquires the status of a parliamentary recommendation that the government is expected to act upon.

Historically, JPCs have been invoked for high‑profile investigations where the executive’s accountability is under intense public scrutiny. The first major JPC was constituted in 1987 to probe the Bofors howitzer deal; its 1990 report, though inconclusive on criminal liability, established the precedent that a JPC can access classified defence documents. The 2G spectrum allocation JPC (2011‑2013) examined the allocation of 122 licences and recommended cancellation of licences, a recommendation that fed into the Supreme Court’s 2012 judgement cancelling 122 licences. The Coal Block Allocation JPC (2012‑2014) similarly scrutinised the allocation of 218 coal blocks and its report underpinned the Supreme Court’s 2014 judgement declaring all allocations since 1993 illegal. More recently, the JPC on the Personal Data Protection Bill (2019‑2021) examined the draft legislation clause‑by‑clause, recommending over 80 amendments that shaped the eventual Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023.

Procedurally, a JPC’s report is not binding on the government, but the convention of “government response within six months” has been reinforced by successive Speaker’s directions and by the Committee on Government Assurances, which tracks Action Taken Reports (ATRs). The 2018 amendment to the Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha introduced a mandatory “statement of action taken” to be laid within three months of the report’s presentation, tightening the accountability loop. However, the political composition of a JPC often mirrors the ruling coalition’s strength, leading to allegations of partisan dilution. The 2013 JPC on the 2G scam, for instance, saw a split verdict with the majority (ruling‑party members) dissenting from the opposition’s findings, illustrating how party discipline can affect the committee’s credibility.

From an examiner’s perspective, the key distinctions to remember are: (i) a JPC requires a motion in *both* Houses, whereas a Select Committee needs a motion only in the House where the bill originates; (ii) a JPC’s membership reflects the combined strength of both Houses, while a Select Committee draws members solely from the originating House; (iii) a JPC’s report is laid before *both* Houses, whereas a Select Committee reports only to its parent House. These procedural nuances are frequently tested in “match the following” and “statement‑reason” questions.

Select Committees: Ad Hoc Legislative Scrutiny and Their Procedural Distinctiveness

A Select Committee is a temporary, single‑House committee constituted to examine a specific bill clause‑by‑clause. It is formed when a motion for reference is moved in the House where the bill is introduced — usually Lok Sabha for Money Bills and Financial Bills, and either House for ordinary bills. The motion specifies the committee’s composition (normally 15‑30 members), its quorum (one‑third), and a deadline for reporting, which can be extended by the House. Unlike a JPC, a Select Committee does not include members of the other House, and its report is presented only to the House that constituted it. The committee has the same evidentiary powers as a standing committee: it can summon witnesses, call for papers, and administer oaths.

The procedural trajectory of a bill referred to a Select Committee is distinct. After the committee submits its report, the bill is placed on the agenda for “consideration and passing” in the same House. Members may move amendments based on the committee’s recommendations, and the House debates each clause afresh. If the House adopts the report with amendments, the bill proceeds to the other House (or to the President in the case of a Money Bill). The Select Committee route is often used for complex legislation requiring detailed stakeholder consultation — for example, the Select Committee on the Constitution (One Hundred and Twenty‑Fourth Amendment) Bill, 2019 (the Citizenship Amendment Bill) which heard over 200 representations before recommending the final text. Another notable instance is the Select Committee on the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (Amendment) Bill, 2021, which recommended the inclusion of a “pre‑pack” resolution framework for MSMEs, a suggestion later incorporated into the 2022 amendment.

Key procedural differences from a JPC are often examined: (a) a Select Committee can be constituted *only* by the House where the bill originates; (b) its membership is drawn exclusively from that House; (c) its report is *not* laid before the other House unless the bill itself moves there; (d) the committee lapses automatically once the bill is passed or the session ends, whereas a JPC may continue across sessions until its mandated period expires. The Rules of Procedure of Lok Sabha (Rule 298) and Rajya Sabha (Rule 274) govern the constitution and functioning of Select Committees, and the Speaker/Chairman has discretion to nominate members to ensure representation of opposition parties, though the ruling party usually commands a majority.

Examiners frequently test the distinction between “Select Committee” and “Joint Committee” through scenario‑based questions: “A Bill is referred to a Committee consisting of members from both Houses. Which committee is it?” The correct answer is a Joint Committee (or JPC), not a Select Committee. Another trap is the belief that a Select Committee can amend the Constitution; only a Joint Committee on a Constitution Amendment Bill can be constituted under Article 368, but the amendment itself must be passed by the special majority in each House separately.

Worked Example: Distinguishing Committee Types
A Bill to amend the Representation of the People Act, 1951 is introduced in Lok Sabha. The government moves a motion to refer the Bill to a committee comprising 20 members from Lok Sabha and 10 members from Rajya Sabha. The motion is adopted by both Houses. Which type of committee has been constituted? Explain the procedural consequences for the Bill’s passage.
The committee described is a Joint Parliamentary Committee (JPC) because (i) the motion was adopted by *both* Houses, (ii) its membership draws from both Houses in a 2:1 ratio, and (iii) its report will be laid before both Houses. Procedurally, after the JPC submits its report, the Bill will be considered in Lok Sabha (the originating House) on the basis of the JPC’s recommendations. If Lok Sabha passes the Bill with or without amendments, it proceeds to Rajya Sabha for its concurrence. Since the Bill is not a Money Bill, Rajya Sabha can amend or reject it. The JPC’s report does not bind either House, but the government is expected to table an Action Taken Report within three months under the 2018 Rules amendment.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mentions “motion adopted by both Houses” and “members from both Houses”, instantly flag it as a Joint Committee/JPC. For Select Committees, look for “motion in the House where the Bill originates” and “report laid only in that House”. Memorise the 2:1 membership ratio for JPCs and the 15‑30 member range for Select Committees; these numbers appear in multiple‑choice options.

Parliamentary Committee on Education, Women, Children, Youth and Sports: Oversight of National Education Policy and Youth Development

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Education, Women, Children, Youth and Sports (hereafter the Education Committee) is one of the 24 DRSCs constituted under Rule 331C of the Lok Sabha Rules and Rule 269 of the Rajya Sabha Rules. It comprises 31 members — 21 from Lok Sabha and 10 from Rajya Sabha — reflecting the proportional strength of the two Houses. The Committee’s mandate covers the Ministries of Education, Women and Child Development, and Youth Affairs and Sports. Its primary functions are: (i) examination of Demands for Grants of these ministries; (ii) scrutiny of Bills referred to it by either House; (iii) review of annual reports and performance of autonomous bodies such as the University Grants Commission (UGC), All India Council for Technical Education (AICTE), National Council of Educational Research and Training (NCERT), and the Sports Authority of India (SAI); and (iv) monitoring implementation of flagship schemes like Samagra Shiksha, Beti Bachao Beti Padhao, Khelo India, and the National Education Policy (NEP) 2020.

Since the adoption of NEP 2020, the Committee has produced a series of reports that have shaped legislative and executive action. Its 2021 report on “Implementation of NEP 2020: Challenges and Way Forward” recommended a statutory National Higher Education Regulatory Council (NHERC) to replace the UGC and AICTE, a proposal that informed the Higher Education Commission of India (HECI) Bill, 2022. The Committee’s 2022 report on “Digital Divide in School Education” highlighted that only 24 % of government schools had functional internet connectivity, prompting the Ministry to accelerate the PM e‑VIDYA programme and allocate ₹8,000 crore for digital infrastructure in the 2023‑24 Budget. In the domain of women and child development, the Committee’s 2020 report on “Effectiveness of the POSHAN Abhiyaan” flagged data‑quality issues in the ICDS‑CAS (Common Application Software) and recommended real‑time monitoring dashboards, which were subsequently adopted by the Ministry.

On youth and sports, the Committee’s 2023 report on “Khelo India: Assessment of Talent Identification and Infrastructure” noted that only 12 % of identified athletes transitioned to elite‑level support, recommending a structured “Athlete Development Pathway” with defined funding milestones. The Ministry responded by revising the Khelo India guidelines in 2024 to include a three‑tier funding model. The Committee also examines the functioning of the National Anti‑Doping Agency (NADA) and the Board of Control for Cricket in India (BCCI) when matters of public interest arise, though its jurisdiction over BCCI remains limited due to the latter’s autonomous status.

A recurring examiner theme is the Committee’s role in “post‑legislative scrutiny”. For instance, after the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education (Amendment) Act, 2019 (which extended the RTE Act to private unaided schools), the Committee’s 2020 report assessed compliance and found that 38 % of private schools had not reserved the mandated 25 % seats for economically weaker sections. This finding was cited in the Supreme Court’s 2021 judgement in *Society for Unaided Private Schools of Rajasthan v. Union of India*, where the Court directed states to enforce the reservation norm. Candidates should be ready to link Committee reports to judicial pronouncements and budgetary outcomes.

Parliamentary Committee on Commerce and Industry: Trade Policy, Export Promotion and Regulatory Oversight

The Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry (Commerce Committee) is a DRSC overseeing the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, which includes the Department of Commerce and the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT). It consists of 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) and functions under the same procedural rules as other DRSCs. Its core responsibilities are: (i) examination of Demands for Grants of the two departments; (ii) scrutiny of Bills such as the Foreign Trade (Development and Regulation) Amendment Bill, the Special Economic Zones (Amendment) Bill, and the Competition (Amendment) Bill; (iii) review of annual reports of autonomous bodies like the Export‑Import Bank of India (EXIM Bank), the Directorate General of Foreign Trade (DGFT), the National Productivity Council, and the Indian Institute of Foreign Trade (IIFT); and (iv) monitoring of trade agreements, export promotion schemes (MEIS, RoDTEP, PLI schemes), and the implementation of the National Trade Policy.

The Committee’s 2021 report on “India’s Free Trade Agreements: Impact Assessment” analysed the ASEAN‑India FTA, the India‑Japan CEPA, and the India‑South Korea CEPA. It found that while merchandise exports to ASEAN grew at a CAGR of 9 % between 2010‑2020, the trade deficit widened due to a surge in imports of electronic components and chemicals. The Committee recommended a “strategic review clause” in future FTAs, a suggestion incorporated into the India‑UAE Comprehensive Economic Partnership Agreement (CEPA) signed in 2022, which includes a built‑in review mechanism after five years. In 2022, the Committee’s report on “Production Linked Incentive (PLI) Schemes: Effectiveness and Gaps” highlighted that only 45 % of the allocated ₹1.97 lakh crore had been disbursed by March 2022, mainly due to delayed project clearances. The Ministry responded by streamlining the approval process and introducing a “single‑window” portal in 2023.

On regulatory oversight, the Committee examines the functioning of the Competition Commission of India (CCI) through the annual report laid before Parliament. Its 2020 report on “Digital Markets and Competition Law” recommended amending the Competition Act, 2002 to introduce “ex‑ante” regulations for large digital platforms, a recommendation that fed into the Competition (Amendment) Bill, 2023, which proposes a “Digital Markets Unit” within CCI. The Committee also scrutinises the Directorate General of Trade Remedies (DGTR) on anti‑dumping and countervailing duty investigations. Its 2019 report on “Anti‑Dumping Measures: Transparency and Timeliness” noted that the average investigation period exceeded 18 months, well beyond the WTO‑prescribed 12‑month limit, prompting DGTR to adopt a “fast‑track” procedure for cases involving strategic sectors.

Examiners often test the Committee’s interplay with the executive on trade negotiations. A typical question: “Which parliamentary committee reviews the text of a Free Trade Agreement before it is signed?” The answer is the Committee on Commerce and Industry, which examines the *negotiating mandate* and the *final agreement* through its reports, though the actual signing is an executive act. Another trap is confusing the Commerce Committee with the Committee on External Affairs; the latter deals with diplomatic aspects, while the former focuses on commercial provisions, tariff schedules, and rules of origin.

Parliamentary Committee on Energy: Power Sector Reforms, Renewable Energy Targets and Regulatory Accountability

The Standing Committee on Energy (Energy Committee) is the DRSC responsible for the Ministry of Power and the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy (MNRE). It comprises 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) and operates under the standard DRSC rules. Its mandate includes: (i) examination of Demands for Grants of both ministries; (ii) scrutiny of Bills such as the Electricity (Amendment) Bill, the Energy Conservation (Amendment) Bill, and the National Green Hydrogen Mission Bill; (iii) review of annual reports of Central Public Sector Enterprises (CPSEs) like NTPC, NHPC, Power Grid Corporation, and Solar Energy Corporation of India (SECI); (iv) monitoring of flagship programmes — Deen Dayal Upadhyaya Gram Jyoti Yojana (DDUGJY), Saubhagya, Ujwal DISCOM Assurance Yojana (UDAY), and the National Solar Mission; and (v) oversight of regulatory bodies — Central Electricity Regulatory Commission (CERC), State Electricity Regulatory Commissions (SERCs), and the Appellate Tribunal for Electricity (APTEL).

The Committee’s 2021 report on “Financial Health of Power Distribution Companies (DISCOMs)” revealed that aggregate technical and commercial (AT&C) losses stood at 21.5 % in FY 2020‑21, far above the 15 % target under UDAY. It recommended a “performance‑linked grant” mechanism for states, which was later reflected in the Revamped Distribution Sector Scheme (RDSS) launched in 2022 with a ₹3.03 lakh crore outlay. In 2022, the Committee’s report on “Renewable Energy Integration and Grid Stability” highlighted that variable renewable energy (VRE) share reached 12 % of total generation, but curtailment rates in Tamil Nadu and Gujarat exceeded 8 % due to transmission constraints. The Committee urged accelerated construction of Green Energy Corridors and the operationalisation of the National Renewable Energy Management Centre (NREMC), both of which received budgetary priority in the 2023‑24 Budget.

On the legislative front, the Committee examined the Electricity (Amendment) Bill, 2022, which proposes delicensing of distribution, introduction of “distribution franchisees”, and strengthening of CERC’s powers. Its 2023 report recommended retaining the “universal service obligation” for distribution licensees to protect rural consumers, a suggestion that sparked debate in the House and led to the insertion of a safeguard clause in the Bill’s 2024 version. The Committee also scrutinised the Energy Conservation (Amendment) Bill, 2022, which introduces a carbon credit trading scheme. Its report recommended aligning the scheme with the Bureau of Energy Efficiency’s (BEE) Perform, Achieve and Trade (PAT) mechanism to avoid dual compliance burdens — a recommendation accepted by the Ministry and reflected in the final Act of 2023.

Judicial interaction is a frequent exam theme. The Supreme Court’s 2020 judgement in *M/s. Essar Power Ltd. v. CERC* upheld CERC’s authority to determine tariffs for inter‑state transmission, citing the Energy Committee’s 2018 report that had recommended greater regulatory autonomy for CERC. Similarly, the APTEL’s 2021 order on “Renewable Purchase Obligation (RPO) Compliance” referenced the Committee’s 2019 finding that only 68 % of obligated entities met their RPO targets, prompting the Ministry to enforce penalties under the Electricity Act, 2003. Candidates should be able to trace the chain: Committee report → Ministry action → Regulatory order → Judicial endorsement.

  • Confusing a Joint Parliamentary Committee with a Select Committee — JPCs need motions in both Houses; Select Committees need a motion only in the originating House.
  • Assuming a Select Committee’s report is laid before both Houses — it is presented only to the House that constituted it.
  • Believing the Committee on Education, Women, Children, Youth and Sports can directly amend the NEP — it only reviews implementation and recommends changes; legislative amendment requires a Bill.
  • Thinking the Commerce Committee negotiates Free Trade Agreements — it reviews the negotiating mandate and final text, but negotiation is an executive function.
  • Overlooking that the Energy Committee’s recommendations on DISCOM reforms feed into the Revamped Distribution Sector Scheme — the link between Committee reports and scheme design is a favourite examiner hook.
  • Assuming the Energy Committee has jurisdiction over state electricity boards — it oversees central ministries and CERC; state boards fall under SERCs and state legislatures.
  • Mixing up the Committee on External Affairs with the Committee on Commerce and Industry on trade matters — the former handles diplomatic aspects, the latter commercial provisions.
The above sections collectively deepen the aspirant’s grasp of parliamentary committee architecture, procedural nuances, and the concrete impact of committee work on legislation, policy and judicial outcomes.

Parliamentary Committee on Commerce and Industry: Trade Policy Scrutiny, FDI Review and Export Promotion Oversight

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry examines the work of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, covering both the Department of Commerce and the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT). Its mandate includes scrutiny of the Foreign Trade Policy (FTP), annual Export‑Import (EXIM) data, Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) policy revisions, and the implementation of Production‑Linked Incentive (PLI) schemes across sectors such as electronics, pharmaceuticals, and automobiles. The Committee’s 2021 report on “Ease of Doing Business – Regulatory Reforms” noted a substantial reduction in the average time for business incorporation over recent years, linking the improvement to the SPICe+ portal and the decriminalisation of minor procedural lapses under the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020.

In 2022 the Committee reviewed the PLI scheme for large‑scale electronics manufacturing and found that a significant portion of committed investment had not yet materialised, mainly due to supply‑chain constraints and delayed clearances from state‑level authorities. It recommended a “single‑window clearance” mechanism at the state level and a mid‑term review clause in the PLI guidelines — recommendations that aligned with the revised PLI framework notified subsequently. The Committee also examined India’s decision to stay out of the Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership (RCEP) in 2019; its 2020 report argued that the absence of adequate safeguards for the dairy and textile sectors justified the opt‑out, a position later reflected in the Ministry’s “Strategic Trade Policy” document.

Judicial interaction is limited but notable. The Supreme Court’s judgement in M/s. Vodafone Idea Ltd. v. Union of India referenced the Committee’s observation that retrospective tax amendments created regulatory uncertainty for foreign investors. The Court upheld the government’s sovereign right to amend tax law but urged Parliament to consider the Committee’s recommendation for a “prospective‑only” amendment principle, a principle the Ministry of Finance indicated it would apply to future tax changes.

Parliamentary Committee on Railways: Capital Expenditure, Safety Audits and Public‑Private Partnership Monitoring

The Standing Committee on Railways oversees the Ministry of Railways, the Railway Board, and associated public sector undertakings such as Indian Railway Finance Corporation (IRFC), RailTel, and the Dedicated Freight Corridor Corporation of India (DFCCIL). Its core functions include examination of the annual Railway Budget (now merged with the Union Budget), scrutiny of capital expenditure on new lines, gauge conversion, electrification, and high‑speed rail projects, and oversight of safety performance indicators such as derailments, collisions, and level‑crossing accidents.

The Committee’s 2020 report “Railway Safety – A Decadal Review” documented a marked decline in consequential train accidents over the previous decade, associating the improvement with the introduction of the “Rashtriya Rail Sanraksha Kosh” (RRSK) fund, the deployment of the Train Protection and Warning System (TPWS) on a substantial stretch of track, and the elimination of numerous unmanned level crossings. However, the report flagged a persistent gap in track renewal targets and recommended a “performance‑linked allocation” for the RRSK, a suggestion that influenced subsequent budgetary allocations.

On public‑private partnerships, the Committee examined the station redevelopment programme (e.g., Habibganj, Gandhinagar) and the Mumbai‑Ahmedabad High‑Speed Rail (MAHSR) corridor. Its 2022 report noted that land acquisition for MAHSR had progressed substantially, but cost escalation had increased the project estimate significantly. The Committee urged the Railway Board to adopt a “fixed‑price EPC” model for future PPP stations and to institutionalise a quarterly “Project Monitoring Dashboard” shared with the Committee — a mechanism that has since been operationalised.

Examiner's twist
A question asks: “Which parliamentary committee examined the implementation of the Production‑Linked Incentive scheme for electronics and recommended a single‑window clearance at the state level?” The answer expects the Committee on Commerce and Industry, not the Committee on Finance or the Committee on Industry (which does not exist as a separate DRSC).
The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry, in its report on the PLI scheme for large‑scale electronics manufacturing, identified delayed state‑level clearances as a key bottleneck and recommended a single‑window clearance mechanism. This recommendation was reflected in the revised PLI guidelines notified subsequently.
Answer‑writing tip
When a question links a specific scheme (PLI, FTP, RCEP) to a committee, first identify the ministry that administers the scheme. The DRSC attached to that ministry is the default examining body. Memorise the ministry‑committee mapping: Commerce & Industry → Committee on Commerce and Industry; Railways → Committee on Railways; Personnel & Law → Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice; Food & Consumer Affairs → Committee on Food, Consumer Affairs and Public Distribution; Chemicals & Fertilizers → Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers.

Parliamentary Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice: Civil Service Reforms, Judicial Appointments Oversight and Legislative Drafting Quality

This Standing Committee supervises the Department of Personnel and Training (DoPT), the Ministry of Law and Justice (including the Legislative Department), the Central Vigilance Commission (CVC), the Central Information Commission (CIC), and the Election Commission of India (ECI) in matters of administrative reforms. Its reports have shaped major governance initiatives such as the “Mission Karmayogi” capacity‑building programme, the lateral entry scheme for joint secretary‑level posts, and the repeal of a large number of obsolete statutes under the Repealing and Amending Acts.

The Committee’s 2021 report “Judicial Appointments – Transparency and Merit” analysed the collegium system and the draft Judicial Appointments Commission Bill. It recommended a “search‑cum‑evaluation committee” with a fixed tenure, a mandatory cooling‑off period for retired judges, and public disclosure of criteria — recommendations that informed subsequent judicial directions to finalise a Memorandum of Procedure (MoP) incorporating transparency norms.

On legislative drafting, the Committee’s “Quality of Legislation” report introduced a “Legislative Impact Assessment” (LIA) template for government Bills, requiring a regulatory impact statement, a compliance cost estimate, and a sunset clause review after five years. The Ministry of Law and Justice adopted the LIA framework in its “Guidelines for Pre‑Legislative Consultation”, and the Digital Personal Data Protection Bill was among the first to undergo this process.

Parliamentary Committee on Food, Consumer Affairs and Public Distribution: PDS Leakage, Buffer Stock Management and Consumer Protection

The Committee monitors the Department of Food and Public Distribution (DFPD) and the Department of Consumer Affairs. Its oversight covers the Public Distribution System (PDS) under the National Food Security Act (NFSA), 2013, the operations of the Food Corporation of India (FCI), buffer stock norms, and the implementation of the Consumer Protection Act, 2019.

The 2020 report “PDS Leakage and Aadhaar Seeding” estimated that a notable proportion of foodgrains allocated under NFSA did not reach intended beneficiaries, primarily due to ghost ration cards and diversion at fair‑price shops. The Committee recommended universal Aadhaar‑based biometric authentication, end‑to‑end computerisation of supply chain (e‑POS), and a “social audit” mechanism at the gram panchayat level. Subsequent data indicates a high proportion of ration cards are now Aadhaar‑seeded and e‑POS coverage has expanded significantly, with estimated leakage reduced considerably.

On buffer stocks, the Committee’s 2021 review noted that wheat and rice stocks held by FCI had risen well above strategic reserve norms, leading to high carrying costs. It recommended a “dynamic release policy” linked to open market prices and export parity, which the DFPD adopted in the “Open Market Sale Scheme (OMSS)” allowing weekly e‑auctions. The Committee also examined the Consumer Protection Act, 2019; its report highlighted that a minority of district consumer commissions were functional within the prescribed disposal timeline, prompting the Ministry to launch the “e‑Daakhil” portal for online filing and set a target for full functionality.

Parliamentary Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers: Subsidy Rationalisation, Pricing Formula and Environmental Compliance

This Standing Committee oversees the Department of Chemicals and Petrochemicals and the Department of Fertilizers. Its work directly influences the fiscal burden of fertilizer subsidies, the pricing of natural gas for urea production, and the environmental regulation of chemical industries.

The Committee’s 2019 report “Urea Subsidy Rationalisation” analysed the New Urea Policy (NUP) 2015 and the subsequent “Neem‑Coated Urea” mandate. It found that neem coating reduced diversion but the subsidy per tonne remained high because the “cost‑plus” pricing formula did not reflect efficiency gains. The Committee recommended a “nutrient‑based subsidy (NBS) for urea” aligned with the NBS already applied to phosphatic and potassic fertilizers. The government introduced a “fixed‑subsidy” component for urea, capping the subsidy and linking the balance to a benchmark gas price.

On natural gas pricing, the Committee’s report “Gas Pricing for Fertilizer Sector” examined the Administered Price Mechanism (APM) and the “Unified Gas Price”. It highlighted that the domestic gas price remained above international benchmarks, inflating urea production costs. The Committee urged a “gas‑price ceiling” for fertilizer use and a transparent “gas‑allocation formula” prioritising urea plants. The Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas incorporated a “fertilizer‑priority allocation” clause in the Gas Allocation Policy.

Environmental compliance featured in the report “Pollution Control in Fertilizer Units”, which cited pollution control board data showing a significant proportion of urea plants exceeded effluent discharge norms for ammonia and nitrate. The Committee recommended mandatory “Zero Liquid Discharge (ZLD)” for all new urea projects and a time‑bound retrofit for existing units — recommendations reflected in subsequent amendments to the Environment (Protection) Rules prescribing ZLD for new fertilizer plants.

  • Confusing the Committee on Commerce and Industry with the Committee on Finance when a question refers to PLI schemes or Foreign Trade Policy.
  • Assuming the Committee on Railways has jurisdiction over metro rail projects — those fall under the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs and are examined by the Committee on Urban Development.
  • Attributing the lateral‑entry reform solely to the Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice; the reform was initiated by the DoPT and later endorsed by the Committee.
  • Believing the Committee on Food, Consumer Affairs and Public Distribution directly manages FCI procurement — it only reviews policy and performance; operational decisions rest with the DFPD.
  • Mixing up the nutrient‑based subsidy (NBS) for phosphatic/potassic fertilizers with the urea subsidy regime — the Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers recommended extending NBS to urea, but the government has only introduced a fixed‑subsidy cap so far.
  • Overlooking that the Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice also scrutinises the Election Commission’s administrative expenditure, not just civil‑service matters.
  • Remember to verify committee jurisdiction before citing its reports in answers.

Committee on Commerce and Industry: Mandate, Functioning and Impact on Trade Policy

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry examines the work of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, encompassing the Department of Commerce and the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT). Its jurisdiction covers foreign trade policy, export promotion schemes, special economic zones, intellectual property rights, and the administration of production‑linked incentive (PLI) schemes across sectors such as electronics, pharmaceuticals, automobiles and textiles. The committee consists of 31 members — 21 from Lok Sabha and 10 from Rajya Sabha — nominated by the Speaker and the Chairman respectively, with a tenure of one year. It meets during each session and may hold additional sittings for detailed scrutiny of bills, demands for grants, and policy documents.

Recent reports illustrate its influence. A report on ease of doing business analysed the Business Reform Action Plan and recommended a single‑window clearance portal, which aligned with the later launch of the National Single Window System. A review of the PLI scheme for electronics manufacturing noted committed investment but flagged procedural bottlenecks in disbursement; the committee urged a time‑bound claim settlement mechanism, a suggestion reflected in subsequent revised PLI guidelines. In its examination of the Foreign Trade Policy 2023‑28, the committee highlighted the need for a dynamic tariff‑rate quota system for agricultural exports and recommended a periodic review clause, which the Department of Commerce indicated it would consider in its implementation roadmap.

The committee’s oversight extends to autonomous bodies such as the Directorate General of Foreign Trade (DGFT), the Export‑Import Bank of India (EXIM Bank) and the National Institute of Design. Its recommendations on the DGFT’s “Advance Authorisation” scheme contributed to the simplification of norm‑fixing procedures. By summoning senior officials, seeking written replies, and conducting on‑site visits to SEZs, the committee ensures executive accountability while maintaining a non‑partisan tone. Its reports are tabled in both Houses, and the government is required to furnish an Action Taken Report within a prescribed period, a procedural safeguard that helps translate scrutiny into policy correction.

Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas: Energy Security, Pricing and Regulatory Oversight

The Standing Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas oversees the Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas and its public sector undertakings — ONGC, Oil India Limited, Indian Oil Corporation, Bharat Petroleum, Hindustan Petroleum, GAIL (India) and the strategic petroleum reserve entities. Its mandate includes exploration and production policies, refining and marketing margins, natural gas pricing mechanisms, pipeline infrastructure, and the transition toward cleaner fuels such as compressed biogas and green hydrogen. The committee comprises 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) and follows the standard one‑year term.

Key reports demonstrate its policy impact. A study on gas pricing reforms examined the shift from administered pricing to market‑linked mechanisms and recommended a floor price for domestic gas to protect upstream investment alongside a ceiling for fertilizer‑sector consumption; the Ministry later introduced a floor‑price band in its pricing guidelines. A report on strategic petroleum reserves assessed existing caverns and urged additional capacity, a proposal that received budgetary support in a subsequent Union Budget. An examination of the National Green Hydrogen Mission recommended a dedicated financing window and a clear certification framework; the Ministry subsequently issued a Green Hydrogen Standard. The committee also monitors the Hydrocarbon Exploration and Licensing Policy (HELP) and the Open Acreage Licensing Programme (OALP). Its scrutiny of OALP rounds highlighted low bid participation and suggested fiscal incentives for deep‑water blocks, influencing later bid‑round terms. By calling for periodic reports from the Petroleum Planning and Analysis Cell (PPAC) and the Directorate General of Hydrocarbons, the committee maintains a data‑driven oversight loop that informs both legislative debate and executive decision‑making.

Examiner's Technique: Comparative Committee Jurisdiction
A question asks: “Which parliamentary committee examines the Production Linked Incentive (PLI) scheme for the electronics sector and the Foreign Trade Policy 2023‑28?” The options list the Committee on Finance, the Committee on Commerce and Industry, the Committee on Industry, and the Committee on Science and Technology.
The correct answer is the Committee on Commerce and Industry. PLI schemes are administered by DPIIT under the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, and the Foreign Trade Policy is formulated by the Department of Commerce, both falling squarely within this committee’s jurisdiction. The Committee on Finance deals with fiscal matters and the Union Budget, not sector‑specific incentive schemes. There is no separate “Committee on Industry” in the current committee system, and the Committee on Science and Technology oversees scientific research agencies, not trade policy.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question cites a specific scheme or policy, first identify the nodal ministry. Then match that ministry to the relevant Department‑Related Standing Committee (DRSC). Memorise the 24 DRSCs and their parent ministries; a quick mental map eliminates distractors such as “Committee on Finance” or non‑existent committees.

Committee on Power: Generation, Distribution and Renewable Energy Transition

The Standing Committee on Power scrutinises the Ministry of Power, the Central Electricity Authority (CEA), and major central PSUs — NTPC, NHPC, Power Grid Corporation of India, SJVN, THDC and the Damodar Valley Corporation. Its remit covers generation capacity addition, transmission planning, distribution reforms, tariff policy, and the integration of renewable energy into the grid. The committee’s 31 members serve a one‑year term and meet regularly during sessions, often convening special sittings to hear from state‑level distribution utilities and regulators.

Significant reports include a study on renewable energy integration and grid stability, which analysed the technical challenges of absorbing large‑scale renewable capacity. The committee recommended mandatory forecasting and scheduling for wind and solar generators, a directive later reflected in Central Electricity Regulatory Commission regulations. A report on distribution reforms evaluated the financial turnaround of state discoms, noting that aggregate technical and commercial losses had declined but remained above target; it urged a performance‑linked grant component, which the Ministry incorporated in subsequent allocations. A review of green energy corridors assessed intra‑state and inter‑state transmission projects, recommending accelerated land acquisition and a single‑window clearance; the Power Ministry subsequently issued a streamlined approval process. The committee also monitors the National Smart Grid Mission and the Perform, Achieve and Trade (PAT) scheme for energy efficiency. Its scrutiny of PAT cycle results highlighted compliance gaps, prompting the Bureau of Energy Efficiency to tighten verification protocols. By demanding quarterly progress reports from the CEA and the National Load Despatch Centre, the committee ensures that policy targets translate into measurable grid‑level outcomes.

Committee on Tourism: Infrastructure, Promotion and Sustainable Development

The Standing Committee on Tourism oversees the Ministry of Tourism, the India Tourism Development Corporation (ITDC), the National Council for Hotel Management and Catering Technology, and the various state tourism development corporations. Its mandate spans the Swadesh Darshan and PRASHAD schemes, the development of iconic tourist circuits, the promotion of medical and wellness tourism, and the preservation of cultural heritage sites. The committee comprises 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) with a one‑year tenure.

Key reports illustrate its policy reach. A review of Swadesh Darshan and PRASHAD implementation found that a significant share of sanctioned projects faced delays, mainly due to land‑acquisition and coordination gaps. The committee recommended a project‑monitoring dashboard and dedicated nodal officers, measures adopted in later scheme guidelines. A report on the impact of COVID‑19 quantified a sharp decline in foreign tourist arrivals and suggested a credit‑guarantee fund for hospitality MSMEs; the Ministry launched a tourism infrastructure fund incorporating this recommendation. An examination of sustainable tourism and heritage conservation at World Heritage Sites urged adoption of global sustainable tourism criteria and visitor‑management plans; the Archaeological Survey of India subsequently issued site‑specific management plans for priority sites. The committee also reviews the “Incredible India” branding campaigns and the e‑visa regime. Its scrutiny of e‑visa expansion highlighted increased arrivals but flagged security‑clearance bottlenecks; the Ministry responded by streamlining the online clearance workflow. Through regular interaction with the National Tourism Advisory Council and state tourism secretaries, the committee maintains a feedback loop that aligns central schemes with on‑ground realities.

Committee on Culture: Heritage Conservation, Museums and Intangible Cultural Heritage

The Standing Committee on Culture examines the Ministry of Culture, the Archaeological Survey of India (ASI), the National Museum, the National Gallery of Modern Art, the Sangeet Natak Akademi, the Sahitya Akademi, the Lalit Kala Akademi, and the Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts. Its scope includes the conservation of centrally protected monuments, the management of museums and archives, the promotion of performing and visual arts, and the safeguarding of intangible cultural heritage under the UNESCO 2003 Convention. The committee has 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) serving a one‑year term.

Notable reports include an assessment of World Heritage Sites in India, which found that several faced encroachment pressures and inadequate buffer‑zone management. The committee recommended statutory buffer‑zone notification under the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains (Amendment) Act, 2010, a step the Ministry initiated for selected sites. A report on intangible cultural heritage documentation evaluated the National List, noting limited inscriptions on the UNESCO Representative List. It urged a community‑led inventory process and a dedicated fund for transmission workshops; the Ministry launched a grant scheme incorporating these suggestions. A review of museum modernisation and digital access recommended a public‑private partnership model for conservation labs and a unified digital catalogue; the National Museum adopted a PPP framework for its conservation laboratory. The committee also monitors the “Adopt a Heritage” scheme, under which private entities maintain monuments. Its scrutiny revealed that many adopted sites lacked formal maintenance plans, prompting the Ministry to mandate a tripartite MoU with clear performance indicators. By summoning the Director‑General of ASI and the heads of Akademis, the committee ensures that cultural policy remains accountable to parliamentary oversight while respecting the autonomy of creative institutions.

  • Confusing the Committee on Commerce and Industry with the Committee on Finance when a question refers to PLI schemes or Foreign Trade Policy.
  • Assuming the Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas has jurisdiction over renewable energy policy — that falls under the Committee on Power and the Committee on New and Renewable Energy.
  • Attributing the Swadesh Darshan scheme solely to the Ministry of Tourism without recognising the Committee on Tourism’s oversight role in its implementation.
  • Mixing up the Committee on Culture’s mandate with that of the Committee on Tourism regarding heritage sites; the former deals with conservation and intangible heritage, the latter with tourism infrastructure.
  • Believing the Committee on Power directly sets electricity tariffs — tariff determination is the domain of CERC and SERCs, while the committee only reviews policy and outcomes.
  • Overlooking that the Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas also scrutinises the strategic petroleum reserves, not just pricing.
  • Failing to verify the correct committee before citing its report in an answer leads to factual errors and loss of marks.

Parliamentary Committee on the Welfare of Minorities: Mandate, Reports and Impact

The Committee on Welfare of Minorities is a Department‑Related Standing Committee (DRSC) attached to the Ministry of Minority Affairs. It consists of 31 members — 21 from Lok Sabha and 10 from Rajya Sabha — elected for a one‑year term under the proportional representation principle. The committee’s mandate covers the formulation, implementation and monitoring of policies for the six notified minority communities (Muslims, Christians, Sikhs, Buddhists, Parsis and Jains). Its work programme includes examination of the Ministry’s annual reports, Demands for Grants, and the performance of schemes such as the Prime Minister’s 15‑Point Programme, the Multi‑Sectoral Development Programme (MsDP), the Pre‑Matric and Post‑Matric Scholarship Schemes, and the Nai Manzil skill‑development initiative.

Since its reconstitution the committee has produced numerous reports. A 2015 report on the implementation of the 15‑Point Programme highlighted significant under‑utilisation of allocated funds in the initial years, attributing the shortfall to delayed state‑level approvals and weak district‑level monitoring. The committee recommended a real‑time fund‑tracking dashboard and mandatory quarterly reviews by the Ministry; these suggestions were reflected in revised guidelines issued subsequently. A later report examined the impact of the MsDP on educational infrastructure in minority‑concentration districts and found a substantial proportion of sanctioned school buildings incomplete after several years. Its recommendation to link further instalments to physical verification was accepted, contributing to improved completion rates noted in later Ministry outcome budgets.

The committee also scrutinises the functioning of the National Commission for Minorities (NCM) and the Central Waqf Council. A report on the NCM’s adjudicatory backlog noted a large number of complaints pending beyond the statutory 90‑day limit. The committee urged the Ministry to amend the NCM Act to provide for time‑bound disposal and to augment the Commission’s staff; a bill to that effect was introduced in Parliament. By summoning the Secretary, Ministry of Minority Affairs, and the Chairperson of the NCM, the committee ensures that executive commitments are tested against parliamentary expectations, while respecting the constitutional autonomy of the Commission.

Key Procedural Features

  • Reports are adopted by consensus; dissent notes are appended but do not delay presentation.
  • The committee may call for papers, examine witnesses, and undertake study tours to minority‑concentration districts.
  • Action‑Taken Reports (ATRs) from the Ministry are laid before the House within six months of report presentation.

Parliamentary Committee on the Welfare of Persons with Disabilities: Structure, Functioning and Legislative Outcomes

The Committee on Welfare of Persons with Disabilities is a DRSC under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. It comprises 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) serving a one‑year term. Its jurisdiction spans the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 (RPwD Act), the National Trust for the Welfare of Persons with Autism, Cerebral Palsy, Mental Retardation and Multiple Disabilities Act, 1999, and the schemes of the Department of Empowerment of Persons with Disabilities (DEPwD) such as the Accessible India Campaign (Sugamya Bharat Abhiyan), the Scheme for Implementation of the RPwD Act (SIPDA), and the National Fellowship for Persons with Disabilities.

The committee’s 2018 report on the implementation of the RPwD Act revealed that only a minority of states had notified the state‑level rules within the prescribed two‑year window. It recommended a central monitoring cell and a compliance‑linked grant mechanism; the Ministry subsequently issued a model rule template and tied a portion of the SIPDA allocation to rule notification. A later report examined the Accessible India Campaign and found a high percentage of identified public buildings non‑compliant with accessibility standards. Its recommendation to introduce third‑party audits and a public grievance portal was accepted, leading to the launch of a dedicated mobile application.

Legislatively, the committee’s scrutiny of the amendment to the RPwD Act (which expanded the definition of disability to include 21 conditions) resulted in the insertion of a clause mandating periodic review of the schedule of disabilities every five years. The committee also monitors the National Trust’s financial health; a report flagged a shortfall in corpus fund growth, prompting the Ministry to propose a revised investment policy approved by the Finance Ministry.

Operational Highlights

  • Quarterly meetings with the Secretary, DEPwD, and the Chief Commissioner for Persons with Disabilities.
  • Field visits to district‑level disability rehabilitation centres to verify scheme delivery.
  • Annual “Disability Inclusion Index” prepared by the committee secretariat, used by the Ministry for inter‑state ranking.
Worked Answer Technique
Question: “Explain how the Committee on Welfare of Persons with Disabilities influences executive policy on accessibility. Illustrate with two concrete outcomes.”
The committee influences policy through (1) evidence‑based reports that identify implementation gaps, and (2) formal recommendations that the Ministry is obliged to respond to via Action‑Taken Reports. First, its audit of the Accessible India Campaign exposed widespread non‑compliance; the committee’s recommendation for third‑party audits led directly to the launch of a mobile app enabling citizens to report inaccessible infrastructure in real time. Second, the finding that few states had framed RPwD rules prompted the committee to recommend a compliance‑linked grant; the Ministry adopted a model rule template and tied SIPDA fund releases to rule notification, accelerating state‑level compliance. Both outcomes demonstrate the committee’s capacity to translate parliamentary scrutiny into executable administrative reforms.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “parliamentary oversight of disability policy”, name the Committee on Welfare of Persons with Disabilities, cite its key reports on RPwD implementation and the Accessible India Campaign, and link each recommendation to a specific government action (model rules, mobile app). This “report → recommendation → executive response” pattern fetches full marks.

Parliamentary Committee on the Welfare of Senior Citizens: Oversight of Pension, Health and Social Security Schemes

The Committee on Welfare of Senior Citizens is a DRSC attached to the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. It has 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) elected for a one‑year term. Its mandate covers the National Policy for Older Persons (1999), the Maintenance and Welfare of Parents and Senior Citizens Act, 2007, the Indira Gandhi National Old Age Pension Scheme (IGNOAPS), the National Programme for Health Care of the Elderly (NPHCE), and the recently launched Atal Vayo Abhyuday Yojana (AVYAY). The committee also monitors the functioning of the National Council for Senior Citizens (NCSC) and the state‑level Senior Citizens Welfare Boards.

In a report the committee highlighted that a significant proportion of eligible beneficiaries did not receive IGNOAPS payments on time, attributing delays to Aadhaar‑seeding errors and bank‑level reconciliation lapses. It recommended a direct‑benefit‑transfer (DBT) architecture with real‑time Aadhaar verification; the Ministry rolled out a “Pension DBT” module, raising on‑time disbursement substantially. A later report on NPHCE found that geriatric wards existed in only a fraction of district hospitals. The committee urged a “hub‑and‑spoke” model linking medical colleges to district hospitals; the Ministry’s subsequent budget allocated funds for establishing new geriatric units, citing the committee’s recommendation.

The committee’s scrutiny of the Maintenance and Welfare of Parents and Senior Citizens Act led to an amendment that introduced a time‑bound (90‑day) disposal of maintenance applications by tribunals and empowered district magistrates to enforce orders. A recent review of AVYAY noted that the scheme’s convergence with the Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PM‑JAY) remained limited; it recommended a unified beneficiary database, a proposal now under consideration by the National Health Authority.

Procedural Tools

  • Quarterly “Senior Citizens Welfare Dashboard” presented to the Ministry, tracking pension disbursement, tribunal pendency, and geriatric bed availability.
  • Public hearings with representatives of senior‑citizen associations and state welfare boards.
  • Mandatory ATRs laid before both Houses within 180 days of report adoption.

Parliamentary Committee on the Welfare of Children: Monitoring Child Protection, Education and Nutrition Policies

The Committee on Welfare of Children is a DRSC under the Ministry of Women and Child Development. It comprises 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) serving a one‑year term. Its remit includes the Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act, 2015, the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences (POCSO) Act, 2012, the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education (RTE) Act, 2009, the Integrated Child Development Services (ICDS) scheme, the Poshan Abhiyaan (National Nutrition Mission), and the Mission Vatsalya child‑protection framework.

The committee’s report on the implementation of the Juvenile Justice Act revealed that a large proportion of Child Welfare Committees (CWCs) were non‑functional due to vacancies. It recommended a centralised online vacancy‑monitoring system and a mandatory quarterly review by the Ministry; a “CWC‑Track” portal was launched, reducing vacancy rates significantly. A report examining the POCSO Act’s conviction rate highlighted concerns; its recommendation for fast‑track special courts and a victim‑compensation fund was incorporated into an amendment that established numerous fast‑track courts and a compensation corpus.

On nutrition, a report on Poshan Abhiyaan identified that many Anganwadi centres lacked functional growth‑monitoring equipment. The committee urged a “digital growth‑chart” integration with the Poshan Tracker app; the Ministry piloted the feature across several states, reporting improvement in timely detection of severe acute malnutrition. The committee also monitors RTE compliance; a review found that a notable percentage of private schools failed to reserve seats for economically weaker sections. It recommended a penalty‑linked compliance certificate, a measure now reflected in the draft RTE amendment bill.

Key Outputs

  • Annual “Child Welfare Index” ranking states on CWC functionality, POCSO conviction, ICDS coverage, and RTE seat‑fill rates.
  • Field visits to Anganwadi centres, observation homes, and special courts.
  • Direct interaction with the National Commission for Protection of Child Rights (NCPCR) and state commissions.

Parliamentary Oversight of the National Human Rights Commission: Constitutional Design, Reporting and Accountability Mechanisms

The National Human Rights Commission (NHRC) was established under the Protection of Human Rights Act, 1993, as a statutory body with a mandate to inquire into violations of human rights by public servants, recommend remedial measures, and promote human‑rights literacy. Although the NHRC enjoys functional autonomy, its accountability to Parliament is structured through multiple channels. The Act requires the Commission to submit an annual report to the Central Government, which lays it before each House of Parliament. The report is then referred to the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Home Affairs for detailed examination.

The Committee on Home Affairs has regularly produced dedicated reports on NHRC functioning. A report highlighted a large backlog of pending cases and recommended an increase in the Commission’s sanctioned strength, besides the creation of regional benches. The government accepted the recommendation for regional benches; benches were operationalised in subsequent years. A later report examined the NHRC’s “suo motu” cognisance powers and found a low proportion of inquiries resulting in enforceable recommendations. The committee urged amendment of the Act to make recommendations binding on the concerned authority; a bill to that effect was introduced in Parliament but lapsed.

Parliamentary questions, Zero Hour mentions, and Calling Attention motions provide real‑time oversight. In a recent session, numerous starred questions were addressed to the Minister of Home Affairs on NHRC vacancies, prompting the Ministry to fill vacant member posts. The NHRC Chairperson is also invited to depose before the Committee on Home Affairs during the examination of the annual report, allowing members to probe specific investigations.

Judicial review complements parliamentary oversight. The Supreme Court has held that the Commission’s recommendations, while not legally binding, carry persuasive authority and non‑compliance must be justified in the ATR laid before Parliament. This jurisprudence reinforces the committee’s role in ensuring that the executive provides reasoned responses to NHRC findings.

Accountability Architecture

  • Annual report → laid before Parliament → referred to Committee on Home Affairs.
  • Committee adopts report, issues recommendations, monitors ATRs.
  • Parliamentary questions and debates enforce timely executive action.
  • Supreme Court pronouncements define the legal weight of NHRC recommendations.
  • Confusing the Committee on Welfare of Minorities with the Committee on Social Justice and Empowerment when a question refers to the 15‑Point Programme.
  • Attributing the Accessible India Campaign solely to the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs; the Committee on Welfare of Persons with Disabilities monitors it under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment.
  • Assuming the Committee on Welfare of Senior Citizens directly administers the IGNOAPS; it only oversees implementation and recommends DBT reforms.
  • Mixing up the Committee on Welfare of Children’s jurisdiction over the POCSO Act with the Committee on Home Affairs, which deals with police‑level enforcement.
  • Believing the NHRC’s recommendations are legally binding; they are persuasive, and the Committee on Home Affairs tracks compliance through ATRs.
  • Overlooking that the Committee on Welfare of Persons with Disabilities also scrutinises the National Trust’s corpus fund, not just the RPwD Act.
  • Failing to cite the specific year of a committee report (e.g., 2018 RPwD implementation report) when using it as evidence in an answer.

Parliamentary Oversight of the National Commission for Women: Reporting Mechanism, Committee Review and Policy Impact

The National Commission for Women (NCW) was constituted in January 1992 under the National Commission for Women Act, 1990. The Act mandates the Commission to investigate and examine all matters relating to the safeguards provided for women under the Constitution and other laws, to present annual reports to the Central Government, and to recommend remedial legislative measures. The annual report, together with a memorandum of action taken on the recommendations, is laid before each House of Parliament. Once laid, the report is automatically referred to the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Empowerment of Women for detailed examination. The Committee holds sittings where the Chairperson and Members of the NCW are invited to depose, allowing members to probe specific investigations, the status of pending cases, and the implementation of earlier recommendations.

In practice, the Committee on Empowerment of Women has produced reports that have influenced legislative and administrative changes. For instance, a Committee report on the functioning of the NCW highlighted a significant backlog of complaints and recommended the creation of additional benches and the empowerment of the Commission to issue binding directions. The government accepted the recommendation for additional benches, and regional benches were subsequently operationalised. The Committee also scrutinised the NCW’s role in monitoring the implementation of the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013, and recommended a mandatory annual audit of Internal Complaints Committees in organisations employing more than ten persons. This recommendation found expression in subsequent amendments to the Rules under the Act.

Parliamentary questions, Zero Hour mentions and Calling Attention motions provide real‑time oversight. In recent sessions, numerous starred questions were addressed to the Minister of Women and Child Development concerning vacancies in the NCW, delays in publishing the annual report, and the effectiveness of the Commission’s helpline. The Ministry responded by filling vacant Member posts and publishing the delayed report with a detailed action‑taken note. The NCW Chairperson’s appearance before the Committee on Empowerment of Women during the examination of the annual report has become a regular feature, enabling members to seek clarification on specific cases such as the handling of acid‑attack victim compensation and the monitoring of the Beti Bachao Beti Padhao scheme.

Judicial pronouncements complement parliamentary scrutiny. The Supreme Court has held that while the NCW’s recommendations are not legally binding, they carry persuasive authority and non‑compliance must be justified in the action‑taken report laid before Parliament. This jurisprudence reinforces the Committee’s role in ensuring that the executive provides reasoned responses to NCW findings. The interplay of statutory reporting, committee examination, parliamentary questioning and judicial review creates a multi‑layered accountability architecture that seeks to translate the NCW’s advisory mandate into concrete policy outcomes.

Parliamentary Oversight of the Central Vigilance Commission: Institutional Design, Reporting and Committee Examination

The Central Vigilance Commission (CVC) was originally set up by an executive resolution in 1964 and later conferred statutory status by the Central Vigilance Commission Act, 2003. The Act provides for a Central Vigilance Commissioner and up to two Vigilance Commissioners, appointed by the President on the recommendation of a high‑powered committee comprising the Prime Minister, the Minister of Home Affairs and the Leader of the Opposition in the Lok Sabha. The CVC’s mandate is to exercise superintendence over the vigilance administration of the Central Government, to inquire into allegations of corruption against public servants, and to advise the government on vigilance matters.

Under Section 12 of the Act, the CVC is required to submit an annual report to the President, who causes it to be laid before each House of Parliament. The report is then referred to the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Home Affairs for detailed scrutiny. The Committee examines the Commission’s performance indicators — number of cases received, disposed of, pending, and the rate of conviction in cases referred to the Central Bureau of Investigation. In a recent report, the Committee on Home Affairs noted a rising pendency of cases and recommended an increase in the sanctioned strength of the Commission, the creation of additional regional offices, and the introduction of a time‑bound disposal framework. The government accepted the recommendation for additional regional offices; new offices were subsequently inaugurated.

Parliamentary questions serve as a complementary oversight tool. During recent sessions, the Minister of Home Affairs answered numerous starred and unstarred questions on CVC vacancies, the status of the Whistle‑Blower Protection Act implementation, and the Commission’s role in the investigation of high‑profile cases. The Ministry’s responses indicated that the vacant post of Vigilance Commissioner was filled and that a draft amendment to the Whistle‑Blower Protection Act was under inter‑ministerial consultation.

The CVC also interacts with the Committee on Public Accounts (PAC) when the Comptroller and Auditor General’s audit reports highlight vigilance failures. In a notable instance, the PAC’s report on a major scam referenced the CVC’s delayed referral of certain cases, prompting the Committee to recommend a protocol for timely coordination between the CVC, the CBI and the CAG. The government subsequently issued an office memorandum formalising a quarterly coordination meeting among the three bodies.

Judicial review has delineated the boundaries of the CVC’s powers. The Supreme Court has held that the CVC’s advice is not binding on the disciplinary authority but that a departure from such advice must be recorded in writing. This principle has been cited in the Committee on Home Affairs’ reports to press for greater transparency in the decision‑making process of disciplinary authorities. The combined effect of statutory reporting, committee examination, parliamentary questioning, PAC coordination and judicial pronouncements constitutes a robust, albeit evolving, accountability framework for the Central Vigilance Commission.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which parliamentary committee examines the annual report of the Central Vigilance Commission?” The answer key lists the Committee on Home Affairs. A candidate mistakenly writes the Committee on Public Accounts. Why is the Committee on Home Affairs the correct answer?
The Central Vigilance Commission Act, 2003 (Section 12) requires the CVC’s annual report to be laid before Parliament. By convention and the Rules of Procedure, reports of bodies under the Ministry of Home Affairs are referred to the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Home Affairs. The Committee on Public Accounts examines audit reports of the Comptroller and Auditor General, not the statutory annual reports of the CVC. Therefore, the Committee on Home Affairs is the designated committee for CVC report scrutiny.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links a statutory body to its parliamentary oversight committee, first identify the parent ministry of that body. The Department‑Related Standing Committee attached to that ministry is almost always the examining committee. Memorise the ministry‑committee mapping for major bodies (CVC → Home Affairs, NCW → Women & Child Development, Lokpal → Personnel, Public Grievances, Law & Justice, NDMA → Home Affairs, CCI → Finance). This shortcut eliminates guesswork and saves time.

Parliamentary Oversight of the Lokpal and Lokayuktas: Appointment, Functional Autonomy and Committee Scrutiny

The Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act, 2013 established the Lokpal at the Centre and mandated the creation of Lokayuktas in states. The Lokpal consists of a Chairperson and up to eight Members, appointed by the President on the recommendation of a selection committee comprising the Prime Minister, the Speaker of the Lok Sabha, the Leader of the Opposition in the Lok Sabha, the Chief Justice of India (or a Supreme Court judge nominated by him) and an eminent jurist. The Act requires the Lokpal to submit an annual report to the President, which is laid before each House of Parliament. The report is referred to the Department‑Related Standing Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice for detailed examination.

The Committee’s report on the functioning of the Lokpal highlighted the delay in the appointment of the first Chairperson and Members — a gap of several years after the Act’s enactment. The Committee recommended a time‑bound appointment process and the creation of a dedicated secretariat with adequate staffing. The government acted on the recommendation by notifying the Lokpal (Appointment and Conditions of Service) Rules and appointing the first Chairperson.
