The Court held it was a colourable exercise of the taxing power (Entry 49, State List) because a tax that destroys the subject of taxation is not a tax but a penalty, and the State lacked competence to impose penalties on property rights without a law under Entry 42, List III. The excessiveness test for taxation — whether the tax is so burdensome as to be confiscatory — remains a live application of the doctrine.

Recent jurisprudence has refined the doctrine. In Union of India v. H.S. Dhillon (1971), the Court held that a law enacted under Entry 97 (residuary power) cannot be colourable if the subject is not covered by any other entry. In State of Rajasthan v. G. Chawla (1959), the Court clarified that the doctrine applies only when the legislature lacks competence altogether, not when it exercises competence imperfectly. The principle underpins challenges to laws that use the Money Bill route (Article 110) to bypass Rajya Sabha — the argument being that certifying a non-Money Bill as a Money Bill is a colourable exercise of the Speaker's power under Article 110(3). The Aadhaar judgment (2018) majority held the Aadhaar Act was validly a Money Bill; Justice Chandrachud's dissent called it a "fraud on the Constitution" — the language of colourable legislation applied to parliamentary procedure itself.

Parliamentary Oversight of Intelligence Agencies: Structural Gaps and Comparative Models

India's intelligence architecture — the Intelligence Bureau (IB) under the Ministry of Home Affairs, the Research and Analysis Wing (RAW) under the Cabinet Secretariat, the National Technical Research Organisation (NTRO) under the National Security Adviser, and the Financial Intelligence Unit-India (FIU-IND) under the Department of Revenue — operates without a dedicated parliamentary oversight committee. The Standing Committee on Home Affairs examines only the IB's budgetary demands (Demand No. 51, Ministry of Home Affairs). It has no jurisdiction over RAW, NTRO, or FIU-IND. The Committee's reports on IB focus on infrastructure, manpower, and financial utilisation; they do not scrutinise operations, human intelligence sources, or counter-terrorism outcomes. The Public Accounts Committee audits expenditure post-facto through CAG reports, but CAG's audit of intelligence agencies is limited to financial regularity, not operational efficacy.

This gap contrasts with established democracies. The United States has the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence (SSCI) and the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence (HPSCI), both with statutory authority to oversee all intelligence activities, including covert action notifications. The United Kingdom has the Intelligence and Security Committee of Parliament (ISC), a statutory committee of nine parliamentarians appointed by the Prime Minister in consultation with the Leader of Opposition, with access to all intelligence material and the power to investigate operations. Canada has the National Security and Intelligence Committee of Parliamentarians (NSICOP) and the National Security and Intelligence Review Agency (NSIRA), providing both parliamentary and independent expert review. Australia has the Parliamentary Joint Committee on Intelligence and Security (PJCIS).

In India, the K. Subrahmanyam Committee (1999) on national security recommended a Parliamentary Standing Committee on National Security. The Naresh Chandra Committee (2012) recommended a National Security Oversight Mechanism. The Second Administrative Reforms Commission (2007) recommended a Parliamentary Committee on Intelligence and Security with access to classified material. None has been implemented. The primary objections cited are: (i) the risk of political leakage of sensitive intelligence; (ii) the absence of a security clearance framework for parliamentarians; (iii) the executive's prerogative over national security under Article 73. However, the Official Secrets Act, 1923 and the National Security Act, 1980 already criminalise unauthorised disclosure; a statutory oversight committee with security-cleared members, bound by confidentiality oaths, would not dilute these protections.

The 2019 amendment to the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act expanded the definition of "terrorist act" and empowered the government to designate individuals as terrorists. The absence of parliamentary oversight over the intelligence inputs underlying such designations creates an accountability vacuum. The Pegasus spyware controversy (2021) highlighted the need for a mechanism to examine allegations of unauthorised surveillance. The Supreme Court appointed a technical committee, but Parliament was bypassed. A dedicated Intelligence Oversight Committee, modelled on the UK ISC with statutory backing, bipartisan composition, security-cleared members, and mandatory annual reporting to both Houses (with classified annexures), would align India with democratic norms without compromising operational security. The constitutional basis exists: Article 118 empowers each House to make rules for its procedure and conduct of business, including committee formation. Political consensus, not constitutional amendment, is the barrier.

The Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy: Institutional Design, Implementation Gaps and Parliamentary Committee Interface

The Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy (PLCP), notified by the Ministry of Law and Justice in 2014, mandates that every legislative proposal by the Government of India must be placed in the public domain for a minimum of 30 days before being submitted to the Cabinet. The policy requires the sponsoring ministry to publish: (i) the draft bill or subordinate legislation; (ii) a brief note on the necessity, objectives, and financial implications; (iii) a summary of the proposed law in simple language; and (iv) the contact details for submitting comments. The policy applies to all bills except those certified as urgent by the Prime Minister, Money Bills, and bills replacing ordinances. The policy is executive in origin, not statutory, and lacks parliamentary enforcement mechanisms.

Compliance data reveals significant gaps. Studies by PRS Legislative Research indicate that a minority of government bills introduced in Parliament have undergone pre-legislative consultation. Major legislation — the Farm Laws (2020), the Citizenship (Amendment) Act (2019), the Labour Codes (2020), the Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023) — were introduced without meaningful public consultation. The Farm Laws were promulgated as ordinances in June 2020 and passed as bills in September 2020 without any pre-legislative consultation, triggering year-long protests. The Labour Codes consolidated 29 central laws but the draft codes were not placed for public comment in their final form; the Standing Committee on Labour examined them after introduction, not before.

The interface with parliamentary committees is critical. The PLCP envisions that public comments will inform the final draft before Cabinet approval. In practice, bills are often introduced with minimal changes, and the Department-Related Standing Committees (DRSCs) become the de facto consultation forum — but after introduction, when political positions have hardened. The Standing Committee on Finance examined the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (2016) after introduction and recommended numerous amendments, most accepted. The Standing Committee on Home Affairs examined the Criminal Procedure (Identification) Bill (2022) and recommended safeguards against misuse of biometric data. However, the government is not bound to accept committee recommendations, and the PLCP's pre-introduction consultation remains aspirational.

Strengthening the PLCP requires three reforms: (1) Statutory backing through an amendment to the General Clauses Act or a standalone Pre-Legislative Consultation Act, making consultation mandatory and justiciable; (2) Integration with DRSC workflow — the ministry's consultation report should be a mandatory annexure to the bill when introduced, and the DRSC should evaluate the quality of consultation as part of its scrutiny; (3) Digital infrastructure — a unified portal (like the UK's legislation.gov.uk consultation hub) with version tracking, comment analytics, and ministry response publication. The Law Commission's 261st Report (2015) recommended statutory pre-legislative consultation; the Second ARC recommended a "Legislative Impact Assessment" framework. Until the PLCP acquires legal force and parliamentary teeth, it remains a discretionary executive guideline — honoured in breach more often than observance.

  • Confusing the Speaker's casting vote with a deliberative vote: The Speaker never votes in the first instance; the casting vote arises only on a tie and follows the status quo convention.
  • Assuming Article 293 gives States unlimited borrowing power: State borrowing is subject to legislative ceilings and the Centre's consent under Article 293(3) when outstanding Central loans exist.
  • Equating colourable legislation with mala fide motive: The doctrine examines legislative competence (substance and effect), not legislative intent; a law within competence is valid regardless of motive.
  • Believing the Standing Committee on Home Affairs oversees all intelligence agencies: It examines only IB's budget; RAW, NTRO, and FIU-IND fall outside its jurisdiction.
  • Thinking the Pre-Legislative Consultation Policy is legally binding: It is an executive guideline without statutory force; compliance is voluntary and frequently bypassed for major legislation.
  • Assuming the FRBM Act's 3% fiscal deficit target is immutable: The target is set by FRBM Rules, amended multiple times, and includes escape clauses for structural reforms and calamities.
  • Overstating the Public Accounts Committee's role in intelligence oversight: PAC examines CAG's financial audit reports; it has no mandate or access to scrutinise operational intelligence activities.

Standing Committee on Education, Women, Children, Youth and Sports: Mandate, Reports and Impact

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Education, Women, Children, Youth and Sports (formerly the Committee on Human Resource Development) is one of the 24 DRSCs reconstituted after every general election. Its jurisdiction spans the Ministry of Education, the Ministry of Women and Child Development, and the Ministry of Youth Affairs and Sports. The committee examines demand for grants, scrutinises bills referred to it, and undertakes suo‑motu studies on policy implementation. Since the 17th Lok Sabha (2019) the committee has produced three major reports that illustrate its oversight depth. The 2021 report on the National Education Policy (NEP) 2020 assessed the rollout of the 5+3+3+4 curricular structure, the establishment of the National Assessment Centre (PARAKH), and the progress of the Higher Education Commission of India (HECI) bill. It highlighted gaps in teacher‑training funding and recommended a dedicated NEP implementation fund. The 2022 report on the Mid‑Day Meal Scheme evaluated nutritional standards, coverage of pre‑primary children, and the impact of the COVID‑19 disruption; it urged the adoption of a real‑time monitoring dashboard and the integration of the scheme with the Poshan Abhiyaan. The 2023 report on sports infrastructure reviewed the Khelo India programme, the Target Olympic Podium Scheme, and the utilisation of the National Sports Development Fund, recommending a transparent selection criterion for elite athletes and a periodic audit of stadium maintenance contracts. The committee’s recommendations are not binding, but the Ministry of Education accepted 78 % of the NEP report’s suggestions, leading to the 2022 amendment of the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act to include early‑childhood care. The committee also conducts on‑site visits to schools, anganwadi centres and sports academies, a practice that distinguishes it from many other DRSCs which rely largely on written submissions.

Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry: Trade Policy, Investment Climate and MSME Support

The Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry oversees the Ministry of Commerce and Industry (including the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade) and the Ministry of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises. Its mandate covers the examination of the annual Demand for Grants, the scrutiny of the Foreign Trade Policy (FTP), and the review of legislative proposals such as the Competition (Amendment) Bill, 2022. In the 17th Lok Sabha the committee issued three influential reports. The 2021 report on the FTP 2021‑26 analysed the shift from a five‑year to a three‑year policy cycle, the introduction of the Remission of Duties and Taxes on Exported Products (RoDTEP) scheme, and the rationalisation of export promotion capital goods (EPCG) authorisations. It recommended a sunset clause for sector‑specific incentives and a quarterly performance dashboard for the Directorate General of Foreign Trade. The 2022 report on Ease of Doing Business evaluated the impact of the Business Reform Action Plan (BRAP) across states, noting that only 12 states achieved the top‑tier ranking; it urged the central government to link fiscal devolution to reform milestones. The 2023 report on Production‑Linked Incentive (PLI) schemes for electronics, pharmaceuticals and telecom equipment examined disbursement rates, finding that only 42 % of the allocated ₹1.97 lakh crore had been released by March 2023, and called for a mid‑term review with stricter milestone‑based tranches. The committee also monitors the implementation of the MSME Development Act, 2006, and the 2020 amendment that raised the investment and turnover thresholds. Its 2022 study on credit flow to MSMEs highlighted the persistent gap between the Emergency Credit Line Guarantee Scheme (ECLGS) sanctions and actual disbursements, prompting the Ministry of Finance to issue a 2023 directive for weekly bank‑level reporting. The committee’s practice of summoning industry associations (CII, FICCI, ASSOCHAM) and state industry secretaries adds a multi‑stakeholder dimension that enriches its recommendations.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "Which parliamentary committee examines the Foreign Trade Policy and the Production‑Linked Incentive schemes?" The options include the Standing Committee on Finance, the Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry, the Committee on Estimates, and the Committee on Public Accounts.
The correct answer is the Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry. The Committee on Finance deals with the Union Budget and fiscal policy; the Committee on Estimates examines expenditure estimates; the Public Accounts Committee reviews CAG audit reports. Only the Commerce and Industry committee has the FTP and PLI schemes within its subject‑matter jurisdiction.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links a specific policy (FTP, PLI, RoDTEP, BRAP) to a committee, map the policy to the ministry that administers it. The ministry’s name appears in the committee’s title — Commerce and Industry for trade and MSME, Power for energy, Railways for rail. This ministry‑committee match eliminates three distractors instantly.

Standing Committee on Energy: Power Sector Reforms, Renewable Targets and Regulatory Oversight

The Standing Committee on Energy has jurisdiction over the Ministry of Power, the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy (MNRE), and the Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas. Its work programme includes the examination of the Demand for Grants for the three ministries, scrutiny of the Electricity (Amendment) Bill, 2022, and oversight of the National Electricity Plan (NEP) and the Integrated Energy Policy. In the 17th Lok Sabha the committee produced three substantive reports. The 2021 report on Power Sector Reforms evaluated the progress of the Ujwal DISCOM Assurance Yojana (UDAY) 2.0, the implementation of the Revamped Distribution Sector Scheme (RDSS) launched in 2021, and the status of the National Smart Grid Mission. It flagged the persistent aggregate technical and commercial (AT&C) losses above 20 % in several states and recommended a performance‑linked grant mechanism for distribution utilities. The 2022 report on Renewable Energy Targets assessed the 450 GW renewable capacity goal for 2030, the progress of the Solar Park Scheme, the Green Energy Corridor project, and the Production‑Linked Incentive for high‑efficiency solar PV modules. It noted that only 115 GW of non‑fossil capacity had been commissioned by March 2022 and urged the MNRE to streamline land acquisition and transmission evacuation clearances. The 2023 report on Gas Pricing and Market Development examined the Administered Price Mechanism (APM) for domestic gas, the move towards a unified gas price under the New Domestic Gas Pricing Guidelines, 2023, and the development of the Indian Gas Exchange (IGX). It recommended a transparent price discovery mechanism and the separation of transport and marketing functions of GAIL (India) Ltd. The committee regularly invites the Chairperson of the Central Electricity Regulatory Commission (CERC) and the Appellate Tribunal for Electricity (APTEL) to discuss regulatory orders, a practice that strengthens legislative oversight of quasi‑judicial bodies.

Standing Committee on Railways: Capital Expenditure, Safety Modernisation and Freight Corridor Monitoring

The Standing Committee on Railways examines the Ministry of Railways’ Demand for Grants, the Railway Budget (merged with the Union Budget since 2017), and legislative proposals such as the Railways (Amendment) Bill, 2022. Its oversight extends to capital expenditure planning, safety modernisation, and the implementation of the Dedicated Freight Corridor (DFC) projects. In the 17th Lok Sabha the committee released three major reports. The 2021 report on Railway Safety analysed the implementation of the Rashtriya Rail Sanraksha Kosh (RRSK), the deployment of the Train Collision Avoidance System (TCAS) — branded as Kavach — and the status of level‑crossing elimination. It highlighted that only 1,200 km of track had been equipped with Kavach by March 2021 against a target of 34,000 km, and recommended a phased rollout with mandatory fitment on all new locomotives. The 2022 report on the Dedicated Freight Corridor reviewed the progress of the Eastern and Western DFCs, noting that the Western DFC (1,504 km) achieved 85 % physical completion while the Eastern DFC (1,856 km) lagged at 62 % due to land‑acquisition disputes in Uttar Pradesh and Bihar. The committee urged the Ministry to invoke the Right to Fair Compensation and Transparency in Land Acquisition, Rehabilitation and Resettlement Act, 2013, provisions for expedited acquisition. The 2023 report on High‑Speed Rail examined the Mumbai‑Ahmedabad bullet train project, assessing the loan agreement with the Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA), the status of civil works, and the environmental clearance conditions. It recommended a revised financial model that incorporates fare‑box revenue projections and a sovereign guarantee framework for future corridors. The committee’s practice of conducting spot inspections of railway workshops, signalling centres and construction sites provides ground‑truth data that supplements the Ministry’s self‑reported progress.

  • Confusing the Standing Committee on Energy with the Standing Committee on Power — the former covers petroleum and renewable energy in addition to power.
  • Assuming the Committee on Commerce and Industry also examines the Department of Revenue — revenue matters fall under the Standing Committee on Finance.
  • Believing the Committee on Railways has jurisdiction over the Metro Rail projects — urban metro systems are overseen by the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs and its respective committee.
  • Thinking the Committee on Education, Women, Children, Youth and Sports deals with higher education funding alone — it also monitors school‑level schemes, child‑welfare programmes and sports infrastructure.
  • Overstating the binding nature of committee recommendations — they are advisory; ministries may accept, reject or partially implement them.
  • Mixing up the Committee on Energy’s oversight of the Central Electricity Regulatory Commission with that of the Committee on Subordinate Legislation — the latter scrutinises delegated legislation, not regulatory orders.

These advanced sections equip aspirants with the granular committee‑level knowledge that distinguishes high‑scoring answers in the mains examination.

Committee on Commerce and Industry: Mandate, Working and Impact on Trade Policy

The Department‑Related Standing Committee on Commerce and Industry is one of the 24 standing committees that scrutinise the work of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, which comprises the Department of Commerce and the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT). The committee consists of 31 members — 21 elected from the Lok Sabha and 10 from the Rajya Sabha — reflecting the proportional strength of parties in each House. Its primary functions are to examine the Demands for Grants of the two departments, to study bills referred to it (such as the Foreign Trade (Development and Regulation) Amendment Bill), and to undertake suo‑motu studies on subjects like export promotion, foreign direct investment policy, intellectual property rights, and the implementation of the Production‑Linked Incentive (PLI) schemes.

In the 17th Lok Sabha the committee produced reports on the Foreign Trade Policy (2021‑26), Ease of Doing Business, and PLI schemes that illustrate its policy focus. Its report on the Foreign Trade Policy evaluated the shift from MEIS to RoDTEP and recommended a time‑bound transition with a review mechanism. The Ease of Doing Business report analysed the Business Reform Action Plan across states, noting uneven implementation and urging DPIIT to link central assistance to reform milestones. The PLI report assessed disbursement against committed outlays, flagging low release rates and recommending a quarterly monitoring dashboard accessible to Parliament.

The committee’s practice of inviting the Commerce Secretary, the Director General of Foreign Trade, and representatives of industry associations (CII, FICCI, ASSOCHAM) for evidence sessions provides a direct channel for stakeholder input. Its recommendations, while advisory, have frequently informed policy circulars; for instance, suggestions on rationalising the Advance Authorisation scheme were reflected in subsequent Foreign Trade Policy amendments. The committee also monitors WTO commitments, especially in agriculture and services, and has flagged the need for a domestic legal framework to address digital trade issues raised in the Joint Statement Initiative on E‑Commerce.

Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice: Administrative Reforms and Judicial Oversight

The Standing Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice oversees the Ministry of Personnel, Public Grievances and Pensions and the Ministry of Law and Justice. Its 31 members (21 from Lok Sabha, 10 from Rajya Sabha) examine the Demands for Grants of both ministries, scrutinise legislation such as the Repealing and Amending Bill, the Tribunals Reforms Bill, and the proposed Judicial Standards and Accountability Bill, and conduct studies on civil service reforms, pension policy, and the functioning of the Central Administrative Tribunal (CAT) and the National Judicial Appointments Commission (NJAC) debates.

Key reports in the 17th Lok Sabha include a 2020 study on Administrative Reforms recommending performance‑based appraisal for Group A officers, a centralised grievance portal with time‑bound disposal, and rationalisation of the Central Secretariat Service cadre. A 2021 report on Judicial Vacancies and Infrastructure highlighted high vacancy rates in High Courts and urged a permanent secretariat for the Collegium. A 2022 report on the Right to Information (Amendment) Act, 2019 assessed the impact on Information Commission autonomy, recommending restoration of fixed tenure and salary parity with Election Commissioners.

The committee regularly summons the Secretary (Personnel), the Secretary (Justice), the Chairperson of the Central Information Commission, and the Registrar General of the Supreme Court for oral evidence. Its 2023 interim report on CAT pointed to a large case backlog and suggested additional benches in the North‑East and video‑conferencing for all hearings. While the executive is not bound to accept recommendations, the Ministry of Personnel has incorporated a performance‑appraisal framework into the Central Civil Services (Performance Management) Rules, 2023, indicating the committee’s practical influence.

Examiner's twist
A question asks: "Which parliamentary committee examines the Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Law and Justice and also reviews the functioning of the Central Administrative Tribunal?" The answer expects the precise name of the committee and its composition.
The correct answer is the Standing Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice. It consists of 31 members — 21 from Lok Sabha and 10 from Rajya Sabha — and its mandate covers both the Ministry of Personnel, Public Grievances and Pensions and the Ministry of Law and Justice, including oversight of the Central Administrative Tribunal.
Mains strategy
When a question asks for the committee that scrutinises a specific ministry, always name the exact Department‑Related Standing Committee and give its member strength (21+10). Pair this with one recent report title and a concrete recommendation to fetch the full 2‑mark allocation.

Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers: Industrial Policy, Pricing and Environmental Regulation

The Standing Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers examines the Ministry of Chemicals and Fertilizers, which includes the Department of Chemicals and Petrochemicals, the Department of Fertilizers, and the Department of Pharmaceuticals. Its 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) review the Demands for Grants, study bills such as the Pesticides Management Bill and the Drugs (Prices Control) Amendment Bill, and undertake studies on fertilizer subsidy rationalisation, pharmaceutical pricing, and chemical safety standards.

In the 17th Lok Sabha the committee released reports on Fertilizer Subsidy, Pharmaceutical Pricing, and Chemical Safety. The subsidy report analysed the Nutrient Based Subsidy regime, noting the high fiscal burden and recommending a direct benefit transfer model linked to soil health cards to curb leakages. The pricing report evaluated the Drug Price Control Order (DPCO) 2013, finding many essential formulations outside price control, and urged NPPA to expand the schedule and introduce a reference‑price mechanism for patented drugs. The safety report reviewed implementation of the MSIHC Rules, 1989, highlighting gaps in on‑site emergency plans at major accident hazard units and recommending mandatory third‑party audits and a real‑time incident reporting portal.

The committee’s evidence sessions routinely include the Secretary (Fertilizers), the Chairperson of NPPA, the Director General of the Central Pollution Control Board, and representatives of the Indian Chemical Council. Its recommendation to link urea subsidy to neem‑coated urea adoption was reflected in subsequent budget announcements. Although recommendations are advisory, the Ministry has issued office memoranda aligning with several committee suggestions, particularly on DBT for fertilizers and expansion of the DPCO schedule.

Committee on Food, Consumer Affairs and Public Distribution: Food Security, PDS Reforms and Consumer Protection

The Standing Committee on Food, Consumer Affairs and Public Distribution oversees the Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and Public Distribution, which comprises the Department of Food and Public Distribution and the Department of Consumer Affairs. Its 31 members (21 from Lok Sabha, 10 from Rajya Sabha) examine the Demands for Grants, scrutinise legislation such as the Consumer Protection Act, 2019, the Essential Commodities (Amendment) Bill, and the National Food Security (Amendment) Bill, and conduct studies on the Public Distribution System (PDS), food grain procurement, storage, and consumer redressal mechanisms.

Major reports in the 17th Lok Sabha include a 2020 study on PDS Leakages and Technology Integration, which flagged significant diversion and recommended end‑to‑end computerisation, biometric authentication at Fair Price Shops, and integration of the One Nation One Ration Card (ONORC) platform. A 2021 report on Implementation of the National Food Security Act, 2013 assessed coverage gaps, noting large numbers of eligible households remained excluded, and urged expedited identification using SECC data. A 2022 report on the Consumer Protection Act, 2019 evaluated the functioning of the Central Consumer Protection Authority and district commissions, highlighting low operationalisation and recommending a time‑bound recruitment drive and a national multilingual consumer helpline.

The committee regularly invites the Secretary (Food), the Chairman of FCI, the Director General of BIS, and consumer‑rights organisations for evidence. Its recommendation on ONORC portability aligned with the nationwide rollout completed in 2022. The Department of Consumer Affairs has since issued guidelines for mediation cells in every district commission, reflecting the committee’s suggestion.

Committee on Tourism and Culture: Heritage Conservation, Tourism Promotion and Cultural Diplomacy

The Standing Committee on Tourism and Culture examines the Ministry of Tourism and the Ministry of Culture. Its 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) review the Demands for Grants, study bills such as the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains (Amendment) Bill, and undertake studies on heritage conservation, tourism infrastructure, and India’s cultural diplomacy initiatives.

In the 17th Lok Sabha the committee produced reports on Heritage Conservation, Tourism Infrastructure, and Cultural Diplomacy. The heritage report assessed the National Conservation Policy, noting that many centrally protected monuments lacked approved conservation plans, and recommended a dedicated heritage fund with a 60:40 centre‑state sharing pattern. The tourism report evaluated the Swadesh Darshan Scheme, finding significant project delays due to land acquisition and clearances, and urged a single‑window clearance portal with fund release linked to physical milestones. The cultural diplomacy report reviewed Festival of India outreach, highlighting static budget allocations and recommending performance‑based funding linked to measurable outcomes.

The committee’s evidence sessions include the Secretary (Tourism), the Director General of ASI, the Chairman of the National Monuments Authority, and representatives of INTACH. Its recommendation for a single‑window clearance was incorporated into the revised Swadesh Darshan 2.0 guidelines. While recommendations are advisory, the Ministry of Culture established a Heritage Conservation Monitoring Cell in response to the heritage report, illustrating the committee’s capacity to trigger institutional change.

  • Confusing the Committee on Commerce and Industry with the Committee on Finance — the former deals with trade policy and PLI schemes, the latter with fiscal matters and revenue.
  • Assuming the Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice also examines the Ministry of Home Affairs — internal security and police fall under the Committee on Home Affairs.
  • Believing the Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers has jurisdiction over the Department of Pharmaceuticals alone — it covers fertilizers, chemicals and pharmaceuticals together.
  • Thinking the Committee on Food, Consumer Affairs and Public Distribution only monitors the PDS — it also oversees consumer protection law, standards (BIS) and the Essential Commodities Act.
  • Overstating the binding nature of the Committee on Tourism and Culture’s recommendations — they are advisory; ministries may accept, modify or reject them.
  • Mixing up the Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers with the Committee on Subordinate Legislation — the latter scrutinises delegated legislation across all ministries, not sector‑specific policy.
  • Neglecting the fixed composition of 31 members (21+10) for each Department‑Related Standing Committee — this figure is a frequent factual checkpoint in mains answers.

These sections complete the advanced coverage of department‑related standing committees not previously addressed in the lesson.

Committee on Commerce: Mandate, Working and Impact on Trade Policy

The Committee on Commerce is one of the twenty‑four Department‑Related Standing Committees (DRSCs) constituted under Rule 331 of the Lok Sabha Rules. It consists of 31 members — 21 from the Lok Sabha and 10 from the Rajya Sabha — nominated by the Speaker and the Chairman respectively, reflecting the party strength in each House. The committee examines the Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, scrutinises bills such as the Foreign Trade (Development and Regulation) Amendment Bill, and undertakes studies on subjects ranging from export promotion schemes to the functioning of Special Economic Zones (SEZs). Its reports are tabled in both Houses and the ministry is required to furnish an Action Taken Report within six months.

In recent Lok Sabhas the committee has examined the Foreign Trade Policy, the implementation of the Remission of Duties and Taxes on Exported Products (RoDTEP) scheme, and the impact of the Goods and Services Tax on export competitiveness. Reports on RoDTEP have highlighted delays in refund disbursement and recommended an automated, IT‑driven refund module integrated with the customs portal. Studies on the Foreign Trade Policy have assessed the shift from product‑specific incentives to a sector‑agnostic framework and urged the ministry to publish a clear roadmap for the transition period. Such reports are typically debated during the Budget Session and the ministry has accepted several recommendations, issuing revised guidelines for RoDTEP claims in due course.

The committee’s working methodology follows the standard DRSC pattern: it receives written memoranda from the ministry, holds oral evidence sessions with the Secretary (Commerce), the Director General of Foreign Trade, and representatives of export promotion councils, and then deliberates in camera before finalising its report. The chairperson, usually a senior member of the largest party in the Lok Sabha, controls the agenda in consultation with the Business Advisory Committee. The committee also interacts with the Committee on Finance on matters of customs duty and with the Committee on External Affairs on trade agreements, ensuring a coordinated parliamentary oversight of India’s commercial diplomacy.

Recommendations from the committee have been associated with policy changes. Reports on SEZ performance have been followed by the launch of a single‑window clearance portal and moves to link fiscal incentives to employment generation targets. Studies on the Market Access Initiative have coincided with enhanced budget allocations for participation in international trade fairs. However, the advisory nature of the reports means that acceptance depends on executive willingness; in several instances the ministry has partially implemented suggestions citing fiscal constraints.

For aspirants, the key examination points are the fixed composition of 31 members, the committee’s jurisdiction over the Ministry of Commerce and Industry (not the Ministry of Finance), its role in scrutinising trade agreements before ratification, and the distinction between its reports on policy implementation versus those on legislative proposals. Confusing this committee with the Committee on Finance or the Committee on External Affairs is a frequent error.

Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers: Oversight of Fertiliser Subsidy and Chemical Regulation

The Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers is a DRSC with the same 31‑member composition (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha). It oversees the Department of Chemicals and Petrochemicals, the Department of Fertilizers, and the Department of Pharmaceuticals. Its mandate covers the Demands for Grants of these three departments, bills such as the Fertiliser (Control) Amendment Bill, and studies on pricing, subsidy delivery, and regulatory frameworks for the chemical sector.

In recent Lok Sabhas the committee has submitted reports on the Nutrient Based Subsidy (NBS) regime, the Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) of fertiliser subsidies to farmers, and the promotion of nano‑fertilisers. Reports on the NBS have pointed out that the subsidy per kilogram of nutrient had not been revised for extended periods, leading to distortions in fertiliser use patterns, and have recommended a periodic review linked to international price indices and a shift towards balanced nutrient application. Studies on DBT have evaluated pilot projects and found that Aadhaar‑linked transfers reduced leakages, though connectivity issues in remote areas remained a bottleneck. Reports on nano‑fertilisers have endorsed field trials and suggested a fast‑track regulatory pathway under the Fertiliser Control Order.

The committee’s evidence sessions regularly involve the Secretary (Fertilizers), the Chairman of the Fertiliser Association of India, representatives of the Indian Council of Agricultural Research, and farmer‑producer organisations. It also examines the functioning of the Central Institute of Plastics Engineering and Technology and the National Institute of Pharmaceutical Education and Research, linking chemical regulation to public health outcomes.

Policy changes have followed committee recommendations in several areas. Revisions of the NBS rates have incorporated suggestions for a nutrient‑wise subsidy ceiling. The DBT rollout was expanded to all states after the committee highlighted pilot successes. On the pharmaceutical side, reports on drug pricing have urged the National Pharmaceutical Pricing Authority to adopt a cost‑plus methodology for essential medicines, a suggestion reflected in subsequent Drug Price Control Order amendments.

Examination focus areas include the committee’s three‑department jurisdiction, the distinction between the NBS and the earlier product‑specific subsidy, the procedural requirement for the ministry to submit an Action Taken Report within six months, and the fact that the committee does not oversee the Ministry of Agriculture’s fertiliser distribution logistics — that falls under the Committee on Agriculture. Misattributing the DBT design to the Committee on Finance is another common trap.

Worked Example
A question asks: “Distinguish the oversight jurisdiction of the Committee on Commerce from that of the Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers, and explain how each committee’s reports influence executive policy.”
The Committee on Commerce oversees the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, dealing with foreign trade policy, export promotion schemes, SEZs, and trade agreements. Its reports have been followed by the launch of a single‑window SEZ portal and revised RoDTEP guidelines. The Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers oversees three departments — Chemicals & Petrochemicals, Fertilizers, and Pharmaceuticals — focusing on fertiliser subsidy design (NBS, DBT), chemical regulation, and drug pricing. Its reports have been associated with NBS rate revisions and the nationwide expansion of fertiliser DBT. Both committees follow the same 31‑member DRSC structure, but their subject‑matter jurisdictions are distinct and non‑overlapping.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question lists multiple DRSCs, first note the parent ministry or departments each committee supervises. Use the fixed 31‑member composition as a quick factual anchor. Then match the committee’s recent report themes (trade policy vs fertiliser subsidy) to the policy outcomes mentioned in the stem. This two‑step mapping eliminates confusion between committees with similar‑sounding names.

Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice: Judicial Appointments, Law Reform and Grievance Redressal

The Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice is a DRSC with the standard 31‑member composition. It examines the Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Personnel, Public Grievances and Pensions, the Ministry of Law and Justice, and the Department of Justice. Its remit includes bills such as the Tribunals Reforms Bill, the Judicial Standards and Accountability Bill, and studies on judicial vacancies, the functioning of the Central Administrative Tribunal (CAT), and the implementation of Law Commission recommendations.

In recent Lok Sabhas the committee has presented reports on judicial vacancies in the High Courts and the Supreme Court, noting a large number of unfilled posts and recommending a time‑bound appointment calendar coordinated by the Supreme Court Collegium and the Ministry of Law. Reports on the CAT have highlighted a significant backlog of cases and urged digitisation of case records, video‑conferencing hearings, and the creation of additional benches. Studies on Law Commission recommendations, including those on the death penalty, have recommended a moratorium pending comprehensive review, sparking debate in the House.

The committee’s evidence sessions routinely involve the Secretary (Personnel), the Secretary (Justice), the Registrar General of the Supreme Court, the Chairperson of the CAT, and representatives of the Bar Council of India. It also interacts with the Committee on Home Affairs on matters of police reforms and with the Committee on Law and Justice (a separate select committee) when a bill is referred for detailed clause‑by‑clause scrutiny.

Policy impact includes notifications by the Department of Justice mandating quarterly vacancy reports from High Courts, a follow‑up to the committee’s recommendation. The CAT’s e‑filing portal was launched after the committee’s digitisation push. However, the committee’s advisory reports on judicial appointments cannot bind the Collegium, and the executive has occasionally cited separation of powers to defer action.

Key examination points: the committee’s three‑ministry jurisdiction, its role in scrutinising tribunals and judicial standards legislation, the distinction between its oversight of administrative grievances (Centralised Public Grievance Redress and Monitoring System) and the Committee on Petitions, and the fact that it does not examine the functioning of the Election Commission — that falls under the Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice only for administrative matters, while the Election Commission’s autonomy is reviewed by the Committee on Law and Justice in a different context.

Committee on Energy: Power Sector Reforms, Renewable Targets and Distribution Viability

The Committee on Energy is a DRSC with the same 31‑member structure. It oversees the Ministry of Power, the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy, and the Department of Atomic Energy (for nuclear power). Its mandate covers the Demands for Grants of these ministries, bills such as the Electricity (Amendment) Bill, and studies on distribution company (DISCOM) finances, renewable energy integration, and nuclear safety.

In recent Lok Sabhas the committee has submitted reports on the financial health of state‑owned DISCOMs, estimating aggregate losses at significant levels and recommending a time‑bound tariff rationalisation framework, mandatory quarterly financial audits, and a performance‑linked grant mechanism under the Revamped Distribution Sector Scheme. Reports on renewable energy have assessed progress towards national targets, flagging land acquisition and grid‑evacuation bottlenecks, and suggested a single‑window clearance for solar‑park developers. Studies on nuclear safety have reviewed the Atomic Energy Regulatory Board’s independence and recommended statutory autonomy aligned with International Atomic Energy Agency standards.

The committee’s evidence sessions include the Secretary (Power), the Secretary (New and Renewable Energy), the Chairman of the Central Electricity Authority, the Managing Director of the Solar Energy Corporation of India, and representatives of state electricity regulatory commissions. It also coordinates with the Committee on Finance on subsidy outlays for the Ujwal DISCOM Assurance Yojana (UDAY) and with the Committee on Environment and Forests on environmental clearances for hydro projects.

Impact is visible in amendments to the Electricity Act that introduced mandatory tariff reviews, reflecting the committee’s recommendations. The Revamped Distribution Sector Scheme incorporated performance‑linked grants as suggested. On the renewable side, the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy issued guidelines for a unified portal for solar‑park approvals, reflecting the committee’s single‑window proposal.

For the exam, remember the committee’s three‑ministry jurisdiction, the distinction between its oversight of conventional power (Ministry of Power) and renewable energy (Ministry of New and Renewable Energy), the fact that nuclear safety falls under the Department of Atomic Energy but is examined here, and the procedural requirement for the ministries to submit Action Taken Reports within six months. Confusing this committee with the Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas (which handles upstream oil and gas) is a frequent error.

  • Assuming the Committee on Commerce also examines the Ministry of Finance’s customs policy — customs duty falls under the Committee on Finance.
  • Believing the Committee on Chemicals and Fertilizers controls the Ministry of Agriculture’s fertiliser distribution network — logistics are overseen by the Committee on Agriculture.
  • Thinking the Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice can direct judicial appointments — it can only recommend; the Collegium retains constitutional authority.
  • Confusing the Committee on Energy with the Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas — the latter deals with upstream oil and gas, not power generation or renewables.
  • Overstating the binding nature of DRSC reports — they are advisory; ministries may accept, modify or reject recommendations.
  • Neglecting the fixed 31‑member composition (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) for every DRSC — this figure is a standard factual checkpoint.
  • Remember to verify the parent ministry before attributing a policy outcome to a specific committee.

Committee on Commerce: Mandate, Trade Policy Scrutiny and Export Promotion Oversight

The Committee on Commerce is one of the 24 Department‑Related Standing Committees (DRSCs) constituted under Rule 331 of the Lok Sabha Rules and Rule 269 of the Rajya Sabha Rules. It consists of 31 members — 21 from the Lok Sabha and 10 from the Rajya Sabha — nominated by the Speaker and the Chairman respectively, reflecting the proportional strength of parties in each House. Its jurisdiction covers the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, comprising the Department of Commerce and the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT). The committee examines the Demands for Grants of both departments, scrutinises bills such as the Foreign Trade (Development and Regulation) Amendment Bill, and reviews the Annual Reports of the Directorate General of Foreign Trade (DGFT), the Export Promotion Councils, and the Indian Trade Promotion Organisation.

In practice, the committee’s work cycles through three broad streams. First, it conducts a detailed examination of the Foreign Trade Policy (FTP) released every five years, assessing the coherence of export‑promotion schemes such as the Remission of Duties and Taxes on Exported Products (RoDTEP), the Rebate of State and Central Taxes and Levies (RoSCTL), and the Interest Equalisation Scheme. Reports have highlighted concerns such as delays in RoDTEP rate notifications and suggested a time‑bound, automated rate‑setting mechanism linked to sectoral export intensity. Second, the committee monitors India’s engagement at the World Trade Organization (WTO), including dispute‑settlement outcomes, the Special Safeguard Mechanism for agriculture, and ongoing negotiations on e‑commerce and fisheries subsidies. It has called for a dedicated inter‑ministerial coordination cell to align domestic regulatory changes with WTO commitments. Third, it evaluates the functioning of Export Promotion Councils (EPCs) and Commodity Boards, focusing on their governance, financial autonomy, and the effectiveness of market‑access initiatives such as the Market Access Initiative (MAI) and the Trade Infrastructure for Export Scheme (TIES).

The committee’s evidence sessions routinely summon the Commerce Secretary, the DGFT, the Chairman of the Export Promotion Council for Engineering, and representatives of the Federation of Indian Export Organisations (FIEO). It also coordinates with the Committee on Finance on customs‑duty implications of trade agreements and with the Committee on External Affairs on strategic trade dialogues. Action Taken Reports (ATRs) are required within six months of the presentation of each report, though compliance varies. Recommendations on simplifying the Advance Authorisation scheme and rationalising the Export Promotion Capital Goods (EPCG) scheme have informed recent FTP revisions. For examination purposes, candidates should remember the committee’s dual‑department jurisdiction, its role in FTP review, the distinction between its oversight of trade‑policy formulation (Department of Commerce) and investment‑facilitation measures (DPIIT), and the fact that it does not examine the Ministry of Textiles or the Ministry of Steel — those fall under separate DRSCs.

Committee on Industry: Industrial Licensing, FDI Policy and Manufacturing Competitiveness

The Committee on Industry is the DRSC tasked with overseeing the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT) and the erstwhile Department of Heavy Industry, now subsumed under the Ministry of Heavy Industries. Its 31‑member composition mirrors that of all DRSCs. The committee’s mandate extends to the examination of Demands for Grants, the scrutiny of bills such as the Industries (Development and Regulation) Amendment Bill, and the review of policy documents including the National Manufacturing Policy, the Production Linked Incentive (PLI) scheme guidelines, and the Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) policy circulars issued under the Consolidated FDI Policy.

Three thematic pillars dominate the committee’s recent work. First, it has undertaken a comprehensive appraisal of the PLI schemes across 14 sectors — ranging from mobile manufacturing and pharmaceuticals to specialty steel and drones. Its reports have highlighted the need for a robust monitoring framework that tracks not only committed investment but also actual production, employment generation, and export orientation. The committee recommended a quarterly dashboard shared with Parliament, a suggestion that has been partially adopted by DPIIT through the PLI Monitoring Portal. Second, the committee examines the evolution of industrial licensing, particularly the progressive delicensing of sectors and the residual licensing requirements for strategic industries such as defence, aerospace, and atomic energy. It has urged the government to publish a consolidated, annually updated licensing matrix to reduce regulatory uncertainty. Third, the committee reviews FDI policy changes, including the automatic route expansions in sectors like insurance, defence, and telecom, and the conditionality attached to government‑approval routes. Reports have recommended clearer definitions of “inventory‑based” versus “marketplace” models in e‑commerce to prevent regulatory arbitrage.

Evidence sessions feature the Secretary (DPIIT), the Additional Secretary (Industrial Policy), the Managing Director of the National Industrial Corridor Development Corporation, and industry representatives from the Confederation of Indian Industry (CII) and the Federation of Indian Chambers of Commerce and Industry (FICCI). The committee also interacts with the Committee on Commerce on overlapping issues such as the Export Promotion Capital Goods scheme and with the Committee on Finance on tax incentives for manufacturing. Recommendations on simplifying the Single Window Clearance System (SWCS) and on establishing a dedicated grievance redressal mechanism for PLI applicants have informed recent amendments to the Industrial Corridor Development Guidelines. For the exam, key points are: the committee’s jurisdiction over DPIIT and the Ministry of Heavy Industries, its role in PLI oversight, the distinction between its scrutiny of licensing policy (a legislative function) and its review of FDI circulars (executive policy), and the fact that it does not examine the Ministry of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises — that falls under the Committee on Labour and the Committee on Rural Development for specific programmes.

Technique
A question asks: “Which parliamentary committee examines the Production Linked Incentive (PLI) schemes and the Foreign Direct Investment policy circulars issued by DPIIT?” The options include Committee on Commerce, Committee on Industry, Committee on Finance, and Committee on Heavy Industries.
The correct answer is the Committee on Industry. The Committee on Industry is the DRSC mandated to oversee the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT), which formulates and administers the PLI schemes and issues FDI policy circulars under the Consolidated FDI Policy. The Committee on Commerce deals with the Department of Commerce and trade‑policy matters, while the Committee on Finance handles fiscal legislation and the Committee on Heavy Industries does not exist as a separate DRSC; heavy industry matters are now within the Ministry of Heavy Industries, which also falls under the Committee on Industry’s purview.
Exam Day Strategy
When a question links a specific scheme (PLI, RoDTEP, RoSCTL) to a committee, first identify the parent ministry/department that administers the scheme. Then match that ministry to the correct DRSC using the standard 31‑member committee list. Remember that DPIIT = Committee on Industry, Department of Commerce = Committee on Commerce, Ministry of Finance = Committee on Finance, Ministry of Heavy Industries = Committee on Industry. This mapping eliminates confusion between similarly named committees.

Committee on Mines: Mineral Auctions, Royalty Regimes and Sustainable Extraction

The Committee on Mines is the DRSC responsible for the Ministry of Mines, which administers the Mines and Minerals (Development and Regulation) Act, 1957 (MMDR Act) and the Coal Mines (Special Provisions) Act, 2015. Its 31‑member composition follows the standard DRSC pattern. The committee examines the Demands for Grants of the Ministry, scrutinises amendment bills to the MMDR Act, and reviews the functioning of the Indian Bureau of Mines (IBM), the National Mineral Exploration Trust (NMET), and the District Mineral Foundation (DMF) framework.

Recent committee work has concentrated on three areas. First, the auction regime for mineral concessions introduced by the 2015 amendment to the MMDR Act. Reports have assessed the transparency of the e‑auction platform, the adequacy of reserve prices, and the participation of small‑scale miners. Recommendations include a periodic independent audit of the auction algorithm and a mechanism to ensure that revenue from auctions is promptly credited to the DMFs. Second, the royalty regime: the committee has analysed the ad valorem versus specific royalty debate for major minerals such as iron ore, bauxite, and limestone, suggesting a hybrid formula that captures price volatility while providing predictability for state revenues. Third, sustainable extraction and environmental compliance. The committee has reviewed the integration of the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) process with mining lease grants, the implementation of the Sustainable Development Framework (SDF) for mining, and the utilisation of DMF funds for affected communities, highlighting delays in DMF fund disbursement and recommending a direct‑benefit transfer model for welfare projects.

Evidence sessions typically involve the Secretary (Mines), the Director General of the Indian Bureau of Mines, the Chairperson of the National Mineral Exploration Trust, and representatives of state mining departments. The committee coordinates with the Committee on Environment and Forests on forest‑clearance issues for mining projects and with the Committee on Water Resources on the impact of mining on groundwater. Recommendations on mandating a “mine closure plan” as a condition for lease renewal have been incorporated into the Mineral Conservation and Development Rules. For examination, remember: the committee’s exclusive jurisdiction over the Ministry of Mines (not the Ministry of Coal, which is overseen by the Committee on Energy), its role in reviewing the auction methodology and DMF utilisation, and the fact that it does not examine the regulation of offshore minerals — that falls under the Ministry of Earth Sciences and the Committee on Science and Technology, Environment and Forests.

Committee on Steel: Capacity Expansion, Raw Material Security and Decarbonisation Roadmap

The Committee on Steel oversees the Ministry of Steel, which is responsible for the National Steel Policy (NSP) 2017, the Steel Scrap Recycling Policy, and the administration of public sector undertakings such as Steel Authority of India Limited (SAIL), Rashtriya Ispat Nigam Limited (RINL), and NMDC Limited (for iron ore). The committee’s 31 members examine the Ministry’s Demands for Grants, review the performance of steel PSUs, and scrutinise bills affecting the sector, including the Mines and Minerals (Development and Regulation) Amendment Bill insofar as it impacts iron‑ore availability.

The committee’s recent focus areas are: (1) Capacity expansion and the 300‑million‑tonne target by 2030. Reports have evaluated the progress of brownfield expansions at SAIL and RINL, the status of greenfield projects in Odisha and Jharkhand, and the financing gaps, recommending a dedicated Steel Development Fund with contributions from the National Investment and Infrastructure Fund to de‑risk private investment. (2) Raw material security, particularly iron ore and coking coal. The committee has examined the allocation of captive iron‑ore mines to PSUs, the auction of merchant mines, and the import dependence for coking coal (over 85 %), suggesting a strategic coking‑coal reserve and long‑term off‑take agreements with overseas suppliers. (3) Decarbonisation and the transition to green steel. The committee has reviewed the Ministry’s “Green Steel Mission” draft, the role of hydrogen‑based direct reduced iron (DRI), and the carbon‑credit framework under the Perform, Achieve and Trade (PAT) scheme, urging the Ministry to set interim emission‑intensity targets for 2025 and 2030 and to align the NSP with the National Hydrogen Mission.

Witnesses include the Secretary (Steel), the Chairman of SAIL, the Managing Director of RINL, the Director General of the Indian Institute of Steel Development, and representatives of the Joint Plant Committee (JPC). The committee coordinates with the Committee on Energy on coking‑coal imports, with the Committee on Mines on iron‑ore auction outcomes, and with the Committee on Environment and Forests on emission‑norm compliance. Recommendations to mandate the use of at least 15 % scrap in electric‑arc furnace (EAF) production have been reflected in the revised Steel Scrap Recycling Policy. For the exam, key facts: the committee’s jurisdiction over the Ministry of Steel and its PSUs, its role in monitoring the NSP targets, the distinction between its oversight of raw‑material policy (shared with Committee on Mines and Committee on Energy) and its exclusive scrutiny of steel‑specific decarbonisation initiatives, and that it does not examine the Ministry of Heavy Industries’ capital‑goods sector — that remains with the Committee on Industry.

Committee on Textiles: Value Chain Integration, PLI Scheme Monitoring and Labour Standards

The Committee on Textiles is the DRSC tasked with the Ministry of Textiles, which administers the textile value chain from fibre production (cotton, jute, silk, man‑made fibres) to apparel and technical textiles. Its 31‑member composition follows the standard DRSC formula. The committee examines the Ministry’s Demands for Grants, reviews the implementation of the Production Linked Incentive (PLI) Scheme for Textiles (notified in 2021), and scrutinises the National Textile Policy drafts, the Jute Packaging Materials (Compulsory Use in Packing Commodities) Act, and the Handloom and Handicrafts sectors.

Three strands dominate its recent work. First, value‑chain integration: reports have mapped bottlenecks in cotton procurement, spinning capacity utilisation, and fabric processing, recommending a digital “Textile Value Chain Dashboard” linking the Cotton Corporation of India, the Textile Committee, and the Export Promotion Councils to provide real‑time data on raw‑material availability and price trends. Second, PLI scheme monitoring: the committee tracks approved PLI applications across man‑made fibre and technical textiles segments, focusing on committed investment, production milestones, and employment generation. It has asked the Ministry to publish quarterly progress reports and to establish a fast‑track grievance redressal window for applicants facing delays in statutory clearances. Third, labour standards and social compliance: the committee has examined the implementation of the Code on Wages, the Occupational Safety, Health and Working Conditions Code, and the Social Security Code in major textile clusters, highlighting the prevalence of contract labour, the need for skill‑upgradation programmes under the Samarth scheme, and the importance of aligning export‑market compliance requirements with domestic labour inspections.

Evidence sessions involve the Secretary (Textiles), the Development Commissioner (Handlooms), the Chairman of the Cotton Corporation of India, the Managing Director of the National Textile Corporation, and representatives of the Apparel Export Promotion Council (AEPC) and the Confederation of Indian Textile Industry (CITI). The committee coordinates with the Committee on Commerce on export‑promotion schemes, with the Committee on Labour on labour‑code implementation, and with the Committee on Agriculture on cotton‑price support mechanisms. Recommendations to extend the RoSCTL scheme to technical textiles have been accepted in recent FTP revisions. For examination, remember: the committee’s exclusive jurisdiction over the Ministry of Textiles, its role in PLI monitoring for man‑made fibre and technical textiles (distinct from the Committee on Industry’s PLI oversight for other sectors), its focus on labour‑code compliance in a highly informal workforce, and that it does not examine the Ministry of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises’ cluster‑development programmes — those fall under the Committee on Rural Development and the Committee on Industry.

  • Confusing the Committee on Commerce with the Committee on Industry — Commerce handles trade policy and export schemes; Industry handles PLI, FDI policy and industrial licensing.
  • Attributing the Ministry of Mines’ auction regime to the Committee on Energy — Energy oversees coal and power; Mines oversees non‑fuel minerals and the MMDR Act.
  • Assuming the Committee on Steel examines the Ministry of Heavy Industries — Heavy Industries is under the Committee on Industry; Steel has its own ministry and committee.
  • Believing the Committee on Textiles monitors the PLI scheme for electronics or pharmaceuticals — those are under the Committee on Industry; Textiles PLI is limited to man‑made fibre and technical textiles.
  • Overlooking the six‑month Action Taken Report deadline that applies uniformly to all DRSCs — ministries may delay, but the procedural requirement is a standard exam fact.
  • Mixing up the Committee on Mines with the Committee on Coal — there is no separate Committee on Coal; coal matters are handled by the Committee on Energy.
  • These traps highlight the need for precise committee identification in answers.

Committee on Railways: Capital Expenditure, Safety Audits and Public‑Private Partnerships

The Committee on Railways is the Department‑Related Standing Committee (DRSC) entrusted with the Ministry of Railways. Its 31‑member composition follows the standard DRSC formula — 21 members from Lok Sabha and 10 from Rajya Sabha — and it serves a one‑year term, renewable by the Speaker and the Chairman respectively. The committee’s mandate covers the examination of the Ministry’s Demands for Grants, scrutiny of the Railway Budget (merged with the Union Budget since 2017), review of the Annual Report of the Railway Board, and oversight of major policy initiatives such as the Dedicated Freight Corridor Corporation (DFCC), the National Rail Plan 2030, and the station redevelopment programme under the Amrit Bharat Station Scheme.

Three thematic pillars dominate its recent work. First, capital expenditure tracking: the committee analyses the utilisation of the Gross Budgetary Support (GBS) and extra‑budgetary resources (EBR) for new lines, gauge conversion, electrification and rolling‑stock procurement. It has repeatedly flagged the gap between sanctioned outlays and actual spend, recommending a quarterly “Capital Expenditure Dashboard” linking the Railway Board, the Finance Ministry and the Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) to improve real‑time monitoring. Second, safety audits and accident investigation: after the 2023 Balasore triple‑train collision, the committee summoned the Chairman, Railway Board, the Director General (Safety) and the Chief Commissioner of Railway Safety. Its report stressed the need for an independent safety regulator, mandatory implementation of the Kavach automatic train protection system on all high‑density routes, and a time‑bound replacement of ageing assets identified through the Track Management System (TMS). Third, public‑private partnership (PPP) models: the committee evaluates concession agreements for station redevelopment, private train operations (e.g., the Tejas and Vande Bharat lease models) and logistics parks. It has asked the Ministry to standardise risk‑allocation clauses, publish a model concession agreement, and establish a dispute‑resolution mechanism outside the Railways Claims Tribunal to avoid delays.

Evidence sessions routinely involve the Secretary (Railways), the Chairman, Railway Board, the Managing Director of DFCCIL, the Director General (Safety), and representatives of the Indian Railway Finance Corporation (IRFC) and the Rail Land Development Authority (RLDA). The committee coordinates with the Committee on Finance on budgetary allocations, with the Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture on multimodal connectivity, and with the Committee on Public Undertakings on the performance of railway PSUs such as IRFC, Container Corporation of India (CONCOR) and RailTel. Recommendations to accelerate the Kavach rollout and to create a Railway Safety Authority have been partially accepted; the Ministry has committed to a phased Kavach deployment covering 34,000 route‑kilometres by 2027. For examination, remember: the committee’s exclusive jurisdiction over the Ministry of Railways, its role in monitoring both GBS and EBR capital flows, its safety‑regulator advocacy post‑Balasore, and that it does not examine the Ministry of Road Transport and Highways’ highway‑rail interface projects — those fall under the Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture.

Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas: Exploration Licensing, Pricing Mechanism and Energy Security

The Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas is the DRSC responsible for the Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas (MoPNG). Its 31 members (21 Lok Sabha, 10 Rajya Sabha) examine the Ministry’s Demands for Grants, the Annual Report of the Directorate General of Hydrocarbons (DGH), and the performance of upstream and downstream public sector undertakings — ONGC, Oil India Limited (OIL), GAIL (India) Ltd, Indian Oil Corporation (IOCL), Bharat Petroleum (BPCL) and Hindustan Petroleum (HPCL). The committee’s work revolves around three inter‑linked domains.

First, exploration licensing regimes: the committee tracks the transition from the New Exploration Licensing Policy (NELP) to the Open Acreage Licensing Policy (OALP) and the Discovered Small Field (DSF) rounds. It has scrutinised the revenue‑sharing contracts, the bidding parameters (work programme, revenue share, investment multiple) and the timelines for grant of Petroleum Exploration Licences (PELs) and Mining Leases (MLs). Its 2022 report recommended a single‑window clearance portal integrating the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change, the Ministry of Defence (for strategic areas) and the State governments to reduce the average lead time from 30 months to under 12 months.

Second, domestic pricing and subsidy architecture: the committee evaluates the Administered Pricing Mechanism (APM) for natural gas, the market‑linked pricing for gas from difficult fields (HP‑HT, deepwater), and the Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) for LPG (PAHAL) and kerosene. It has examined the impact of the 2023 revision of the Gas Price Cap (USD 6.5 per mmBtu) on fertilizer and power sectors, urging the Ministry to publish a transparent price‑discovery formula and to align the domestic gas price with the Indian Gas Exchange (IGX) spot index.

Third, strategic energy security: the committee oversees the Strategic Petroleum Reserve (SPR) programme at Visakhapatnam, Mangalore and Padur, the filling schedule, and the coordination with the International Energy Agency (IEA) stock‑holding obligations. It has also reviewed the City Gas Distribution (CGD) network expansion under the Petroleum and Natural Gas Regulatory Board (PNGRB) authorisation rounds, focusing on the achievement of the 10 % gas‑in‑energy‑mix target by 2030. Evidence sessions involve the Secretary (Petroleum), the Chairman, DGH, the CMDs of ONGC, OIL, GAIL, IOCL, BPCL, HPCL, and the Chairperson, PNGRB. The committee coordinates with the Committee on Energy on coal‑gas substitution, with the Committee on Finance on subsidy outlays, and with the Committee on Commerce on export‑import policy for crude and refined products. For examination, remember: the committee’s exclusive jurisdiction over MoPNG, its oversight of OALP/DSF licensing, its role in gas‑price reform debates, and that it does not examine the Ministry of Power’s electricity‑generation mix — that remains with the Committee on Energy.

Examiner's twist
A question asks: “Which parliamentary committee examines the Open Acreage Licensing Policy (OALP) and the Strategic Petroleum Reserve (SPR) programme? Distinguish its mandate from that of the Committee on Energy.”
The Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas is the DRSC that examines OALP, DSF rounds, DGH reports, and the SPR programme under MoPNG. The Committee on Energy, by contrast, oversees the Ministry of Power, Ministry of New and Renewable Energy, and the coal sector (including Coal India). It does not handle upstream oil‑gas licensing or strategic petroleum reserves. In an answer, name the Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas, cite its 31‑member DRSC composition, and explicitly state that energy‑security matters relating to oil‑gas reserves fall under its purview, while electricity generation, renewable capacity addition and coal production are the domain of the Committee on Energy.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question lists multiple committees, first identify the ministry each committee shadows. Map the policy (e.g., OALP, SPR, CGD) to the correct ministry (MoPNG). Then match the ministry to its DRSC. This two‑step mapping eliminates confusion between the Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas and the Committee on Energy, which is a favourite distractor in both Prelims and Mains.

Committee on Tourism: Infrastructure Development, Visa Policy and Sustainable Tourism

The Committee on Tourism is the DRSC tasked with the Ministry of Tourism. Its 31‑member composition mirrors other DRSCs, and it examines the Ministry’s Demands for Grants, the Annual Report of the India Tourism Development Corporation (ITDC), and the implementation of centrally sponsored schemes such as Swadesh Darshan, PRASHAD (Pilgrimage Rejuvenation and Spiritual Augmentation Drive), and the Champion Services Sector Scheme for tourism. The committee’s recent reports cluster around three thrust areas.

First, tourism infrastructure financing: the committee reviews the allocation and utilisation of the Tourism Infrastructure Development Fund (TIDF), the viability gap funding (VGF) for hotel projects in the North‑East and Himalayan states, and the progress of the 50‑odd “Iconic Tourist Sites” identified for world‑class facilities. It has recommended a project‑monitoring portal integrating the Ministry, the State Tourism Departments, and the National Projects Construction Corporation (NPCC) to track physical and financial milestones in real time.

Second, visa and entry‑facilitation reforms: the committee analyses the impact of the e‑Visa regime (tourist, business, medical, conference categories) on arrival numbers, the expansion of the Visa‑on‑Arrival facility to additional nationalities, and the proposed “Tourist Visa on Arrival” for select countries. Its 2023 report urged the Ministry of Home Affairs to harmonise the e‑Visa fee structure, reduce processing time to under 24 hours, and introduce a multi‑year multiple‑entry tourist visa for high‑spending source markets.

Third, sustainable and responsible tourism: the committee scrutinises the implementation of the Sustainable Tourism Criteria for India (STCI), the carrying‑capacity assessments for ecologically fragile destinations (e.g., Ladakh, Andaman & Nicobar, Western Ghats), and the community‑based tourism models under the “Dekho Apna Desh” campaign. It has asked the Ministry to mandate carrying‑capacity studies before sanctioning new infrastructure projects in notified eco‑sensitive zones and to link central assistance to measurable sustainability indicators (waste management, water conservation, local employment). Evidence sessions involve the Secretary (Tourism), the Director General (Tourism), the CMD of ITDC, the CEO of the National Tourism Board, and representatives of the Federation of Associations in Indian Tourism & Hospitality (FAITH) and the Adventure Tour Operators Association of India (ATOAI). The committee coordinates with the Committee on Culture on heritage‑site development, with the Committee on Environment and Forests on eco‑sensitive zone clearances, and with the Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture on multimodal connectivity (e.g., UDAN regional airports, railway station upgrades at pilgrimage centres). For examination, remember: the committee’s exclusive jurisdiction over the Ministry of Tourism, its role in monitoring Swadesh Darshan and PRASHAD fund flows, its advocacy for e‑Visa liberalisation, and that it does not examine the Ministry of Civil Aviation’s route‑dispensation decisions — those fall under the Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture.

Committee on Minority Affairs: Scholarship Disbursement, Waqf Administration and Communal Harmony

The Committee on Minority Affairs is the DRSC overseeing the Ministry of Minority Affairs. Its 31 members examine the Ministry’s Demands for Grants, the Annual Report of the National Commission for Minorities (NCM), and the implementation of flagship schemes — Pre‑Matric and Post‑Matric Scholarship Schemes, Merit‑cum‑Means Scholarship, Maulana Azad National Fellowship, Nai Manzil (education and skill training for minority youth), and the Pradhan Mantri Jan Vikas Karyakram (PMJVK) for infrastructure in minority‑concentration areas. Three thematic strands dominate its scrutiny.

First, scholarship delivery architecture: the committee tracks the transition from the National Scholarship Portal (NSP) to the Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) mode, the Aadhaar‑seeding compliance rates, and the timeliness of fund release to State Implementing Agencies (SIAs). Its 2022 report highlighted a 14 % dropout in renewal applications due to delayed disbursement and recommended a “Scholarship Payment Dashboard” accessible to students, SIAs, and the Ministry for real‑time status updates.

Second, Waqf administration and property management: the committee reviews the functioning of State Waqf Boards under the Waqf Act, 1995 (as amended in 2013), the progress of the Waqf Properties Management System (WPMS) digitisation, and the incidence of encroachment on waqf lands. It has urged the Ministry to mandate annual social audits of waqf properties, empower the Central Waqf Council to issue binding directives to State Boards, and establish a fast‑track tribunal for waqf‑related disputes.

Third, communal harmony and institutional safeguards: the committee examines the NCM’s recommendations on incidents of communal violence, the implementation of the Prime Minister’s 15‑Point Programme for the Welfare of Minorities, and the coordination with the Ministry of Home Affairs on the National Foundation for Communal Harmony (NFCH). Evidence sessions involve the Secretary (Minority Affairs), the Chairperson, NCM, the Secretary, Central Waqf Council, the Director General, PMJVK, and representatives of the All India Muslim Personal Law Board (AIMPLB), the Sikh Gurdwara Prabandhak Committee (SGPC), and the Christian Council of India. The committee coordinates with the Committee on Social Justice and Empowerment on overlapping scholarship schemes, with the Committee on Home Affairs on communal‑violence data, and with the Committee on Law and Justice on Waqf Act amendments. For examination, remember: the committee’s exclusive jurisdiction over the Ministry of Minority Affairs, its focus on DBT‑enabled scholarship pipelines, its oversight of waqf‑property digitisation, and that it does not examine the Ministry of Tribal Affairs’ Vanbandhu Kalyan Yojana — that falls under the Committee on Tribal Affairs (not a separate DRSC but covered by the Committee on Social Justice and Empowerment).

Committee on Women and Child Development: Nutrition Schemes, Protection Laws and Gender‑Responsive Budgeting

The Committee on Women and Child Development is the DRSC responsible for the Ministry of Women and Child Development (MWCD). Its 31 members examine the Ministry’s Demands for Grants, the Annual Reports of the National Commission for Women (NCW) and the National Commission for Protection of Child Rights (NCPCR), and the implementation of core programmes — Integrated Child Development Services (ICDS), POSHAN Abhiyaan (National Nutrition Mission), Beti Bachao Beti Padhao (BBBP), Mission Vatsalya (child protection), and the recently launched Saksham Anganwadi and Poshan 2.0. The committee’s work is organised around three pillars.
