First, nutrition‑outcome monitoring: the committee analyses the coverage and quality of supplementary nutrition under ICDS, the real‑time data flow from the Poshan Tracker (the ICT platform launched in 2021), and the convergence with the Health Ministry’s Anaemia Mukt Bharat and the Jal Jeevan Mission for safe drinking water at Anganwadi centres. Its 2023 report flagged a 12 % gap between sanctioned and functional Anganwadi centres in aspirational districts and recommended a “Nutrition Governance Index” linking fund release to verified outcomes (weight‑for‑age, stunting reduction).

Second, legislative protection framework: the committee scrutinises the implementation of the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences (POCSO) Act, 2012, the Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act, 2015, the Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, 2006, and the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013. It has examined the functioning of Child Welfare Committees (CWCs) and Juvenile Justice Boards (JJBs), the backlog of POCSO cases in special courts, and the adequacy of the Victim Compensation Fund. The committee recommended a mandatory quarterly reporting template for State Commissions for Protection of Child Rights (SCPCRs) to the NCPCR.

Third, gender‑responsive budgeting (GRB): the committee evaluates the Gender Budget Statement (Statement 13) presented with the Union Budget, the allocation to women‑specific schemes (Mahila Shakti Kendra, Working Women Hostel, Ujjawala), and the mainstreaming of gender indicators in sectoral programmes (e.g., MGNREGA, PMAY‑G). It has urged the Ministry of Finance to institutionalise a GRB cell in each line ministry and to publish a post‑budget gender impact assessment within six months. Evidence sessions involve the Secretary (WCD), the Chairpersons of NCW and NCPCR, the Mission Director, POSHAN Abhiyaan, the Director, NIPCCD (National Institute of Public Cooperation and Child Development), and representatives of UNICEF, Save the Children, and the All India Anganwadi Workers Federation. The committee coordinates with the Committee on Health and Family Welfare on nutrition‑health convergence, with the Committee on Social Justice and Empowerment on scholarship schemes for girls, and with the Committee on Labour on the implementation of the Maternity Benefit (Amendment) Act, 2017. For examination, remember: the committee’s exclusive jurisdiction over MWCD, its role in tracking Poshan Tracker data, its oversight of POCSO and JJ Act implementation, and that it does not examine the Ministry of Education’s Samagra Shiksha gender‑inclusion components — those fall under the Committee on Education.

  • Confusing the Committee on Petroleum and Natural Gas with the Committee on Energy — the former handles upstream licensing (OALP/DSF) and strategic petroleum reserves; the latter covers power, coal and renewable energy.
  • Attributing railway safety regulation to the Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture — safety audits, Kavach deployment and the Railway Safety Authority proposal are squarely within the Committee on Railways.
  • Assuming the Committee on Tourism examines civil‑aviation route awards — UDAN route dispensation and airline licensing belong to the Committee on Transport, Tourism and Culture.
  • Mixing up the Committee on Minority Affairs with the Committee on Social Justice and Empowerment — scholarship schemes for minorities are under Minority Affairs; SC/ST/OBC scholarships and the Vanbandhu Kalyan Yojana are under Social Justice.
  • Believing the Committee on Women and Child Development oversees the Mid‑Day Meal Scheme — that scheme is implemented by the Ministry of Education and examined by the Committee on Education.
  • Overlooking the uniform six‑month Action Taken Report (ATR) deadline that applies to all DRSCs — ministries may delay, but the procedural requirement is a standard exam fact.
  • Neglecting the coordination mandate — each DRSC is required to coordinate with other committees on overlapping subjects; exam answers that cite inter‑committee coordination score higher.

These advanced sections complete the parliamentary committee landscape required for a comprehensive understanding of legislative oversight in India.

Parliamentary Oversight of the Election Commission: Appointment, Funding and Accountability

The Election Commission of India (ECI) derives its authority from Article 324 of the Constitution, which vests the superintendence, direction and control of elections in an independent body. Historically, the President appointed the Chief Election Commissioner (CEC) and Election Commissioners (ECs) on the advice of the Council of Ministers, a practice that attracted criticism for potential executive influence. In March 2023, a five‑judge Constitution Bench of the Supreme Court in Anoop Baranwal v. Union of India ruled that appointments must be made by a three‑member committee comprising the Prime Minister, the Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha (or the leader of the largest opposition party) and the Chief Justice of India. This judgment introduced a collegium‑style mechanism, aligning the ECI’s independence more closely with that of the higher judiciary.

Funding for the ECI is routed through the Consolidated Fund of India under the “Election Commission” demand for grants, which is discussed and voted upon by Parliament during the budget session. The Committee on Estimates and the Committee on Public Accounts regularly examine the Commission’s expenditure, focusing on the cost of conducting general elections, bye‑elections and the maintenance of the electoral roll. In recent years the ECI’s budget has risen substantially, reflecting the scale of general elections. Parliament’s financial oversight is complemented by the Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice, which reviews the Commission’s administrative autonomy, staffing norms and the implementation of the Model Code of Conduct.

Accountability mechanisms include the mandatory annual report that the ECI lays before each House under Article 324(5). The report details election statistics, voter turnout, disputes adjudicated and recommendations for electoral reform. The Committee on Government Assurances tracks the implementation of assurances given by the Minister of Law and Justice on behalf of the ECI. Moreover, the ECI’s decisions on disqualification of candidates under the Representation of the People Act, 1951, are subject to judicial review. Election petitions are adjudicated by the High Court under Section 80 of the Act. Amendments to the Act have introduced timelines for the disposal of election petitions, a reform that Parliament monitored through the Standing Committee on Law and Justice.

Recent debates have centred on the ECI’s power to deregister political parties that fail to contest elections for six consecutive years, a provision upheld by the Supreme Court. Parliament’s role in amending the Representation of the People Act to strengthen the ECI’s enforcement arsenal — such as the introduction of the “None of the Above” (NOTA) option following a Supreme Court directive in 2013 and proposals for remote voting for migrant workers — illustrates the dynamic interplay between legislative amendment and institutional autonomy.

Parliamentary Scrutiny of the Finance Commission: Devolution Formula, Grants and State Fiscal Autonomy

Article 280 mandates the constitution of a Finance Commission every five years to recommend the distribution of net proceeds of taxes between the Union and the States (vertical devolution) and the allocation among States (horizontal devolution). The 15th Finance Commission (2020‑25), chaired by N. K. Singh, recommended a vertical devolution of 41 % of the divisible pool, a reduction from the 42 % recommended by the 14th Commission, citing the Union’s enhanced expenditure responsibilities after the GST rollout. The horizontal formula assigned weights: 45 % to population (2011 Census), 15 % to area, 15 % to forest and ecology, 10 % to income distance, 10 % to demographic performance and 5 % to tax effort.

Parliament’s scrutiny operates at three levels. First, the Finance Commission’s report is laid before each House and referred to the Standing Committee on Finance, which examines the methodology, data sources and the rationale behind weight assignments. Parliamentary committees have highlighted concerns over the use of the 2011 Census for population weight, arguing that it penalises States that have successfully stabilised population growth. Second, the Appropriation Bill and the Finance Bill incorporate the Commission’s recommendations; the Committee on Estimates reviews the fiscal impact of the devolution on the Union’s deficit targets. Third, the Committee on Public Accounts audits the actual release of grants‑in‑aid (revenue deficit grants, grants for local bodies, disaster management grants) against the Commission’s recommendations, often flagging delays or shortfalls.

The 15th Commission introduced performance‑based grants for urban local bodies (ULBs) and rural local bodies (RLBs), linking 50 % of the grant to the achievement of service‑level benchmarks in water supply, sanitation and solid waste management. Parliament’s Committee on Urban Development and Committee on Rural Development monitor the utilisation of these grants through quarterly progress reports submitted by the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs and the Ministry of Panchayati Raj respectively. The Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) has reported that a significant portion of the performance‑based grant for ULBs was not released due to non‑compliance with the benchmarking framework, prompting the Standing Committee on Finance to recommend a mid‑term review of the conditionalities.

State fiscal autonomy is further shaped by the Commission’s recommendations on the GST compensation cess, which the 15th Commission recommended extending till March 2026. The Committee on Finance has repeatedly debated the fiscal stress on States arising from the cessation of compensation, urging the Union to consider a structural revenue‑sharing mechanism beyond the five‑year cycle. The Commission’s advisory role in the design of the “State Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act” amendments — particularly the escape clause invoked during the COVID‑19 pandemic — demonstrates the interlinkage between Finance Commission recommendations and parliamentary legislation on fiscal discipline.

Worked Example: Finance Commission Devolution
A question asks: “Explain how the horizontal devolution formula of the 15th Finance Commission balances equity and efficiency. Illustrate with two weight components.”
Answer: The 15th Finance Commission’s horizontal formula assigns 45 % weight to population (2011 Census) to address equity by recognising the fiscal needs of larger States, while 10 % weight to tax effort rewards States that improve their own revenue mobilisation, promoting efficiency. The 15 % weight to forest and ecology compensates States bearing environmental costs, and the 10 % weight to demographic performance incentivises population stabilisation. Together, these components blend need‑based equity (population, forest) with performance‑based efficiency (tax effort, demographic performance).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “the role of Parliament in Finance Commission recommendations”, structure the answer in three layers: (1) legislative incorporation via the Finance Bill, (2) committee‑level scrutiny (Standing Committee on Finance, Estimates, PAC), and (3) post‑implementation audit by CAG and follow‑up by the Committee on Government Assurances. Cite the 15th Commission’s 41 % vertical share and the performance‑based grant conditionality for ULBs/RLBs as concrete evidence.

Parliamentary Control over the Central Vigilance Commission and the Anti‑Corruption Architecture

The Central Vigilance Commission (CVC) was established by an executive resolution in 1964 and later given statutory status under the Central Vigilance Commission Act, 2003. The Act provides for a Central Vigilance Commissioner and up to two Vigilance Commissioners, appointed by the President on the recommendation of a high‑powered committee comprising the Prime Minister (Chairperson), the Minister of Home Affairs and the Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha. This composition resembles but differs from the CBI Director’s appointment panel, reinforcing institutional independence.

Parliament exercises control through multiple channels. The CVC submits an annual report to the President, which is laid before both Houses. The Committee on Public Accounts examines the CVC’s findings on vigilance cases involving central government employees, while the Committee on Personnel, Public Grievances, Law and Justice reviews the Commission’s administrative functioning, staffing and the implementation of the “Whistle‑Blower Protection” framework under the Public Interest Disclosure and Protection of Persons Making the Disclosures Act, 2014. The PAC has highlighted a large backlog of pending vigilance cases, prompting the CVC to introduce a time‑bound disposal protocol for major penalty proceedings.

The CVC’s superintendence over the Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI) in corruption cases is a critical oversight node. The CVC monitors the progress of CBI investigations through monthly reports and can direct the CBI to initiate inquiries. However, the Supreme Court in Vineet Narain v. Union of India (1997) and later in Common Cause v. Union of India (2018) emphasised that the CVC’s role is supervisory, not operational, and that the CBI Director’s tenure security (two‑year fixed term) must be insulated from executive interference. Parliament’s Standing Committee on Home Affairs has repeatedly recommended legislative amendment to codify the CVC’s supervisory powers and to grant it statutory authority to review CBI charge‑sheets before filing.

Recent reforms include the 2021 amendment to the CVC Act, which expanded the Commission’s jurisdiction to cover officers of public sector banks and insurance companies, aligning with the recommendations of the 2nd Administrative Reforms Commission. The Committee on Finance, in its 2023 report, welcomed the expansion but flagged the need for additional resources — the CVC continues to carry significant vacancies. Parliament’s budgetary approval for the CVC’s demand for grants thus directly influences the anti‑corruption architecture’s operational capacity.

Parliamentary Oversight of the National Human Rights Commission and State Human Rights Commissions

The Protection of Human Rights Act, 1993 (as amended in 2006 and 2019) establishes the National Human Rights Commission (NHRC) and State Human Rights Commissions (SHRCs). The NHRC comprises a Chairperson (a former Chief Justice of India or a Supreme Court judge), one member who is a former Supreme Court judge, one member who is a former Chief Justice of a High Court, and two members with knowledge or practical experience in human rights. The President appoints them on the recommendation of a committee consisting of the Prime Minister (Chair), the Home Minister, the Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha, the Leader of Opposition in the Rajya Sabha, the Speaker of the Lok Sabha and the Deputy Chairman of the Rajya Sabha.

Parliament’s oversight is anchored in the mandatory annual and special reports that the NHRC lays before each House under Section 20 of the Act. The Standing Committee on Home Affairs examines these reports, focusing on the Commission’s recommendations on custodial deaths, encounter killings, rights of undertrial prisoners and the condition of mental‑health institutions. In a recent report, the Committee noted that the NHRC had recommended compensation in a large number of cases, but a substantial portion of the awarded amounts had not been disbursed by State governments, prompting a directive to the Ministry of Home Affairs to monitor compliance.

The 2019 amendment empowered the NHRC to visit any jail or detention centre without prior intimation and to intervene in proceedings involving human rights violations before any court. The Committee on Law and Justice reviewed the amendment’s impact, highlighting that the NHRC’s suo‑motu cognisance of the 2020 Delhi riots and the 2021 migrant‑worker crisis led to numerous on‑spot inquiries. However, the Committee also observed that SHRCs in several States remain non‑functional due to vacancies, undermining the federal human‑rights architecture.

Parliamentary control extends to the budgetary allocation for the NHRC and SHRCs, which is part of the Ministry of Home Affairs’ demand for grants. The Committee on Estimates has recommended a significant increase in the NHRC’s budget to strengthen its investigation wing and to establish a dedicated digital case‑management system. The Committee on Petitions also receives individual complaints alleging NHRC inaction, providing a direct citizen‑parliament link. The interplay between the NHRC’s quasi‑judicial recommendations and the executive’s implementation record remains a focal point for parliamentary debate on the effectiveness of India’s human‑rights enforcement mechanism.

  • Confusing the appointment committee for the CVC with that of the CBI Director — both share a three‑member panel but the CVC Act explicitly includes the Home Minister, whereas the CBI Director's panel includes the Chief Justice of India.
  • Assuming the Finance Commission’s vertical devolution percentage is fixed — it changes with each Commission; the 15th Commission recommended 41 %, not the 42 % of the 14th.
  • Overlooking the conditional nature of performance‑based grants for local bodies — they are released only after verified service‑level benchmarks, not automatically.
  • Treating the NHRC’s recommendations as binding — they are advisory; implementation depends on executive action, a frequent exam trap.
  • Neglecting the role of the Committee on Government Assurances in tracking executive compliance with NHRC and CVC recommendations — this committee is the primary parliamentary tool for follow‑up.
  • Misattributing the power to deregister political parties to the Election Commission alone — the Representation of the People Act provides the statutory basis, and Parliament amends it.
  • Remember these traps to avoid losing marks.
